Hyperbolic 3-manifolds with boundary of polyhedral type
Roman Prosanov

Abstract
Let M M be a compact orientable 3-manifold with hyperbolizable interior and non-empty boundary such that all boundary components have genii at least 2. We study an Alexandrov-Weyl-type problem for convex hyperbolic cone-metrics on ∂ M \partial M . We consider a class of hyperbolic metrics on M M with convex boundary, which we call bent metrics, and which naturally generalize hyperbolic metrics on M M with convex polyhedral boundary. We show that for each convex hyperbolic cone-metric d d on ∂ M \partial M , with few simple exceptions, there exists a bent metric on M M such that the induced intrinsic metric on ∂ M \partial M is d d . Next, we prove that if a bent realization is what we call controllably polyhedral, then it is unique up to isotopy. We exhibit a large subclass of hyperbolic cone-metrics on ∂ M , \partial M, called balanced , which is open and dense among all convex hyperbolic cone-metrics in the sense of Lipschitz topology, and for which we show that their bent realizations are controllably polyhedral. We additionally prove that any convex realization of a convex hyperbolic cone-metric on ∂ M \partial M is bent. Finally, we deduce that there exists an open subset of the space of convex cocompact metrics on the interior of M M , including all metrics with polyhedral convex cores, such that the metrics in this subset are (1) globally rigid with respect to the induced intrinsic metrics on the boundaries of their convex cores; (2) infinitesimally rigid with respect to their bending laminations. This gives partial progress towards conjectures of W. Thurston.



1
Introduction

1.1
Motivation

Two remarkable related results of convex geometry state that (1) every non-negatively curved metric on the 2-sphere can be realized as the induced intrinsic metric on the boundary of a convex body in the Euclidean 3-space
\mathbb{E}^{3}
, and (2) convex bodies in
\mathbb{E}^{3}
are uniquely determined by the intrinsic geometry of the boundary. This story has two classical chapters: polyhedral and smooth, though there is a common generalization obtained in the works of Alexandrov and Pogorelov. It was further extended to convex bodies in the other two model constant-curvature spaces,
\mathbb{S}^{3}
and
\mathbb{H}^{3}
. (We will say
model spaces
for short for all three.) In this paper we will be mostly interested in the polyhedral side of the story, and will generalize these results to the case of non-trivial topology, for which purpose we need to restrict ourselves to the realm of hyperbolic geometry.

Let
P
be a compact convex polyhedron in one of the model spaces. The boundary of
P
is the topological 2-sphere, and it is not hard to see that the induced intrinsic metric on the boundary is what is called a convex cone-metric.

Definition 1.1
.

Let
S
be a surface. We say that
d
is a
Euclidean cone-metric
(resp.
spherical
or
hyperbolic
) on
S
if it is locally isometric to the Euclidean plane (resp. to the standard sphere or the hyperbolic plane) except a discrete set of points where it is locally isometric to a Euclidean cone (resp. spherical or hyperbolic) of total angle
\neq 2\pi
. The exceptional points are called
vertices
of
d
. We say that
d
is
convex
if all cone angles are
<2\pi
.

We note that we allow the set of vertices to be empty, in which case
d
is a smooth metric on
S
of constant curvature
0
or
\pm 1
.

In
[
1
]
Alexandrov proved

Theorem 1.2
.

Every Euclidean convex cone-metric on the 2-sphere can be realized on the boundary of a convex polyhedron
P\subset\mathbb{E}^{3}
, which is unique up to isometry.

Later Alexandrov extended Theorem
1.2
to the other model spaces, see
[
2
]
and Alexandrov’s books
[
3
,
4
]
. The existence part of Theorem
1.2
is a polyhedral counterpart to the renowned
Weyl problem
, which asks if a smooth metric of positive curvature on the 2-sphere can always be realized as the induced intrinsic metric on the boundary of a convex body in
\mathbb{E}^{3}
. It was resolved positively by Nirenberg
[
68
]
employing the methods of partial differential equations (preceded by a proof in the analytic class due to Lewy
[
53
]
), and independently by Alexandrov
[
3
]
and Pogorelov
[
71
]
. The uniqueness part of Theorem
1.2
extends the classical Legendre–Cauchy theorem on the rigidity of polyhedra with the same combinatorics, and is parallel to the rigidity of smooth convex bodies in
\mathbb{E}^{3}
established by Herglotz
[
44
]
and preceded by a proof in the analytic class due to Cohn-Vossen
[
27
]
.

In 70s Thurston rocked the field of low-dimensional topology by formulating his geometrization conjecture (whose proof was finalized by Perelman), showing many its spectacular corollaries and providing non-trivial evidences in its favor. One of the main parts of the conjecture highlighted the unexpected ubiquity and diversity of hyperbolic manifolds in dimension 3. This attracted many geometers to a deeper study of hyperbolic 3-manifolds and their properties. Some attention was paid to hyperbolic 3-manifolds with convex boundary. In particular, Labourie proved in
[
51
]
an analogue of the Weyl problem for compact hyperbolic 3-manifolds with boundary. For the rest of the paper, let
M
be an oriented smooth compact 3-manifold with non-empty boundary, distinct from the solid torus and from the 3-ball, admitting a hyperbolic metric with convex boundary, and let
N
be the interior of
M
. The result of Labourie is

Theorem 1.3
.

For every smooth Riemannian metric
d
on
\partial M
of Gaussian curvature
>-1
there exists a hyperbolic metric
g
on
M
such that the boundary is convex and the induced intrinsic metric is isometric to
d
.

Thanks to the foundational work of Mostow
[
66
]
it is known that for any two hyperbolic metrics on a closed manifold of dimension at least 3 there exists an isometry between them homotopic to identity. In dimension 3 this was strengthened by Gabai–Meyerhoff–Thurston
[
41
]
to isotopy.

Theorem 1.4
.

Every two hyperbolic metrics on a closed 3-manifold are isotopic.

Theorem
1.4
together with the rigidity of smooth convex bodies in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
make it reasonable to conjecture that the geometry of compact hyperbolic 3-manifolds with non-empty smooth convex boundary should be dictated by their topology and by the intrinsic geometry of the boundary. This was confirmed by Schlenker in
[
81
]
provided that the boundary is strictly convex, which means that the shape operator of the boundary is everywhere positive-definite.

Theorem 1.5
.

Let
g_{1}
,
g_{2}
be two hyperbolic metrics on
M
such that the boundary is smooth, strictly convex and the induced intrinsic boundary metrics are isotopic. Then
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
are isotopic.

We note that in
[
81
]
Schlenker also gave another prove of Theorem
1.3
and considered the toy case of the solid torus.

Curiously enough, there were no polyhedral counterparts of Theorems
1.3
and
1.5
, and the main purpose of the present paper is to fill this gap. One of the main difficulties is that convex hyperbolic cone-metrics on
\partial M
admit convex realizations that are not really polyhedral and that are somewhat more difficult to handle. Another difficulty is that the core proof-ideas of Theorems
1.3
and
1.5
in
[
51
]
and
[
81
]
are quite specific to the smooth setting, and do not admit direct polyhedral generalizations. In the same time, standard approaches to the rigidity of polyhedra in the model spaces do not have direct generalizations to non-trivial 3-manifolds with polyhedral boundary. Thus, although our general framework (
the continuity method
) is similar to the one employed by Schlenker in
[
81
]
, in our implementation of all main technical steps we have to choose a different route to follow.

1.2
Our results

Let us specify what we mean by a hyperbolic metric on
M
with (convex) polyhedral boundary.

Definition 1.6
.

We say that a hyperbolic metric
g
on
M
is
polyhedral
if
\partial M
is locally convex and each point
p\in\partial M
has a neighborhood isometric to
U\cap P
, where
P\subset\mathbb{H}^{3}
is a convex polyhedron in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
and
U\subset\mathbb{H}^{3}
is an open domain intersecting
\partial P
.

Now we generalize this definition.

Definition 1.7
.

We say that a hyperbolic metric
g
on
M
is
bent
if
\partial M
is locally convex and each point
p\in\partial M
, except finitely many, belongs to the relative interior of a geodesic segment. The exceptional points are called
vertices
of
g
.

Here and further by a geodesic segment we mean a geodesic segment of
g
. If we need to consider a geodesic segment in
\partial M
with respect to the induced intrinsic metric, possibly bent in
(M,g)
, we will be referring to it as to an
intrinsic geodesic segment
. It is easy to see that a polyhedral hyperbolic metric on
M
is bent. Moreover, one can notice that in the model spaces a closed convex surface satisfying the bent condition is actually a polyhedron. This is no longer true for compact hyperbolic 3-manifolds with boundary. Well-known examples of non-polyhedral bent hyperbolic metrics without vertices are given by the convex cores of so-called convex cocompact hyperbolic 3-manifolds, which we define now.

Definition 1.8
.

A subset
C
of a complete Riemannian manifold is called
totally convex
if it is closed and contains every geodesic segment between any two points of
C
.

Definition 1.9
.

A hyperbolic metric
\overline{g}
on
N
is called
convex cocompact
if it is complete and
(N,\overline{g})
contains a compact totally convex subset.

It is not hard to see that
M
admits a hyperbolic metric with convex boundary if and only if its interior
N
admits a convex cocompact metric. Moreover, for every hyperbolic metric
g
on
M
with convex boundary there exists a unique convex cocompact metric
\overline{g}
on
N
and a unique isometric embedding
(M,g)\hookrightarrow(N,\overline{g})
, where the uniqueness is in some sense, which we will clarify in Section
2.4
. We will mostly use the notation
\overline{g}
to denote the metric on
N
obtained from
g
by this construction, but sometimes we will just write
\overline{g}
to denote a convex cocompact metric on
N
in the absence of metric
g
. Our restrictions on
M
imply that
M
is irreducible and atoroidal. Since
M
is distinct from the solid torus and the 3-ball, this particularly means that all components of
\partial M
have genii at least 2. Thurston’s hyperbolization theorem implies that the converse holds: every compact 3-manifold satisfying these topological conditions admits a hyperbolic metric with convex boundary, see
[
57
,
48
]
. In particular, there are plenty of such 3-manifolds.

Definition 1.10
.

The
convex core

C(\overline{g})
of a convex cocompact hyperbolic metric
\overline{g}
on
N
is the inclusion-minimal closed totally convex subset of
(N,\overline{g})
.

We will recall in Section
2.4
that the convex core is well-defined and, except few degenerate cases, is homeomorphic to
M
. It was shown by Thurston in
[
90
]
that the induced intrinsic metric on
\partial C(\overline{g})
is hyperbolic, but
\partial C(\overline{g})
may be bent in
(N,\overline{g})
along an irrational geodesic lamination, in which case it is neither smooth nor polyhedral. However,
\partial C(\overline{g})
satisfies our bent condition. In Section
3.1.2
we will sketch an argument showing the existence of bent metrics with vertices.

We need to introduce two important subclasses of polyhedral hyperbolic metrics on
M
.

Definition 1.11
.

We say that a polyhedral hyperbolic metric
g
on
M
is
strictly polyhedral
if
\partial M
is at positive distance from
C(\overline{g})
. We say that a polyhedral hyperbolic metric
g
on
M
is
controllably polyhedral
if
\partial M\cap C(\overline{g})
is empty or consists only of simple closed geodesics.

In particular, every strictly polyhedral metric is controllably polyhedral. We also need to distinguish a special class of metrics on
\partial M
.

Definition 1.12
.

We say that a convex hyperbolic cone-metric
d
on
\partial M
is
proto-Fuchsian
if
M
is homeomorphic to
S\times[-1,1]
for a surface
S
and either (1)
S
is closed,
d
does not have vertices and the restrictions of
d
to the components of
\partial M
are isotopic as metrics on
S
; or (2)
S
is compact with non-empty boundary and
(\partial M,d)
is the double of a hyperbolic metric on
S
with convex piecewise-geodesic boundary and with no cone-points in the interior of
S
. Otherwise, we say that
d
is
non-proto-Fuchsian
.

Note that in case (2)
M
is a handlebody. Now we can formulate the main result of our paper.

Theorem A
.

For each non-proto-Fuchsian convex hyperbolic cone-metric
d
on
\partial M
there exists a bent metric
g
on
M
such that the induced intrinsic metric on
\partial M
is
d
. If either (1)
g
is controllably polyhedral, or (2)
g
is polyhedral and does not have vertices, then
g
is unique up to isotopy (among bent metrics on
M
).

We make few remarks to explain the roles of classes of metrics participating in Theorem
A
.
First, when
d
is proto-Fuchsian, its realization degenerates to a surface and it is not possible to realize such
d
by a hyperbolic metric on
M
with convex boundary. Thereby, the realization part of Theorem
A
covers all realizable convex hyperbolic cone-metrics on
\partial M
.
Second, in the smooth setting the condition that the Gaussian curvature of a metric
d
is
>-1
implies that a convex realization of
d
is at positive distance from the convex core. In the polyhedral setting this corresponds to a strictly polyhedral boundary. Hence, Theorem
A
includes a polyhedral counterpart to Theorem
1.5
. This research was actually initiated by an attempt to prove the rigidity in the strictly polyhedral class. However, we found out that it is not really possible without extending our consideration to the larger class of controllably polyhedral manifolds. Third, the class of bent metrics on
M
is the right class to consider convex realizations of convex hyperbolic cone-metrics on
\partial M
. Indeed, we will prove in Section
3.1.2
that the induced metric on the boundary of a bent metric on
M
is a convex hyperbolic cone-metric. Furthermore, we will establish

Theorem B
.

Let
g
be a hyperbolic metric on
M
such that the boundary is convex and the induced intrinsic metric
d
on
\partial M
is a hyperbolic cone-metric. Then
g
is bent.

Our approach to Theorem
A
implies a special corollary, which we present now. By
\mathcal{CH}(N)
we denote the space of convex cocompact hyperbolic metrics on
N
up to isotopy, endowed with a natural topology, which we define in Section
2.4
. As we mentioned, the induced intrinsic metric on the boundary of the convex core of a convex cocompact metric is hyperbolic. This defines a map
\mathcal{I}:\mathcal{CH}(N)\rightarrow\mathcal{T}(\partial M)
, where
\mathcal{T}(\partial M)
is the Teichmüller space of
\partial M
. It follows from the work of Labourie
[
51
]
and from an approximation argument that this map is surjective. (The present paper provides an alternative proof of the surjectivity of this map as a particular case of Theorem
A
.) There is another object called
the bending lamination
(some authors say
pleating lamination
) of the convex core measuring how much the boundary is bent, see Section
3.1.1
for details. (In the polyhedral case this is just the data of the exterior dihedral angles.) This defines a map
\mathcal{I}^{*}:\mathcal{CH}(N)\rightarrow\mathcal{ML}(\partial M)
, where
\mathcal{ML}(\partial M)
is the space of
measured geodesic laminations
on
\partial M
. In this case the map is not surjective, and its image is described in the works
[
17
,
52
]
of Bonahon–Otal and Lecuire. Note that the space
\mathcal{ML}(\partial M)
lacks a natural differential structure, but has a natural PL-structure.

Thurston conjectured that
\mathcal{I}
is a homeomorphism and
\mathcal{I}^{*}
is a homeomorphism onto the image outside the Fuchsian locus. The injectivity of these maps remains an open problem, though it was shown in
[
17
]
that if
\overline{g}
has a non-degenerate polyhedral convex core, then
\mathcal{I}^{*}(\overline{g})
has a single
\mathcal{I}^{*}
-preimage.
1
1

1

After this paper was finished, Thurston’s conjecture for
\mathcal{I}^{*}
was resolved in a spectacular work
[
31
]
by Dular–Schlenker. It is interesting that one of the ingredients of their proof does not work for
\mathcal{I}
.
By the work
[
14
]
of Bonahon, the map
\mathcal{I}
is
C^{1}
and the map
\mathcal{I}^{*}
is tangentiable, which is a relaxation of the notion of differentiability to the PL-setting.
We show

Corollary C
.

Let
\overline{g}\in\mathcal{CH}(N)
have non-degenerate polyhedral convex core. Then there exists a neighborhood
U
of
\overline{g}
in
\mathcal{CH}(N)
such that

(1) the restriction of
\mathcal{I}
to
U
is a
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism onto the image. Moreover, for each
\overline{g}^{\prime}\in U
the metric
\mathcal{I}(\overline{g}^{\prime})
has a single
\mathcal{I}
-preimage in
\mathcal{CH}(N)
.

(2) For each
\overline{g}^{\prime}\in U
the tangent map of
\mathcal{I}^{*}
is an isomorphism at
\overline{g}^{\prime}
.

The key point here is the infinitesimal rigidity of
\mathcal{I}
at
\overline{g}
. Together with the inverse function theorem it implies the first claim of Part (1). We will show that local rigidity implies global rigidity, obtaining the second claim of Part (1). The infinitesimal rigidity problems for
\mathcal{I}
and for
\mathcal{I}^{*}
are equivalent as implied by the works of Bonahon
[
14
,
13
]
. Note that the rigidity part of
[
17
]
does not imply infinitesimal or local injectivity of
\mathcal{I}
or
\mathcal{I}^{*}
at
\overline{g}
.

A convex realization of a convex hyperbolic cone-metric might be also obtained from Theorem
1.3
together with an approximation argument, though some non-trivial work is required to exclude possible degenerations. In the case when
M
is homeomorphic to
S\times[-1,1]
this approach was implemented by Slutskiy in
[
84
]
. Theorem
B
says that such a realization is necessarily bent, leading to an alternative proof of the existence part of Theorem
A
. The most non-trivial contribution of the present paper is the proof of the uniqueness part of Theorem
A
. The proof of Theorem
B
follows the ideas of Olovyanishnikov, who similarly proved that a convex realization of a Euclidean cone-metric on the 2-sphere in the Euclidean 3-space is a polyhedron
[
69
]
.
We provide Theorem
B
to complement our main result Theorem
A
.

As we already mentioned, our proof of Theorem
A
is obtained from a generalization of the classical continuity method popularized by Alexandrov. Let
V\subset\partial M
be a finite set, possibly empty. By
\mathcal{D}_{c}(\partial M,V)
we denote the space of convex hyperbolic cone-metrics on
\partial M
with the vertex set
V
up to isotopy. By
\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
we denote the space of bent hyperbolic metrics on
M
with the vertex set
V
up to isotopy. (There are some technical restrictions on the isotopies, see details in Sections
2.2
and
3.1.2
.) We will endow these spaces with some natural smooth topologies making them finite-dimensional manifolds of the same dimension. There is the realization map

\mathcal{I}_{V}:\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{c}(\partial M,V)

sending a bent metric on
M
to the induced intrinsic metric on the boundary.

We will frequently abuse the notation and write
g\in\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
or
d\in\mathcal{D}_{c}(\partial M,V)
meaning that
g
or
d
are metrics with their isotopy classes belonging to the spaces
\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
and
\mathcal{D}_{c}(\partial M,V)
. The uniqueness part of Theorem
A
follows from the following intermediate steps.

Proposition 1.13
.

If
g\in\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
is locally rigid, i.e., the map
\mathcal{I}_{V}
is a local homeomorphism around
g
, then
g
is globally rigid, i.e., for every other
g^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
such that
\mathcal{I}_{V}(g)=\mathcal{I}_{V}(g^{\prime})
, the metrics
g
and
g^{\prime}
are isotopic.

Proposition 1.14
.

Controllably polyhedral metrics on
M
and polyhedral metrics without vertices are locally rigid.

Proposition
1.14
for controllably polyhedral metrics is proved in three steps: we show that the subset of controllably polyhedral metrics is open in
\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
, that the map
\mathcal{I}_{V}
is differentiable over this subset, and that the differential is non-degenerate. We remark that two most technically complicated places in our paper are the proofs of the latter two claims occupying Sections
3.4
and
4
. Interestingly, while the differential
d\mathcal{I}_{V}
is continuous over strictly polyhedral metrics, we cannot really show this over all controllably polyhedral metrics, but we found a way to deal with it. To show the infinitesimal rigidity we develop the theory of affine automorphic vector fields, which allow us to connect together separate approaches to the infinitesimal theory of hyperbolic cone-3-manifolds.

Contrary to the smooth case, in our setting it is not immediately obvious if there are many convex hyperbolic cone-metrics on
\partial M
admitting controllably polyhedral realizations. We will, however, show that this is a generic case, by exhibiting a large subclass of such metrics, which we call
balanced
. The actual definition is slightly technical, and we postpone it to Section
2.2.1
, but the heuristic is that a cone-metric is balanced if the set of vertices is sufficiently dense. We have

Proposition 1.15
.

The subset of balanced metrics is open and dense in the space of convex hyperbolic cone-metrics on a closed surface
S
with respect to the Lipschitz topology.

Proposition 1.16
.

Every convex realization of a convex balanced hyperbolic cone-metric on
\partial M
is controllably polyhedral.

1.3
Related work

Here we review some related work. The only previously known case of the rigidity part of Theorem
A
was established by Fillastre
[
34
]
, who considered the case of so-called
Fuchsian manifolds
with convex boundary. These are homeomorphic to
{S\times[-1,1]}
, where
S
is a closed surface of genus greater than one, and contain an embedded totally geodesic surface isotopic to
S\times\{0\}
. Fillastre also proved that for every convex hyperbolic cone-metric on
S
with vertices there exists a Fuchsian manifold with convex boundary (which is necessarily strictly polyhedral) realizing it. In
[
35
]
Fillastre went further to allow so-called ideal and hyperideal vertices. There is a similar work of Fillastre–Izmestiev
[
36
]
on the case when the manifold is homeomorphic to
T\times(-1,1]
, where
T
is the 2-torus, but this does not fit to our restrictions on
M
.

Similar results were obtained for the model Lorentzian spaces of constant curvature. In the case of the
de Sitter 3-space

d\mathbb{S}^{3}
(of constant curvature 1) there is a well-known duality between points in
d\mathbb{S}^{3}
and planes in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
, which allows to consider the corresponding questions as “dual” to questions in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
. The cornerstone is the work
[
78
]
of Rivin–Hodgson, where they gave a description of the dual metrics on convex polyhedra in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
. It builds on a previous work of Andreev
[
6
]
characterizing hyperbolic polyhedra with acute dihedral angles. It was further generalized by Rivin
[
77
]
to
ideal
(non-compact) hyperbolic polyhedra. This is of special interest as it allowed to resolve a long-standing question of Steiner to describe the combinatorial types of polyhedra admitting Euclidean realizations with all vertices on the standard sphere. The work
[
78
]
was generalized to the smooth setting by Schlenker
[
79
]
, and was recently generalized by the author to the setting of strictly polyhedral hyperbolic manifolds
[
74
]
. The case of strictly polyhedral manifolds with ideal vertices was previously considered by Schlenker in an unpublished manuscript
[
80
]
. In
[
35
]
Fillastre examined similar questions for Fuchsian Lorentzian spacetimes, and in
[
37
]
Fillastre–Izmestiev dealt with the case of dual metrics on
T\times(-1,1]
. Quite recently Smith
[
86
]
resolved the smooth Weyl problem for
Minkowski spacetimes
. In
[
89
]
Tamburelli solved the realization part for
anti-de Sitter spacetimes
.

Up to now we were mentioning in parallel the results on convex realizations in the smooth and in the polyhedral cases. It is natural to expect that there should be some common generalization of these settings. This is the topic of the Alexandrov–Pogorelov theory of general convex surfaces. Consider convex surfaces in the Euclidean 3-space
\mathbb{E}^{3}
. Alexandrov managed to give a complete intrinsic description of the induced intrinsic metrics on them. These are so-called
CBB(0) metrics
in modern terminology, whose definition is slightly technical and we refer to the textbooks
[
4
,
22
]
. This notion saw a huge development in last decades due to the connection with degenerations of Riemannian manifolds. With the help of an approximation argument, Alexandrov showed in
[
1
]
that every CBB(0) metric on the 2-sphere is realized as the induced intrinsic metric on the boundary of a convex body. The respective rigidity question was solved by Pogorelov
[
71
]
and is known as notoriously difficult, another approach was given by Volkov
[
92
]
. In recent years there was a streamline of works transferring the general Alexandrov realization theorem to various settings, see
[
38
,
40
,
50
,
84
]
. Of special relevance is the above-mentioned work of Slutskiy
[
84
]
, where he considered general convex realization results for
M
homeomorphic to
S\times[-1,1]
. However, there are no generalizations of the Pogorelov rigidity theorem, except that to the other model spaces (due to Pogorelov himself, see
[
71
]
, and to Milka
[
63
]
), and except the recent work of the author
[
72
]
on the case of Fuchsian manifolds.

Speaking of the injectivity problems for the maps
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{I}^{*}
, in the work
[
16
]
Bonahon considered manifolds
N
homeomorphic to
S\times(-1,1)
for a closed surface
S
of higher genus and showed that
\mathcal{I}^{*}
is a homeomorphism onto the image near the so-called
Fuchsian locus
of
\mathcal{CH}(N)
. In the work
[
83
]
Series proved the conjecture on
\mathcal{I}^{*}
for manifolds homeomorphic to
S\times(-1,1)
, where
S
is a once-punctured torus (the latter manifolds have cusps and are outside of the scope of this paper). As the infinitesimal rigidity problems for
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{I}^{*}
are equivalent, these works also imply the corresponding infinitesimal and local rigidity results for
\mathcal{I}
. We also remark that bending laminations can be considered as degenerate cases of the dual metrics, which we mentioned just above.

The questions whether the maps
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{I}^{*}
are homeomorphisms onto the images are very similar to the known parametrization of the space
\mathcal{CH}(N)
. Every convex cocompact hyperbolic metric on
N
determines a conformal structure “at infinity” on
\partial M
, defining another map
\mathcal{CH}(N)\rightarrow\mathcal{T}(\partial M)
to the Teichmüller space
\mathcal{T}(\partial M)
. The famous Bers
double uniformization
theorem
[
9
]
asserts that this map is a homeomorphism in the case of
N
homeomorphic to
S\times(-1,1)
. It was extended to the case of arbitrary
N
admitting a convex cocompact hyperbolic metric (and, more generally, a geometrically finite hyperbolic metric) in the collective works of Ahlfors, Bers, Kra, Marden, Maskit and Sullivan. See, e.g., the discussion in
[
57
, Chapter 5]
. A long string of efforts was devoted to generalize it to the case of all complete hyperbolic metrics on
N
(where we need other type of invariants at infinity, called
ending laminations
) culminated in the works
[
64
,
20
]
. Recently Chen and Schlenker
[
26
]
explored the case when on each boundary component one prescribes either a conformal structure at infinity or a (smooth) induced metric/dual metric.

A recognized trouble with the continuity method is that while it gives a proof of the existence of a convex realization, it provides no recipe how to construct it. The first approach to overcome it in the case of Theorem
1.2
was undertook by Volkov in
[
93
]
by a variational method, and another approach was proposed in
[
10
]
by Bobenko–Izmestiev. It is interesting to ask if any kind of constructive approach can be given to the realization part of Theorem
A
. In the work
[
72
]
the author built on this approach to show the rigidity of Fuchsian manifolds with general convex boundary. Hence, it can be helpful also on the way to generalize Pogorelov’s rigidity to other hyperbolic manifolds, and to prove the injectivity of
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{I}^{*}
for non-polyhedral convex cores.

The key infinitesimal rigidity statement in our paper is obtained with the help of the works on the rigidity of hyperbolic cone-3-manifolds, see Section
2.3
for the definition. There was a long string of results showing their rigidity in various settings, see
[
45
,
60
,
65
,
96
,
97
]
. We specifically employ the work
[
97
]
of Weiss. The rigidity of hyperbolic cone-3-manifolds has interesting applications to some other geometry problems, see
[
11
,
21
,
46
,
73
]
for examples.

Acknowledgments.
We thank François Fillastre, Ivan Izmestiev and Jean-Marc Schlenker for many valuable discussions and thank Michael Eichmair for his support.

2
Preliminaries

For a background in hyperbolic geometry we refer to
[
8
,
57
,
58
]
. We will mostly rely either on the hyperboloid model or on the Klein model of the hyperbolic space. We will frequently use the hyperbolic version of the well-known Busemann–Feller lemma
[
62
]

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
C\subset\mathbb{H}^{3}
be a closed convex set with non-empty interior,
\tau\subset(\mathbb{H}^{3}\backslash{\rm int}(C))
be a rectifiable curve and
\tau^{\prime}\subset\partial C
be its nearest-point projection on
C
. Then
\tau^{\prime}
is rectifiable and its length is at most the length of
\tau
.

We mentioned in Section
1.3
that the space of oriented planes of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
can be identified with
the de Sitter space

d\mathbb{S}^{3}
, the model Lorentzian space of curvature 1. Vice versa, points of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
correspond to space-like planes in
d\mathbb{S}^{3}
. Sometimes we will be using some simple facts on this duality. We refer to
[
78
,
79
,
74
]
for more details.

2.1
Trigonometry

In this subsection we review some elementary facts from hyperbolic trigonometry that we will use in the remainder.

By a trapezoid we mean a convex hyperbolic quadrilateral
p_{1}p_{2}q_{2}q_{1}
such that
{\angle}p_{1}q_{1}q_{2}={\angle}p_{2}q_{2}q_{1}=\pi/2
. Take a trapezoid
p_{1}p_{2}q_{2}q_{1}
and denote the lengths
p_{1}q_{1}
,
p_{2}q_{2}
,
p_{1}p_{2}
,
q_{1}q_{2}
by
a
,
b
,
c
,
\gamma
and the angles
{\angle}q_{1}p_{1}p_{2}
,
{\angle}q_{2}p_{2}p_{1}
by
\beta
and
\alpha
respectively. In what follows we will be using the analogues of the cosine and sine laws for trapezoids. The proofs are straightforward following, e.g., the approach of Thurston in
[
90
, Chapter 2.6]
.

Lemma 2.2

(The cosine laws)
.

For a trapezoid we have

\cos(\alpha)=\frac{\sinh(b)\cosh(c)-\sinh(a)}{\cosh(b)\sinh(c)},

\cosh(\gamma)=\frac{\sinh(a)\sinh(b)+\cosh(c)}{\cosh(a)\cosh(b)}.

Lemma 2.3

(The sine law)
.

For a trapezoid we have

\frac{\sin(\alpha)}{\cosh(a)}=\frac{\sin(\beta)}{\cosh(b)}=\frac{\sinh(\gamma)}{\sinh(c)}.

Next, we recall few standard formulas on right-angled hyperbolic pentagons and hexagons. Proofs can be found in
[
23
, Chapter 2.3]
.

Lemma 2.4
.

Consider a right-angled hyperbolic pentagon with side lengths
a
,
b
,
\alpha
,
c
,
\beta
clockwise. Then we have

\cosh(c)=\sinh(a)\sinh(b),

\cosh(c)=\coth(\alpha)\coth(\beta).

Lemma 2.5
.

Consider a right-angled hyperbolic hexagon with side lengths
a
,
\gamma
,
b
,
\alpha
,
c
,
\beta
clockwise. Then we have

\cosh(\alpha)=\frac{\cosh(b)\cosh(c)+\cosh(a)}{\sinh(a)\sinh(b)}.

The next lemma, due to Kubota
[
49
]
, is a hyperbolic analogue of the well-known Ptolemy theorem.

Lemma 2.6
.

Consider a hyperbolic quadrilateral inscribed in a circle with side lengths
a
,
b
,
c
and
d
clockwise and diagonal lengths
x
and
y
. Then

\sinh\left(\frac{a}{2}\right)\sinh\left(\frac{c}{2}\right)+\sinh\left(\frac{b}{2}\right)\sinh\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)=\sinh\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)\sinh\left(\frac{y}{2}\right).

Last, we will need a geometric estimate on hyperbolic pairs of pants following from

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
H=p_{1}p_{2}p_{3}p_{4}p_{5}p_{6}\subset\mathbb{H}^{2}
be a convex right-angled hexagon with sides
a,\gamma,b,\alpha,c,\beta
(
p_{1}p_{2}=a
and then cyclically). Assume that for some
\Delta>0
we have
a,b,c\geq\Delta
. Then there exist a number
\delta=\delta(\Delta)>0
and a point
p\in H
such that

d_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(p,\partial H)\geq\delta.

Proof.

Take any
\delta_{1}>0
. First suppose that
\alpha\geq\delta_{1}
. It is easy to see that there exists
\delta_{2}=\delta_{2}(\delta_{1},\Delta)>0
such that since
b\geq\Delta
and
\alpha\geq\delta_{1}
, the inradius of the right-angled triangle
p_{3}p_{4}p_{5}
is at least
\delta_{2}
. We can take the incenter of
p_{3}p_{4}p_{5}
as
p
.

The same procedure applies if
\beta
or
\gamma
are at least
\delta_{1}
. Hence, it remains to suppose that all
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\leq\delta_{1}
.

Let
q
be the intersection of the altitudes and let the foot of the altitude divide
p_{1}p_{2}
in the segments of lengths
a_{1},a_{2}
respectively. We define similarly
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}
,
b_{1},b_{2}
and so on (in the cyclic order). By
h_{a}
denote
d_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(q,p_{1}p_{2})
.
Then

\tanh(h_{a})=\cosh(a_{1})\tanh(\beta_{2}).

From Lemma
2.4
we can also express

\tanh(\beta_{2})=\frac{\coth(\gamma)}{\cosh(a)}.

Substitute this and get

\tanh(h_{a})=\frac{\cosh(a_{1})\cosh(\gamma)}{\cosh(a)\sinh(\gamma)}=\frac{\cosh(a_{1})\cosh(\gamma)\sinh(\beta)}{\cosh(\beta)\cosh(\gamma)+\cosh(\alpha)}=

=\frac{\sinh(a_{1})\cosh(a_{1})\cosh(\gamma)\sinh(\beta)}{\sinh(a_{1})(\cosh(\beta)\cosh(\gamma)+\cosh(\alpha))}=\frac{\coth(a_{1})\cosh(\gamma){\cosh(\alpha_{1})}}{\cosh(\beta)\cosh(\gamma)+\cosh(\alpha)}.

Here we used Lemma
2.4
and Lemma
2.5
. It is not hard to see that under the condition
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\leq\delta_{1}
the last expression is bounded away from zero. Hence, for some
\delta_{3}=\delta_{3}(\delta_{1})>0
we have
h_{a}\geq\delta_{3}
.

Let
q_{a}
be the foot of the altitude to
p_{1}p_{2}
. Either
a_{1}
or
a_{2}
is
\geq\Delta/2
. Assume that
a_{1}\geq\Delta/2
. Consider the right-angled triangle
qq_{a}p_{1}
. There exists
\delta_{4}=\delta_{4}(\delta_{1},\Delta)>0
such that since
a_{1}\geq\Delta/2
and
h_{a}\geq\delta_{3}
, the inradius of
qq_{a}p_{1}
is at least
\delta_{4}
. In this case we can take the incenter of
qq_{a}p_{1}
as
p
.

Hence, we can set
\delta
as the minimum of
\delta_{2}
and
\delta_{4}
.
∎

Corollary 2.8
.

There exists a continuous function
\delta=\delta(\Delta):\mathbb{R}_{>0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that if
P
is a hyperbolic pair of pants with the length of each boundary component at least
\Delta>0
, then for some
p\in P
we have
d(p,\partial P)>\delta
, where
d
is the metric on
P
.

Until the end of the paper we assume that we fixed a function
\delta
from Corollary
2.8
.

2.2
Cone-metrics on surfaces

Let
S
be a closed surface and
V\subset S
be a finite set of marked points. In this section we review some facts about hyperbolic cone-metrics on
S
. In what follows we will be saying just “cone-metric” always meaning “hyperbolic cone-metric”. We denote the set of cone-points of a cone-metric
d
by
V(d)
. We say that
d
is a cone-metric on
(S,V)
if
d
is a cone-metric on
S
and
V\supseteq V(d)
. For
v\in V
we denote its cone-angle by
\kappa_{v}(d)
or just
\kappa_{v}
(which is equal to
2\pi
if
v\notin V(d)
).

Definition 2.9
.

A
triangulation

\mathcal{T}
of
(S,V)
is a collection of simple disjoint arcs with endpoints in
V
that cut
S
into triangles. Here a triangle is a topological 2-disk with three marked points at the boundary (possibly coinciding as points of
V
). We call two triangulations of
(S,V)

equivalent
if they are isotopic on
S
by an isotopy fixing
V
.

We denote the set of edges of
\mathcal{T}
be
E(\mathcal{T})
.

Definition 2.10
.

A
geodesic triangulation
of
(S,V,d)
is a triangulation of
(S,V)
such that all edges are geodesics in
d
.

Definition 2.11
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a triangulation of
(S,V)
and
d
be a cone-metric on
(S,V)
. We say that
\mathcal{T}
is
realized
by
d
if there is a geodesic triangulation of
(S,V,d)
equivalent to
\mathcal{T}
.

It is not hard to notice that for every cone-metric
d
on
(S,V)
there exists a geodesic triangulation of
(S,V,d)
. Of special importance are
Delaunay triangulations
. Generically a Delaunay triangulation of
(S,V,d)
is unique, but in some cases it is not and we need to consider a more general
Delaunay decomposition
.

Definition 2.12
.

Let
d
be a cone-metric on
(S,V)
. A
Delaunay decomposition
of
(S,V,d)
is a collection of simple disjoint geodesic arcs with endpoints in
V
that cut
S
into cells isometric to compact convex hyperbolic polygons such that each of the polygons can be inscribed in a circle and if we develop any two adjacent cells to
\mathbb{H}^{2}
, then the closed disk bounded by the circumcircle of each polygon does not contain vertices of the other polygon except the endpoints of the common edge.

Note that here a priori by a circle we mean either a metric circle in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
, or a horocycle, or a hypercycle. However, it is known that actually the circumcircle of each cell in the Delaunay decomposition of a closed surface is a metric circle, see
[
43
, Theorem 14]
or
[
73
, Remark 3.7]
. Another well-known fact is

Theorem 2.13

(
[
59
]
, Section 4)
.

A Delaunay decomposition of
(S,V,d)
exists and is unique.

Definition 2.14
.

A
Delaunay triangulation
of
(S,V,d)
is a triangulation subdividing the Delaunay decomposition.

In particular, there are finitely many Delaunay triangulations, and any two can be connected by a finite sequence of flips via Delaunay triangulations.

For a map
f:(S_{1},d_{1})\rightarrow(S_{2},d_{2})
between two compact metric spaces we define its
distortion

{\rm dist}(f)
as

{\rm dist}(f):=\sup_{p,q\in S_{1},~p\neq q}\left|\ln\frac{d_{2}(f(p),f(q))}{d_{1}(p,q)}\right|.

We say that a sequence of metrics
\{d_{i}\}
on a surface
S
converges to a metric
d

in the strong Lipschitz sense
if the identity maps
(S,d_{i})\rightarrow(S,d)
have distortions
\varepsilon_{i}\rightarrow 0
. By
\mathfrak{D}(S,V)
we denote the space of hyperbolic cone-metrics on
(S,V)
endowed with the topology of the strong Lipschitz convergence.

We need to introduce two equivalence relations on
\mathfrak{D}(S,V)
. Let
H_{0}(S,V)
be the group of self-homeomorphisms of
S
fixing
V
and isotopic to identity, endowed with the compact-open topology, and let
H_{0}^{\sharp}(S,V)
be its normal subgroup consisting of homeomorphisms for which the isotopy can be chosen to fix
V
. Denote the respective quotients of
\mathfrak{D}(S,V)
by
\mathcal{D}(S,V)
and
\mathcal{D}^{\sharp}(S,V)
. It follows that
\mathcal{D}(S,V)
is the quotient of
\mathcal{D}^{\sharp}(S,V)
by
the pure braid group
of
(S,V)
, which is
B_{0}(S,V):=H_{0}(S,V)/H_{0}^{\sharp}(S,V)
. In most of the paper we will use the space
\mathcal{D}(S,V)
, but for technical reasons we also have to appeal to
\mathcal{D}^{\sharp}(S,V)
. We will abuse the notation, and denote by
d
sometimes a metric, sometimes its class in
\mathcal{D}(S,V)
, but we will denote the elements of
\mathcal{D}^{\sharp}(S,V)
by
d^{\sharp}
.

For a triangulation
\mathcal{T}
of
(S,V)
by
\mathfrak{D}(S,\mathcal{T})\subset\mathfrak{D}(S,V)
and
\mathcal{D}^{\sharp}(S,\mathcal{T})\subset\mathcal{D}^{\sharp}(S,V)
we denote the subsets of (classes of) metrics realizing
\mathcal{T}
. It is evident that if
d^{\sharp}
realizes
\mathcal{T}
, then
d^{\sharp}
is determined by
\mathcal{T}
and by the edge lengths. Hence, the set
\mathcal{D}^{\sharp}(S,\mathcal{T})
can be considered as an open polyhedron in
\mathbb{R}^{E(\mathcal{T})}_{+}
defined by strict triangle inequalities. The charts
\mathcal{D}^{\sharp}(S,\mathcal{T})
endow
\mathcal{D}^{\sharp}(S,V)
with the structure of a smooth manifold of dimension
3(n-k)
where
n:=|V|
and
k:=\chi(S)
. It is not hard to check that this topology coincides with the topology induced from
\mathfrak{D}(S,V)
.
One can also check that
B_{0}(S,V)
acts on
\mathcal{D}^{\sharp}(S,V)
freely and properly discontinuously. Thus,
\mathcal{D}(S,V)
also gets endowed with the structure of a smooth manifold so that the projection
\mathcal{D}^{\sharp}(S,V)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(S,V)
is a covering map.

It is known from the works
[
91
]
of Troyanov or
[
43
]
of Gu–Guo–Luo–Sun–Wu that
\mathcal{D}^{\sharp}(S,V)
is diffeomorphic to the product of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with the Teichmüller space
\mathcal{T}(S,V)
of
(S,V)
, hence

Theorem 2.15
.

The space
\mathcal{D}^{\sharp}(S,V)
is diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{3(n-k)}
.

By
\mathfrak{D}_{c}(S,V)
we denote the subset of
\mathfrak{D}_{c}(S,V)
consisting of convex cone-metrics with
V=V(d)
. By
\overline{\mathfrak{D}}_{c}(S,V)
we denote the subset of
\mathfrak{D}_{c}(S,V)
consisting of convex cone-metrics. It is easy to see that
\mathfrak{D}_{c}(S,V)
is open in
\mathfrak{D}_{c}(S,V)
and
\overline{\mathfrak{D}}_{c}(S,V)
is its closure. The same notation applies to the respective subsets of
\mathcal{D}(S,V)
and
\mathcal{D}^{\sharp}(S,V)
.

By
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{l}
we denote the metric circle of length
l
. A
closed
\varepsilon
-quasi-geodesic
is the image of
S^{1}_{l}
under an
\varepsilon
-quasi-isometry, i.e., under a map
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{l}\rightarrow(S,d)
with distortion at most
\varepsilon
. The following consequence of the Arzelá–Ascoli theorem was shown in
[
78
, Theorem 6.1]
:

Lemma 2.16
.

Let
\{d_{i}\}
be a sequence of metrics on
S
converging in the strong Lipschitz sense to a metric
d
. Let
\{\psi_{i}\subset S\}
be closed
\varepsilon_{i}
-quasi-geodesics in
d_{i}
with
\varepsilon_{i}\rightarrow 0
and the lengths
l_{i}
of
\psi_{i}
converging to
l>0
. Then, up to extracting a subsequence,
\psi_{i}
converge to a closed geodesic
\psi\subset S
in
d
of length
l
.

We recall the following well-known notion

Definition 2.17
.

Let
d
be a metric on
S
. The
systole

\sys(d)
of
d
is the infimum of lengths of non-contractible loops of
S
.

Lemma
2.16
implies that the infimum is achieved.

Lemma 2.18
.

The function
\sys(.)
is continuous over
\mathfrak{D}(S,V)
.

Proof.

Let
\{d_{i}\}
be a sequence of metrics on
S
converging in the strong Lipschitz sense to a metric
d
and
\{\psi_{i}\subset S\}
be a sequence of systolic loops. Lemma
2.16
shows that, up to taking a subsequence, they converge to a loop
\psi\subset S
of length
\liminf_{i\rightarrow\infty}\sys(d_{i})
. Obviously, since
\psi_{i}
are non-contractible, so is
\psi
. Hence

\liminf_{i\rightarrow\infty}\sys(d_{i})\geq\sys(d).

On the other hand, if
\psi^{\prime}
is a systolic loop of
d
, then for a sequence
\varepsilon_{i}\rightarrow 0
the lengths of
\psi^{\prime}
in
d_{i}
is
\sys(d)+\varepsilon_{i}
. Thus,

\limsup_{i\rightarrow\infty}\sys(d_{i})\leq\sys(d).

∎

2.2.1
Balanced cone-metrics

Definition 2.19
.

Let
S
be a closed surface,
V\subset S
be a finite set and
d
be a metric on
S
. The
sparsity

\sp(V,d)
of
V
in
d
is

\sp(V,d):=\sup_{p\in S}d(p,V).

For a cone-metric
d
we define its
cone-sparsity

\cosp(d):=\sp(V(d),d)
.

Due to compactness, it is easy to see that there exist
p\in S
,
v\in V
such that
d(p,v)=\sp(V,d)
.

Lemma 2.20
.

The function
cosp
is upper semicontinuous over
\mathfrak{D}(S,V)
.

Proof.

Let
\{d_{i}\}
be a sequence of cone-metrics on
(S,V)
converging in the strong Lipschitz sense to a metric
d
and
\{p_{i}\subset S\}
be a sequence of points realizing
\cosp(d_{i})
. Up to taking a subsequence, they converge to a point
p\in S
. Since for each
v\in V
the function
\kappa_{v}
is continuous over
\mathfrak{D}(S,V)
, if
v\notin V(d_{i})
for infinitely many
d_{i}
, then
v\notin V(d)
. Hence,

d(p,V(d))\geq\limsup_{i\rightarrow\infty}d(p_{i},V(d_{i})),

\cosp(d)\geq\limsup_{i\rightarrow\infty}\cosp(d_{i}).

∎

Definition 2.21
.

A cone-metric
d
on a surface
S
is called
balanced
if

\cosp(d)<\delta(\sys(d)),

where
\delta
is the function from Corollary
2.8
.

By
{\mathfrak{D}}_{bc}(S,V)
we denote the subset of
\mathfrak{D}(S,V)
consisting of balanced convex cone-metrics. Lemma
2.18
and Lemma
2.20
imply

Lemma 2.22
.

The set
{\mathfrak{D}}_{bc}(S,V)
is open.

For two metrics
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
on
S

the Lipschitz distance
between
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
is defined as the infimum of the distortions of all homeomorphisms
(S,d_{1})\rightarrow(S,d_{2})
. We say that a sequence of metrics
\{d_{i}\}
on a surface
S
converges to a metric
d

in the Lipschitz sense
if the Lipschitz distance between them tends to zero. The obtained Lipschitz topology is one of the most common to endow the space of all metrics on
S
. Now we are going to prove Proposition
1.15
stating that the set of convex balanced cone-metrics is open and dense in the space of all convex cone-metrics on
S
equipped with the Lipschitz topology. It is evident that it is dense, so it remains only to show that it is open. It follows from

Lemma 2.23
.

The function
\cosp
is upper semicontinuous on the space of all cone-metrics on
S
in the Lipschitz topology.

Proof.

The proof is almost identical to the proof of Lemma
2.20
, but we need to establish one additional fact. Let
\{d_{i}\}
be a sequence of hyperbolic cone-metrics converging to a hyperbolic cone-metric
d
in the Lipschitz sense.

Claim 2.23.1
.

For each
v\in V(d)
there exists a sequence
\{v_{i}\in V(d_{i})\}
converging to
v
.

Indeed, otherwise there exists
v\in V(d)
, a small neighborhood
U
of
v
not containing other point of
V(d)
, a domain
U^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{H}^{2}
and a sequence of homeomorphisms
f_{i}:U\rightarrow U^{\prime}
with distortions converging to zero. Let
\sigma
be a circle of small radius
r>0
around
v
in
U
. Then
f_{i}(\sigma)
converges in the Hausdorff sense to a circle of radius
r
around
f(u)
in
U^{\prime}
. Hence, their lengths must converge. On the other hand, their lengths are clearly different.

The rest of the proof is straightforward.
∎

Definition 2.24
.

For a cone-metric
d
and
V\supseteq V(d)
we call
V

balancing

d
if

\sp(V,d)<\delta(\sys(d))

where
\delta
is the function from Corollary
2.8
.

By
\overline{\mathfrak{D}}_{bc}(S,V)
we denote the subset of
\mathfrak{D}_{c}(S,V)
consisting of metrics, for which
V
is balancing. Note that in contrast with
\overline{\mathfrak{D}}_{c}(S,V)
, which is the closure of
{\mathfrak{D}}_{c}(S,V)
,
\overline{\mathfrak{D}}_{bc}(S,V)
is not the closure of
{\mathfrak{D}}_{bc}(S,V)
in
{\mathfrak{D}}(S,V)
as we do not add metrics with
\sp(V,d)=\delta(\sys(d))
. In particular,
\overline{\mathfrak{D}}_{bc}(S,V)
is open in
\overline{\mathfrak{D}}_{c}(S,V)
endowed with the subspace topology.

Now we note that the functions
\sys(.)
,
\cosp(.)
and
\sp(.)
are constant over
H_{0}(S,V)
-orbits. Thereby, we can define similar subsets of the spaces
\mathcal{D}(S,V)
and
\mathcal{D}^{\sharp}(S,V)
, for which we use the obvious notation.

Balanced convex cone-metrics play a special role in our paper. Of particular importance are the “weak” connectivity and simple-connectivity properties of
\mathcal{D}_{c}(S,V)
,
\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\sharp}(S,V)
through balanced convex cone-metrics, Lemmas
2.27
and
2.28
. To obtain them, first we need the following statement.

Lemma 2.25
.

Let
d^{\sharp}\in\mathcal{D}^{\sharp}(S,V)
,
\mathcal{T}
be its Delaunay triangulation and
t\geq 0
be a number. For each edge
e\in E(\mathcal{T})
of length
l_{e}
in
d^{\sharp}
we set a new length
l_{e,t}
given by

\sinh\left(\frac{l_{e,t}}{2}\right):=e^{t}\sinh\left(\frac{l_{e}}{2}\right).

Then the new edge-lengths define a metric class
d_{t}^{\sharp}\in\mathcal{D}^{\sharp}(S,V)
with the same Delaunay decomposition as
d^{\sharp}
and for every
v\in V
and
t>0
we have
\kappa_{v}(d_{t}^{\sharp})<\kappa_{v}(d^{\sharp})
.

Proof.

Let us first show that
d_{t}^{\sharp}
is well-defined, i.e., that the obtained edge-lengths satisfy the triangle inequalities for each triangle of
\mathcal{T}
. Consider the function

f(x):=2\arcsinh(e^{t/2}\sinh(x/2)).

Its derivatives are given by

f^{\prime}(x)=\frac{e^{t/2}\cosh(x/2)}{\sqrt{e^{t}\sinh^{2}(x/2)+1}},

f^{\prime\prime}(x)=\frac{e^{t/2}\sinh(x/2)(1-e^{t})}{2(e^{t}\sinh^{2}(x/2)+1)^{3/2}}.

Hence, for
t>0
and
x>0
it is strictly increasing and concave. Since
f(0)=0
,
f
is subadditive under these conditions. Hence, if three edge-lengths of
\mathcal{T}
in
d^{\sharp}
satisfy the triangle inequalities, the modified lengths satisfy them too.

It was shown in
[
43
, Proposition 10]
that a triangulation
\mathcal{T}
is Delaunay for
d^{\sharp}
if and only if for every two adjacent triangles with the length of the common edge
l_{0}
and with the other edge-lengths
l_{1}
and
l_{2}
in one triangle and
l^{\prime}_{0}
,
l^{\prime}_{1}
in another, we have

\frac{\sinh^{2}(l_{1}/2)+\sinh^{2}(l_{2}/2)-\sinh^{2}(l_{0}/2)}{\sinh(l_{1}/2)\sinh(l_{2}/2)}+\frac{\sinh^{2}(l^{\prime}_{1}/2)+\sinh^{2}(l^{\prime}_{2}/2)-\sinh^{2}(l_{0}/2)}{\sinh(l^{\prime}_{1}/2)\sinh(l^{\prime}_{2}/2)}\geq 0.

This implies that
\mathcal{T}
remains Delaunay in all
d_{t}^{\sharp}
. Moreover, Lemma
2.6
implies that
d_{t}^{\sharp}
are independent on the choice of a Delaunay triangulation
\mathcal{T}
.

It remains to show that for all
t
and all
v
we have
\partial\kappa_{v}(d_{t}^{\sharp})/\partial t<0
. This was basically done in
[
73
, Lemma 5.3.iii]
. It is enough to consider
t=0
. Let
\overline{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{V}
be a vector close to zero. Define a length
l_{e,\overline{t}}
for
e\in E(\mathcal{T})
with endpoints
v
and
w
by

\sinh\left(\frac{l_{e,\overline{t}}}{2}\right):=\exp\left(\frac{\overline{t}_{v}+\overline{t}_{w}}{2}\right)\sinh\left(\frac{l_{e}}{2}\right).

If
\overline{t}
is sufficiently close to zero, then all triangle inequalities still hold for the new lengths and define a cone-metric class on
(S,V)
, which we denote by
d_{\overline{t}}^{\sharp}
. For
v,w\in V
we denote
\partial\kappa_{v}(d_{\overline{t}}^{\sharp})/\partial t_{w}
by
X_{vw}
. Lemma 5.3.iii in
[
73
]
states that for each
v
we have
\sum_{w\in V}X_{vw}<0
, which implies the desired claim. Note that
[
73
]
uses a slightly different setting, but the connection with the present context is explained in
[
73
, Section 4.3]
.
∎

We employ Lemma
2.25
to prove the following fact.

Lemma 2.26
.

Let
D\subset\mathcal{D}^{\sharp}(S,V)
be a compactum. There exists an isotopy
\iota_{t}:D\hookrightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\sharp}(S,V)
,
t\in[0,1]
, of the inclusion map
\iota_{0}:D\hookrightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\sharp}(S,V)
such that
\iota_{1}(D)\subset\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\sharp}(S,V)
and for each
d^{\sharp}\in D
the cone-angle
\kappa_{v}
of each
v\in V
strictly decreases along the respective path
\{\iota_{t}(d^{\sharp}):t\in[0,1]\}
. In particular, if
D\subset\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{c}^{\sharp}(S,V)
, then
\iota_{t}(D)\subset\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\sharp}(S,V)
for all
t>0
.

Proof.

For each
d^{\sharp}\in D
choose a Delaunay triangulation
\mathcal{T}
and define
\iota_{t}(d^{\sharp})
by setting for each edge
e\in E(\mathcal{T})
and for each
t>0

\sinh\left(\frac{l_{e}(\iota_{t}(d))}{2}\right):=e^{t}\sinh\left(\frac{l_{e}(d)}{2}\right),

where
l_{e}(d^{\sharp})
is the length of
e
in metric
d^{\sharp}
.
Lemma
2.25
shows that this indeed defines a class of metrics in
\mathcal{D}^{\sharp}(S,\mathcal{T})
and that for each
v\in V
the cone-angle
\kappa_{v}
strictly decreases. Lemma
2.6
implies that
\iota_{t}(d^{\sharp})
is independent on the choice of a Delaunay triangulation. This means that
\iota_{t}
is a continuous homotopy. Thus, for some
t_{0}>0
we obtain
\iota_{t_{0}}(D)\subset\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\sharp}(S,V)
. It only remains to reparametrize the obtained path. If
D\subset\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{c}^{\sharp}(S,V)
, then
\iota_{t}(D)\subset\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\sharp}(S,V)
for all
t>0
as the cone-angles strictly decrease.
∎

Now we can obtain the desired connectivity properties.

Lemma 2.27
.

For every
d_{0},~d_{1}\in\mathcal{D}_{c}(S,V)
there exist
W\supseteq V
and a continuous path
\alpha:[0,1]\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{bc}(S,W)
such that
\alpha(0)
,
\alpha(1)
are lifts of
d_{0}
,
d_{1}
, and for all
t\in(0,1)
we have
\alpha(t)\in\mathcal{D}_{bc}(S,W)
.

Proof.

Let
d_{0}^{\sharp},d_{1}^{\sharp}
be some lifts of
d_{0},d_{1}
to
\mathcal{D}^{\sharp}(S,V)
and
\alpha^{\prime}:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\sharp}(S,V)
be a path connecting them. Lemma
2.26
shows that it can be transformed to a path
\alpha^{\prime\prime}:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\sharp}(S,V)
connecting
d_{0}^{\sharp}
and
d_{1}^{\sharp}
: just do the respective homotopy and use as
\alpha^{\prime\prime}
the concatenation of the upper and the horizontal sides of the homotopy square. Lift
\alpha^{\prime\prime}
to a path
\mathfrak{a}^{\prime\prime}:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathfrak{D}_{c}(S,V)
. By compactness of the image of
\mathfrak{a}^{\prime\prime}
, we can choose a sufficiently dense set
W\subset S
such that
W
balances
\mathfrak{a}^{\prime\prime}(t)
for all
t
. This projects to a path
\alpha^{\prime\prime\prime}:[0,1]\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{bc}^{\sharp}(S,W)
. Applying once again Lemma
2.26
and projecting to
\mathcal{D}(S,W)
, we obtain the desired path
\alpha
with the property
\alpha(t)\in\mathcal{D}_{bc}(S,W)
for all
t\in(0,1)
.
∎

We highlight that while the previous lemma is equally true with
\mathcal{D}_{c}(S,V)
replaced by
\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\sharp}(S,V)
, the lemma below is true only for
\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\sharp}(S,V)
, and it is the main reason for us to introduce
\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\sharp}(S,V)
.

Lemma 2.28
.

Let
\beta:S^{1}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{c}^{\sharp}(S,V)
be a continuous loop. Then there exist
W\supseteq V
and a lift
\alpha:S^{1}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{c}^{\sharp}(S,W)
of
\beta
such that
\alpha(S^{1})\subset\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{bc}^{\sharp}(S,W)
and
\alpha
is contractible in
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{bc}^{\sharp}(S,W)
.

Proof.

By
\overline{D}^{2}
we denote the closed topological 2-disk with the boundary identified with
S^{1}
. By Theorem
2.15
,
\mathcal{D}^{\sharp}(S,V)
is contractible. Let
\xi^{\prime}:\overline{D}^{2}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\sharp}(S,V)
be a map contracting
\beta
in
\mathcal{D}^{\sharp}(S,V)
. Lemma
2.26
allows us to obtain from
\xi^{\prime}
a map
\xi^{\prime\prime}:\overline{D}^{2}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{c}^{\sharp}(S,V)
contracting
\beta
.
Since
\mathfrak{D}(S,V)
is a trivial topological bundle over
\mathcal{D}^{\sharp}(S,V)
(see
[
72
, Section 2.2]
), we can lift
\xi^{\prime\prime}
to a map
\mathfrak{x}^{\prime\prime}:\overline{D}^{2}\rightarrow\mathfrak{D}(S,V)
.
By compactness of the image of
\mathfrak{x}^{\prime\prime}
, we can choose a sufficiently dense set
W\subset S
such that
W
balances
\mathfrak{x}^{\prime\prime}(z)
for all
z\in\overline{D}^{2}
. This projects to the desired map
\xi:\overline{D}^{2}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{bc}^{\sharp}(S,W)
such that
\xi|_{S^{1}}
is a lift of
\beta
.
∎

2.3
Hyperbolic cone-3-manifolds

A hyperbolic cone-3-manifold is a 3-dimensional generalization of a hyperbolic cone-surface. We do not require that much theory about the set of cone-metrics on a given 3-manifold as we do about cone-metrics on surfaces, so we present the former theory in a simplified form.

Definition 2.29
.

A hyperbolic cone-3-manifold
is a pair
(K,\Sigma)
, where
K
is a 3-manifold, possibly with boundary, equipped with an intrinsic metric and
\Sigma
is an embedded geodesic graph such that

•

every interior point belonging to
K\backslash\Sigma
has a neighborhood isometric to a domain in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
;

•

every boundary point belonging to
K\backslash\Sigma
has a neighborhood isometric to a domain of a boundary point in a half-space of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
;

•

every interior point belonging to
\Sigma
has a neighborhood isometric to a cone of curvature
-1
over a 2-dimensional spherical cone-surface homeomorphic to the 2-sphere, but not isometric to the standard sphere;

•

every boundary point belonging to
\Sigma
has a neighborhood isometric to a cone of curvature
-1
over a 2-dimensional spherical cone-surface with boundary, homeomorphic to the 2-disk, but not isometric to a half of the standard sphere.

We refer to
[
28
,
45
,
60
,
96
]
for an introduction to the theory of closed hyperbolic cone-3-manifolds. If a point of
\Sigma
has a neighborhood isometric to a cone over the sphere with two conical points (in the case of an interior point), or to a spherical lune (in the case of a boundary point), we call it an
edge point
, otherwise we call it a
vertex
.

The main source of cone-3-manifolds are gluings of hyperbolic polyhedra along isometric 2-dimensional faces. We say that a cone-3-manifold
K
is
triangulated
if
\mathcal{T}
is its geodesic triangulation such that all vertices of
\mathcal{T}
belong to
\Sigma
and
\Sigma
is included in the 1-skeleton of
\mathcal{T}
. We note that we do not necessarily require that all vertices of
\mathcal{T}
are vertices of
\Sigma
or that all edges of
\mathcal{T}
belong to
\Sigma
. A triangulated hyperbolic cone-3-manifold
(K,\Sigma,\mathcal{T})
is uniquely determined by the topology of
(K,\mathcal{T})
and by the edge lengths of
\mathcal{T}
. Let
(K,\mathcal{T})
be a triangulated 3-manifold and
E(\mathcal{T})
be the set of edges. There is a domain
\mathcal{C}(K,\mathcal{T})\subset\mathbb{R}^{E(\mathcal{T})}
corresponding to the condition that every
l\in\mathcal{C}(K,\mathcal{T})
turns every tetrahedron of
\mathcal{T}
to a non-degenerate hyperbolic tetrahedron. Hence, we can consider
\mathcal{C}(K,\mathcal{T})
as the space of hyperbolic cone-structures on
(K,\mathcal{T})
. Note, however, that the singular locus of such structures is variable, though it stays belonging to the union of the edges of
\mathcal{T}
. For
e\in E(\mathcal{T})
and
l\in\mathcal{C}(K,\mathcal{T})
by
\nu_{e}
we denote the total dihedral angle of
e
in the cone-structure determined by
l
. This is an analytic function over
\mathcal{C}(K,\mathcal{T})
.

Our proof of the infinitesimal rigidity is based on a comparison of infinitesimal variations of
l\in\mathcal{C}(K,\mathcal{T})
with the standard approach to the infinitesimal variations of hyperbolic cone-structures via the bundle of infinitesimal isometries developed in
[
45
,
96
,
97
]
. This occupies Section
4
.

In what follows we will need triangulations with few special properties

Definition 2.30
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a triangulation of a hyperbolic cone-3-manifold
(K,\Sigma)
, and
L:=K\backslash\Sigma
. The triangulation
\mathcal{T}
is called
large
if for every tetrahedron
\widetilde{T}
of
\mathcal{T}
lifted to
\widetilde{L}
and every
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(L)
,
\gamma\neq e
, the tetrahedron
\gamma\widetilde{T}
does not share with
\widetilde{T}
any 2-face, or any edge coming from
e\in E(\mathcal{T})
that is not in
\Sigma
. It is called
super-large
if
\gamma\widetilde{T}
never shares a vertex with
\widetilde{T}
.

2.4
Convex cocompact hyperbolic 3-manifolds

This section largely follows Section 2.3 in
[
74
]
. Here we repeat the basic definitions and give a brief summary of the basic facts that we will use, but for additional explanations we refer to
[
74
, Section 2.3]
.

Recall that
M
is an oriented smooth compact 3-manifold with non-empty boundary such that its interior
N
admits a cocompact hyperbolic metric, and that
M
is distinct from the solid torus. Let
g
be a hyperbolic metric on
M
such that
\partial M
is locally convex (which implies that it is globally convex). It is well-known that

Lemma 2.31
.

There exists a unique up to isotopy convex cocompact metric
\overline{g}
on
N
such that there is a (unique) isometric embedding
(M,g)\hookrightarrow(N,\overline{g})
such that the complement to the image is a neighborhood of
\partial M
intersected with
N
, homeomorphic to
\partial M\times(0,1)
, and the induced map
\pi_{1}(\partial M)\rightarrow\pi_{1}(\partial M)
is identity.

For a proof look the beginning of
[
74
, Section 2.3]
.
Recall that we are going to use the notation
\overline{g}
to denote the metric on
N
obtained from
g
by Lemma
2.31
, but sometimes we will just write
\overline{g}
to denote a convex cocompact metric on
N
in the absence of metric
g
.
For a convex cocompact hyperbolic metric
\overline{g}
we denote the hyperbolic manifold
(N,\overline{g})
by
N(\overline{g})
. We denote the image of
(M,g)
in
N(\overline{g})
under the embedding from Lemma
2.31
by
M(g)\subset N(\overline{g})
. By
\partial_{\infty}N(\overline{g})
we denote the boundary at infinity of
N(\overline{g})
, i.e.,
\partial M
equipped with the respective conformal structure.

By
G
we denote the group
\rm{Iso}^{+}(\mathbb{H}^{3})
of orientation-preserving isometries of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
, which we identify with
PSL(2,\mathbb{C})
. We will frequently assume that some developing map
\widetilde{N}(\overline{g})\rightarrow\mathbb{H}^{3}
is fixed, so we identify the universal cover
\widetilde{N}(\overline{g})
with
\mathbb{H}^{3}
and
\widetilde{M}(g)
with a convex subset of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
. By
\rho_{\overline{g}}:\pi_{1}(M)\rightarrow G
we denote the corresponding holonomy representation. By
\Lambda(\rho_{\overline{g}})=\Lambda(\overline{g})
we denote its limit set and by
\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
we denote its convex hull. The latter is
\rho_{\overline{g}}
-invariant and projects to the
convex core

C(\overline{g})\subset N(\overline{g})
, an alternative definition of which was given in the introduction as Definition
1.10
.

We recall that
\overline{g}
and
\rho_{\overline{g}}
are called
Fuchsian
if
\Lambda(\overline{g})
is contained in a circle. In the Fuchsian case
C(\overline{g})
is 2-dimensional, and we will frequently have to say something exceptional about Fuchsian metrics. We need to distinguish the subcase when
\Lambda(\overline{g})
is a circle itself, so
\overline{g}
is called
Fuchsian of the first kind
. It is called
Fuchsian of the second kind
in the remaining case when
\Lambda(\overline{g})
is a proper subset of a circle. In the former case
M
is the oriented interval bundle over a closed surface and
C(\overline{g})
is a totally geodesic surface. In the latter case
M
is the oriented interval bundle over a compact surface with non-empty boundary, i.e., a handlebody and
C(\overline{g})
is a totally geodesic surface with non-empty geodesic relative boundary. A well-known observation of Thurston is

Lemma 2.32
.

Let
\overline{g}
be non-Fuchsian. The induced intrinsic metric on
\partial C(\overline{g})
is hyperbolic.

See, e.g.,
[
32
]
and
[
85
]
for a detailed proof. Thus, a connected component of
\partial C(\overline{g})
is an embedded hyperbolic surface, which is, however, non-geodesic as it may be bent along a geodesic lamination.

By
\mathcal{CH}(N)
we denote the space of convex cocompact hyperbolc metrics on
N
up to isotopy. As before, we will not introduce a special notation for an equivalence class of metrics, and will write
\overline{g}
sometimes for a precise metric, and sometimes for an equivalence class. By
\mathcal{T}(\partial M)
we denote the Teichmüller space of
\partial M
. The following version of the generalized Ahlfors–Bers theorem holds

Theorem 2.33
.

The map
\mathcal{AB}:\mathcal{CH}(N)\rightarrow\mathcal{T}(\partial M)
sending
\overline{g}
to the conformal structure at infinity of
\partial_{\infty}N(\overline{g})
is a bijection.

We did not find an exact reference with this formulation of this result, but in
[
74
, Section 2.3]
we explain how to derive it from the existing literature.
We endow
\mathcal{CH}(N)
with the smooth topology of
\mathcal{T}(\partial M)
. In order to introduce in what follows the topology on the space of bent metrics on
M
we need to recall few other objects from
[
74
, Section 2.3]
. By
\mathcal{CH}_{h}(N)
we denote the space of convex cocompact hyperbolic metrics on
N
up to homotopy. By
MCG_{h}(M)
we denote the group of isotopy classes of self-diffeomorphisms of
M
homotopic to identity. We recal that in the case of incompressible boundary Waldhausen
[
94
]
showed that
MCG_{h}(M)
is trivial, while the case of compressible boundary is considered in the monograph
[
61
]
of McCullough and Miller. The group
MCG_{h}(M)
acts freely and properly discontinuously on
\mathcal{CH}(N)
and
\mathcal{T}(\partial M)
. The quotient of the former is exactly
\mathcal{CH}_{h}(N)
, the quotient of the latter is denoted by
\mathcal{T}_{h}(\partial M,M)
. The map
\mathcal{AB}
commutes with the action of
MCG_{h}(M)
, hence
\mathcal{CH}_{h}(N)
and
\mathcal{T}_{h}(\partial M,M)
are diffeomorphic. Note, however, that there is another more standard way to introduce the topology on
\mathcal{CH}_{h}(N)
. By
\mathcal{R}(\pi_{1}(M),G)
we denote the space of representations of
\pi_{1}(M)
in
G
endowed with the topology of a complex algebraic variety coming from the complex algebraic group structure on
G
, and by
\mathcal{X}(\pi_{1}(M),G)
we denote the quotient of
\mathcal{R}(\pi_{1}(M),G)
by the conjugation action of
G
. The developing maps allow us to embed
\mathcal{CH}_{h}(N)
into
\mathcal{X}(\pi_{1}(M),G)
. The global topology of
\mathcal{X}(\pi_{1}(M),G)
is not well-behaved, but results of Marden
[
56
]
imply that the image of
\mathcal{CH}_{h}(N)
belongs to the smooth part of
\mathcal{X}(\pi_{1}(M),G)
, so this embedding endows
\mathcal{CH}_{h}(N)
with the topology of a complex manifold. It is another part of the Ahlfors–Bers theorem that these topologies on
\mathcal{CH}_{h}(N)
coincide. See
[
74
, Section 2.3]
for more explanations.

We will use the following result on the behavior of limit sets under the convergence in
\mathcal{R}(\pi_{1}(M),G)
.

Theorem 2.34
.

Let
\{\rho_{i}:\pi_{1}(M)\rightarrow G\}
be a sequence converging in
\mathcal{R}(\pi_{1}(M),G)
to
\rho:\pi_{1}(M)\rightarrow G
such that all
\rho_{i}
and
\rho
are holonomy maps of convex cocompact hyperbolic metrics on
N
. Then
\Lambda(\rho_{i})
converge to
\Lambda(\rho)
in the Hausdorff sense.

See Proposition 4.5.3 in
[
57
]
or Theorem E in
[
5
]
.

3
Hyperbolic manifolds with boundary of polyhedral type

3.1
Polyhedral and bent manifolds

We start to study bent metrics on
M
.
Denote the set of vertices of a bent metric
g
by
V(g)
. If
p\in\partial M
belongs to the relative interiors of two geodesic segments with distinct tangents at
p
, then the convexity implies that
p
has a neighborhood isometric to a neighborhood of a point in the boundary of a half-space in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
. Then we say that
p
is
regular
in
g
. A connected component of the set of regular points is called a
face
of
g
. If
p
is not a vertex and not regular, it is called an
edge-point
in
g
. Let it belong to the relative interior of a geodesic segment
\psi
. There is a neighborhood of
p
in the relative interior of
\psi
consisting only of edge-points. A maximal geodesic segment consisting of edge-points is called an
edge
of
g
. It is straightforward that the edges are disjoint and simple. We can also speak about bent surfaces in hyperbolic 3-manifolds and about their vertices, edges and faces.

We will frequently consider
\widetilde{M}(g)\subset\mathbb{H}^{3}
and we denote its set of vertices, i.e. the full preimage of
V(g)
in
\widetilde{M}(g)
, by
\widetilde{V}(g)
. We will use all our notions interchangeably between
M(g)
and
\widetilde{M}(g)
. We note that in
\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
every edge extends from both sides either to a point from
\widetilde{V}(g)
, or to a point in
\partial_{\infty}\mathbb{H}^{3}
. In the latter case it necessarily belongs to
\Lambda(\overline{g})
as otherwise the distance to
\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
would grow to infinity along this edge, which contradicts to the compactness of
M
. This provides a classification of the edges of
g
: either a lift of an edge has two endpoints in
\widetilde{V}(g)
, or two endpoints in
\Lambda(\overline{g})
, or it is mixed. We will show below that the third case is impossible. In the second case note that by definition such an edge also belongs to
\partial C(\overline{g})
.

We recall that for any non-Fuchsian convex cocompact metric
\overline{g}
on
N
a homeomorphism between
C(\overline{g})
and
M
provides an example of a bent metric on
M
without vertices. We devote the next subsection to recall the basic facts about the boundary structure of
C(\overline{g})
.

3.1.1
Boundary geometry of convex cores

Let
\overline{g}
be a convex cocompact hyperbolic metric on
N
. Due to Lemma
2.32
, the induced intrinsic metric on
\partial C(\overline{g})
is hyperbolic. The union of edges of
\partial C(\overline{g})
constitutes what is called a
geodesic lamination
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
(S,d)
be a closed hyperbolic surface. A
geodesic lamination

\lambda
is a closed subset of
S
foliated by simple disjoint geodesics, which we will call
edges
of
\lambda
.

We refer to
[
12
,
25
,
24
,
70
]
for various aspects of the theory of geodesic laminations. We remark that we step away from the conventional terminology in favor of coherence as the edges of a geodesic lamination are commonly known in the literature as
leaves
.

A geodesic lamination is called
minimal
if it does not contain any proper geodesic sub-laminations. A simple closed geodesic provides an example of a minimal geodesic lamination. Any other minimal geodesic lamination is called
irrational
. Every minimal irrational lamination consists of uncountably many edges. A general geodesic lamination is called
irrational
if it contains a minimal irrational sub-lamination, and is called
rational
otherwise. For examples of irrational laminations we refer to
[
12
, Chapter 1.3]
or
[
15
]
.

Theorem 3.2

(
[
24
]
, Theorem I.4.2.8)
.

A geodesic lamination
\lambda
on a closed hyperbolic surface consists of finitely many minimal sub-laminations and finitely many open geodesics, isolated in
\lambda
, spiralling onto a minimal sub-lamination from both ends.

We will call connected components of
S\backslash\lambda

faces
of
\lambda
. A metric completion of a face is a hyperbolic surface of finite type with piecewise-geodesic boundary, whose components may be either closed or open geodesics. Every end of an open boundary-geodesic is asymptotic to another such end forming what we call a
spike
. An edge of
\lambda
is called
boundary
if it is a boundary component of a face. Note that even though an irrational lamination contains uncountably many leaves, only finitely many of them are boundary. A lamination is called
maximal
if the completion of each face is isometric to an ideal triangle.

Identify the universal cover
(\widetilde{S},d)
with
\mathbb{H}^{2}
and let
\widetilde{\lambda}
be the full preimage of a minimal irrational lamination
\lambda
. Then every geodesic
\widetilde{\psi}
of
\widetilde{\lambda}
is a limit of other geodesics of
\widetilde{\lambda}
. If
\widetilde{\psi}
is a lift of a boundary edge, then it is a limit from only one side.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
\lambda
be a geodesic lamination on
(S,d)
. A
transverse arc
to
\lambda
is a simple regular arc transverse to each edge of
\lambda
with endpoints in faces. A
transverse measure

\mu
on
\lambda
is a locally finite Borel measure
\mu_{\tau}
on each transverse arc
\tau
such that

(1) for a transverse sub-arc
\tau^{\prime}\subset\tau
the measure
\mu_{\tau^{\prime}}
is the restriction of
\mu_{\tau}
;

(2) the support of
\mu_{\tau}
is
\tau\cap\lambda
;

(3) the measure is invariant through isotopies of
S
preserving
\lambda
.

The pair
(\lambda,\mu)
is called a
measured (geodesic) lamination
.

Note that in particular
\mu_{\tau}(\tau)=0
if and only if
\tau\cap\lambda=\emptyset
. We will denote
\mu_{\tau}(\tau)
just by
\mu(\tau)
. We will sometimes abuse the notation and denote the measured lamination
(\lambda,\mu)
just by
\mu
. We can speak about the measure of each geodesic of
\lambda
meaning the measure of any point belonging to this geodesic on any transverse arc intersecting this geodesic. Not every geodesic lamination can support a transverse measure. A useful criterion is

Lemma 3.4

(
[
58
]
, Proposition 8.3.8)
.

Let
\lambda
be a geodesic lamination supporting a transverse measure and
\widetilde{\lambda}
be its full preimage in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
. Every point
q\in\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}
can be adjacent to at most two geodesics from
\widetilde{\lambda}
, and if it is adjacent to two, then both of them are lifts of boundary edges of
\lambda
belonging to a minimal irrational sub-lamination.

Theorem
3.2
and Lemma
3.4
imply (see a detailed proof also, e.g., in
[
70
, Corollary 1.7.3]
)

Lemma 3.5
.

The support of a measured lamination consists of finitely many minimal sub-laminations.

By identifying the universal cover
\widetilde{S}
with
\mathbb{H}^{2}
a measured lamination on
S
gives rise to a
\pi_{1}(S)
-invariant measure on the space of lines in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
. This allows to consider measured laminations from a metric independent point of view. By
\mathcal{ML}(S)
we denote the space of measured laminations on
S
equipped with the topology of *-weak convergence of measures. There is a natural PL-structure on the space
\mathcal{ML}(S)
induced by so-called
train tracks
on
S
. See the details in
[
12
,
70
]
. A well-known result is

Theorem 3.6

(
[
12
,
70
]
)
.

The space
\mathcal{ML}(S)
is PL-homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{-3\chi(S)}
.

Now we return to our setting when
\overline{g}
is a convex cocompact hyperbolic metric on
N
. The set of edges of
\partial C(\overline{g})
clearly forms a geodesic lamination, which we denote
\lambda_{\overline{g}}
. There is a transverse measure
\mu_{\overline{g}}
measuring how
\partial C(\overline{g})
is bent in
N(\overline{g})
. A careful construction is given in
[
32
]
. We sketch now another construction of
\mu_{\overline{g}}
.

Recall that oriented planes in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
correspond to point in the de Sitter space
d\mathbb{S}^{3}
. Let
\tau
be a transverse arc and
\widetilde{\tau}
be its lift to
\partial\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
. Consider the set of all pairs
(p,\Pi)
where
p\in\widetilde{\tau}
and
\Pi
is a supporting plane to
\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
at
p
, which we consider oriented outwards
\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
. Denote the projection of this set to the second coordinate by
\tau^{*}\subset d\mathbb{S}^{3}
. It is not hard to see that
\tau^{*}
is an arc in
d\mathbb{S}^{3}
, i.e., the image of a closed interval. One can show that
\tau^{*}
is rectifiable. Define
\mu_{\overline{g}}(\tau)
as the length of
\tau^{*}
.

The pair
(\lambda_{\overline{g}},\mu_{\overline{g}})
is called
the bending lamination
of
\overline{g}
. It is possible to extend the notion of geodesic laminations to cone-surfaces (a step towards that was taken, e.g., in
[
18
]
) and the notion of bending lamination to bent surfaces. This would give alternative proofs to the basic facts about bent surfaces in the next section. We, however, chose to take a simpler, more geometric route. We will need the following easy statements.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
\psi\subset\lambda_{\overline{g}}
be a geodesic belonging to an irrational component, and
\widetilde{\psi}\subset\partial\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
be its lift. Then there is a unique supporting plane to
\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
at
\widetilde{\psi}
.

Proof.

The geodesic
\psi
is open, hence, there is an accumulation point of
\psi
in
\partial C(\overline{g})
. Thereby, there exists a transverse arc intersecting
\psi
infinitely many times. Thus, the measure of
\psi
is zero. If there are two distinct support planes to
\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
at
\widetilde{\psi}
, the measure of
\psi
is at least the exterior dihedral angle between these planes, which is non-zero.
∎

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
\widetilde{\psi}_{1},\widetilde{\psi}_{2}\subset\partial\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
be two edges of the same component of
\partial\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
ending in the same point
q\in\Lambda(\overline{g})
. Then
\widetilde{\psi}_{1}
and
\widetilde{\psi}_{2}
belong to a minimal irrational sub-lamination of
\lambda_{\overline{g}}
, and there exists a face of
\partial\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
incident to both of them.

Proof.

Pick
p\in\widetilde{\psi}_{1}
. We claim that the intrinsic distance in
\partial\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
from
p
to
\widetilde{\psi}_{2}
tends to zero as we move
p
towards
q
. Indeed, there exists a finite sequence
\Pi_{1},\ldots,\Pi_{r}
of supporting planes to
\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
such that
\Pi_{1}
is tangent to
\widetilde{\psi}_{1}
,
\Pi_{r}
is tangent to
\widetilde{\psi}_{2}
and every two subsequent planes intersect. This follows from
[
32
, Lemma II.1.8.3]
. By the Busemann-Feller lemma, the intrinsic distance from
p
to
\widetilde{\psi}_{2}
in
\partial\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
is at least the intrinsic distance from
p
to
\widetilde{\psi}_{2}
in the union of pieces of these support planes forming a convex surface
C
. Take the horosphere at
q
passing through
p
and consider in
C
the curve obtained in the intersection. The length of this curve from
p
to the intersection with
\widetilde{\psi}_{2}
tends to zero as we move
p
towards
q
, which implies our claim.

Develop the universal cover of this component of
\partial\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
to
\mathbb{H}^{2}
. (We remark that as the boundary of
M
could be compressible, the boundary components of
\widetilde{M}
could be infinitely connected.) Due to our claim, there are lifts of
\widetilde{\psi}_{1}
and
\widetilde{\psi}_{2}
ending in the same point
q^{\prime}\in\partial_{\infty}\mathbb{H}^{2}
. Due to Lemma
3.4
, they belong to a minimal irrational sub-lamination of
\lambda_{\overline{g}}
and there are no other edges of our lifted lamination ending in
q^{\prime}
. Hence, in the universal cover there is a face between our lifts of
\widetilde{\psi}_{1}
and
\widetilde{\psi}_{2}
, which comes from a face of
\partial\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
adjacent to
\widetilde{\psi}_{1}
and
\widetilde{\psi}_{2}
.
∎

3.1.2
Boundary geometry of bent manifolds

Let
g
be a bent metric on
M
. We will be frequently considering the closed set
\partial M(g)\cap\partial C(\overline{g})
. Let
p\in\partial M(g)\cap\partial C(\overline{g})
. As
\partial C(\overline{g})
does not have vertices,
p
belongs to the relative interior of a segment
\psi\subset\partial C(\overline{g})
. It follows from convexity that
\psi\subset\partial M(g)
. Hence,
\partial M(g)\cap\partial C(\overline{g})
is the union of some edges and faces of
\partial C(\overline{g})
. Note however that such an edge or a face of
\partial C(\overline{g})
is not necessarily an edge or a face of
\partial M(g)
. The following lemma is rather trivial

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
g
be a bent metric on
M
with at least one vertex. Then
M(g)
is the closed convex hull of its vertices in
N(\overline{g})
and all vertices are at positive distance from
C(\overline{g})
. Conversely, let
\overline{g}
be a convex cocompact hyperbolic metric on
N
and
V\subset N(\overline{g})
be a finite set. Then
\overline{\rm conv}(V)
produces a bent metric on
M
except the case when
\overline{g}
is Fuchsian and
V
belongs to the complete totally geodesic surface containing
C(\overline{g})
.

Here
\overline{\rm conv}(V)
is
the closed convex hull
of
V
, i.e., the inclusion-minimal closed totally convex subset of
N(\overline{g})
containing
V
. We will also apply the notion on closed convex hull to subsets of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
.

Proof.

Consider
\widetilde{M}(g)\subset\mathbb{H}^{3}
. A lift of an edge of
g
is a geodesic segment in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
that ends either at
\widetilde{V}(g)
or at
\Lambda(\overline{g})
. A lift of a maximal geodesic segment of
M(g)
passing through a regular point ends either at
\widetilde{V}(g)
or at
\Lambda(\overline{g})
or at an edge-point. Thus,
\widetilde{M}(g)={\rm conv}(\widetilde{V}(g)\cup\Lambda(\overline{g}))
. But each point of
\Lambda(\overline{g})
is an accumulation point of the orbit of every vertex. We get
\widetilde{M}(g)=\overline{\rm conv}(\widetilde{V}(g))
and
M(g)=\overline{\rm conv}(V(g))
.
