If
p\in(\partial\widetilde{M}(g)\cap\partial\widetilde{C}(\overline{g}))
, then it lies in the relative interior of a geodesic segment
\psi\subset\partial\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
. Then
\psi\subset\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
and
p
is not a vertex of
g
. Thus, all vertices of
g
are at positive distance from
C(\overline{g})
.

For the last claim, it is easy to see that if
V\subset N(\overline{g})
is a finite point-set, then
\overline{\rm conv}(V)
is homeomorphic to
M
except the case when
\overline{g}
is Fuchsian and
V
belongs to the complete totally geodesic surface containing
C(\overline{g})
.
We need to show that the boundary is bent. Denote
\overline{\rm conv}(V)
by
M(g)
and consider
\widetilde{M}(g)\subset\mathbb{H}^{3}
. The Caratheodory theorem (which we can apply to
\mathbb{H}^{3}\cup\partial_{\infty}\mathbb{H}^{3}
due to the Klein model) says that each
p\in\widetilde{M}(g)
is in the convex hull of at most four points from
\widetilde{V}(g)\cup\Lambda(\overline{g})
. If a point belongs to the convex hull of four of these points, but no less, then it belongs to the interior of the convex hull. If follows that every point in
\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
belongs to the relative interior of a geodesic segment except possibly points of
\widetilde{V}
. This finishes the proof.
∎

We will also make use of the following easy observation

Lemma 3.10
.

For every
v\in V(g)
there exists a neighborhood of
v
in
\partial M
that does not contain edges of
g
except those that are incident to
v
.

Proof.

Let
B
be a small embedded geodesic ball around
v
in
N(\overline{g})
that does not contain other vertices. If there is a sequence of edges not incident to
v
that converge to
v
, then their intersections with
B
are disjoint geodesic segments with the endpoints in
\partial B
. Then they converge to a geodesic segment in
M(g)\cap B
containing
v
in its relative interior. Thus,
v
is not a vertex.
∎

We now describe the intrinsic geometry of bent metrics.

Lemma 3.11
.

If
g
is a bent metric on
M
, then the induced intrinsic metric
d
on
\partial M
is a convex hyperbolic cone-metric.

This relies on the following proposition, which strengthens the observation of Thurston that the induced intrinsic metric on the boundary of a convex core is intrinsically hyperbolic.

Proposition 3.12

(
[
85
]
)
.

Let
X
be a closed subset of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
. If
{\rm conv}(X)
has non-empty interior, then
\partial{\rm conv}(X)\backslash X
is locally isometric to
\mathbb{H}^{2}
.

Proof of Lemma
3.11
.

Pick a point
p\in\partial M\backslash V(g)
and lift it to
\widetilde{p}\in\partial\widetilde{M}(g)\subset\mathbb{H}^{3}
. Consider a small open ball
B
centered at
\widetilde{p}
that contains no vertices and does not intersect the components of
\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
except the one containing
\widetilde{p}
. Set
X=\partial B\cap\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
. Either
X
is a great circle and
\partial\widetilde{M}(g)\cap B
is a planar disk or
{\rm conv}(X)
has non-empty interior,
\partial\widetilde{M}(g)\cap B
is a connected component of
\partial{\rm conv}(X)\backslash X
and we can apply Proposition
3.12
.

We proved that outside the vertices
\partial M
is locally isometric to the hyperbolic plane. Now pick
v\in V(g)
and take a small simply connected neighborhood
U
of
v
in
\partial M
that does not intersect neither edges nor faces not incident to
v
, which is guaranteed by Lemma
3.10
. Then each point of
U\backslash v
lies on a segment emanating from
v
. Consider a lift
\widetilde{v}
to
\mathbb{H}^{3}
and push slightly a supporting geodesic plane to
\widetilde{M}(g)
at
\widetilde{v}
so its intersection
\alpha
with
\partial\widetilde{M}
is contained in the lift
\widetilde{U}\ni\widetilde{v}
of
U
. Then the neighborhood of
\widetilde{v}
bounded by
\alpha
is the geodesic cone over
\alpha
from
\widetilde{v}
and
\alpha
is a closed planar convex curve. Hence, the metric around
\widetilde{v}
is locally isometric to a convex hyperbolic cone.
∎

As discussed in Section
3.1.1
, the support
\lambda_{\overline{g}}
of the bending lamination of
\partial C(\overline{g})
consists of finitely many disjoint simple closed geodesics and of minimal irrational laminations. Let
\lambda
be a minimal irrational component of
\lambda_{\overline{g}}
and
\psi\subset\lambda
be a geodesic. If
\psi\subset\partial M(g)
, then since
\partial M(g)\cap\partial C(\overline{g})
is closed and the closure of
\psi
is
\lambda
, we see that
\lambda\subset\partial M(g)
. Another simple observation is

Lemma 3.13
.

Let
\lambda
be a minimal irrational sub-lamination of
\lambda_{\overline{g}}
such that
\lambda\subset\partial M(g)
,
\psi\subset\lambda
be a boundary edge and
F\subset\partial C(\overline{g})
be a face adjacent to
\psi
. Then
F\subset\partial M(g)
.

Proof.

Lift all to the universal cover, let
\widetilde{F}
and
\widetilde{\psi}
be the respective lifts. By Lemma
3.4
,
\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
has a unique supporting plane
\Pi
at
\widetilde{\psi}
, which contains
\widetilde{F}
. We have
\widetilde{\psi}\subset\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
, and since any supporting plane to
\widetilde{M}(g)
at
\widetilde{\psi}
is a supporting plane to
\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
,
\Pi
is a supporting plane to
\widetilde{M}(g)
and
(\Pi\cap\partial\widetilde{C}(\overline{g}))\subset(\Pi\cap\partial\widetilde{M}(g))
. It follows that
\widetilde{F}\subset\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
.
∎

We use it first to prove

Lemma 3.14
.

Let
g
be a bent metric on
M
. No edge of
\widetilde{M}(g)
has one end in
\widetilde{V}(g)
and another in
\Lambda(\overline{g})
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\widetilde{\chi}
is such an edge ending at
\widetilde{v}\in\widetilde{V}(g)
and
q\in\Lambda(\overline{g})
. Let
\chi
,
v
be the projections of
\widetilde{\chi}
,
\widetilde{v}
to
\partial M(g)
. Note that the distance to
\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
goes to zero along
\widetilde{\chi}
, but it never attains zero. Indeed, if it is zero at
p\in\widetilde{\chi}
and
p
is the closest point to
\widetilde{v}
with this property (in terms of the intrinsic distance of
\widetilde{\chi}
), then
p
is an edge-point of
\partial\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
and belongs to the relative interior of an edge
\widetilde{\psi}
of
\partial\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
with a different tangent at
p
than
\widetilde{\chi}
. But then
\widetilde{\psi}\subset\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
, so
p
is regular in
\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
and
\widetilde{\chi}
is not an edge.

Consider the closure
\lambda_{0}
of
\chi
in
\partial M(g)
. Because the distance to
C(\overline{g})
goes to zero along
\chi
, the set
\lambda=\lambda_{0}\backslash\chi
is at distance zero to
C(\overline{g})
, thus it is a subset of
\partial M(g)\cap\partial C(\overline{g})
. It is closed as the intersection of two closed subsets,
\lambda_{0}
and
\partial M(g)\cap\partial C(\overline{g})
. The points of
\lambda
belong to the boundary of the set
\partial M(g)\cap\partial C(\overline{g})
in
\partial M(g)
and hence
\lambda
consists of edge-points of
\partial C(\overline{g})
. If
p\in\lambda
, then one can see that the whole edge containing
p
belongs to
\lambda
. Hence
\lambda
is a sub-lamination of
\lambda_{\overline{g}}
.

Cut
\partial M
along
\lambda
, consider the connected component containing
\chi
and let
S
be its metric completion. Due to Lemma
3.10
, the cone-points of
\partial M(g)
are isolated from
\lambda
. Hence, the local picture at the boundary points of
S
is the same as the local picture for metric completions of faces of geodesic laminations on hyperbolic surfaces. Similarly to, e.g.,
[
12
, Chapter 1.4]
, one can see that
S
is a convex hyperbolic cone-surface of finite type with geodesic boundary, whose components may be either closed or open geodesics corresponding to edges of
\lambda
. Each boundary component has a convex neighborhood not containing cone-points, so isometric to a convex subset of
\mathbb{H}^{2}
. It is not hard to see that if
\chi
gets close enough to a closed component, then it either starts spiraling on it (i.e., in the universal cover of a convex neighborhood developed to
\mathbb{H}^{2}
a lift of
\chi
is asymptotically parallel to the respective lift of the boundary component), or
\chi
intersects itself. If
\chi
gets close enough to an open boundary component, then either it is asymptotic to this component (i.e., goes to a spike from one side of the component), or it intersects the boundary. The geodesic
\chi
is simple, disjoint from the boundary of
S
and does not have accumulation points in the interior of
\chi
, hence it either spirals onto a closed geodesic component or goes to a spike.

In the first case
\lambda
is a simple closed geodesic
\psi
. There is its lift
\widetilde{\psi}\subset\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
that is incident to
q
. Indeed, there is a lift
\widetilde{\psi}
of
\psi
such that the intrinsic distance in
\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
from a point of
\widetilde{\chi}
to
\widetilde{\psi}
tends to zero along
\widetilde{\chi}
towards
q
, hence also the extrinsic distance in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
tends to zero and such
\widetilde{\psi}
must end in
q
. Note that
\widetilde{\psi}
is an isolated edge of
\partial\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
. Then
\widetilde{\psi}
is the axis of a pure loxodromic isometry
\gamma\in\rho_{\overline{g}}(\pi_{1}(M))
. The
\gamma
-orbit of
\widetilde{v}
is contained in the plane spanned by
\widetilde{\chi}
and
\widetilde{\psi}
. But then
\widetilde{\chi}
belongs to the relative interior of the convex hull of this orbit, thus
\widetilde{\chi}
is not an edge of
\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
.

In the second case there are edges
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
of
\lambda
corresponding to the boundary components of
S
ending in the same spike of
S
as
\chi
. As the whole
\lambda
is contained in
\partial M(g)
,
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
are boundary edges of the lamination
\lambda
in
\partial\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
. There are their lifts
\widetilde{\psi}_{1}
,
\widetilde{\psi}_{2}
such that the intrinsic distances in
\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
from a point of
\widetilde{\chi}
to
\widetilde{\psi}_{1}
and
\widetilde{\psi}_{2}
tend to zero along
\widetilde{\chi}
towards
q
. Hence so do the extrinsic distances in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
and
\widetilde{\psi}_{1},\widetilde{\psi}_{2}
end in
q
. Lemma
3.7
says that they belong to the boundary of a face
\widetilde{F}
of
\partial\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
. By Lemma
3.13
,
\widetilde{F}
belongs to
\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
. The segment
\widetilde{\chi}
must belong to the support plane to
\widetilde{F}
and lie between
\widetilde{\psi}_{1}
and
\widetilde{\psi}_{2}
. Thus, it passes through interior points of
\widetilde{F}
and can not be an edge.
∎

Now we can show that any bent metric is polyhedral around vertices. This follows from

Corollary 3.15
.

Let
g
be a bent metric on
M
and
v\in V(g)
. Then there are finitely many edges incident to
v
.

Proof.

Consider the universal cover
\widetilde{M}(g)\subset\mathbb{H}^{3}
. Let
\widetilde{v}
be a lift of
v
. Suppose that there are infinitely many edges incident to
\widetilde{v}
. We claim that there is an edge
\widetilde{\chi}
from
\widetilde{v}
to
\Lambda(\overline{g})
. Indeed, this follows from the fact that
\rho_{\overline{g}}(\pi_{1}(M))
acts properly discontinuously and the fact that the set of all edges and vertices is closed in
\partial M
. Thus, we get a contradiction with Lemma
3.14
.
∎

We see from Lemma
3.14
that every edge of
\widetilde{M}(g)
either has both ends at
\widetilde{V}(g)
or both ends at
\Lambda(\overline{g})
, in which case it belongs to
\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})\cap\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
. In the latter case it is either a simple closed geodesic from
\partial C(\overline{g})
, or belongs to an irrational lamination of
\partial C(\overline{g})
. A bent metric is polyhedral if and only if there are no edges of the third type.

It remains to understand how an irrational bending lamination in the boundary of a bent metric can coexist with the presence of vertices.

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
F
be a face of a bent metric
g
on
M
such that it is adjacent to a vertex
v\in V(g)
and to an edge
\psi
belonging to a minimal irrational sub-lamination
\lambda
of
\lambda_{\overline{g}}
. Then there exists a simple closed geodesic
\chi\subset(\overline{F}\cap\partial C(g))
, where
\overline{F}
is the closure of
F
, separating
v
from
\psi
.

In particular, if
g
has vertices and the bending lamination
\mu_{\overline{g}}
has only irrational components, then
g
is strictly polyhedral.

Proof.

Let
\widetilde{F}\subset\widetilde{M}(g)
be a lift of
F
adjacent to a lift
\widetilde{v}
of
v
and to a lift
\widetilde{\psi}
of
\psi
. By
\widetilde{\lambda}
we denote the full preimage of
\lambda
in
\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
and by
\Pi
we denote the supporting plane to
\widetilde{M}(g)
containing
\widetilde{F}
. Note that since
\widetilde{\psi}\subset\Pi
, the plane
\Pi
is also supporting for
\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
. The geodesic
\widetilde{\psi}
divides
\partial\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
in two parts and the nearest-point projection to
\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
maps
\widetilde{F}
to one of these parts (it is easy to see that
\widetilde{F}
cannot be mapped on
\widetilde{\psi}
). We claim that there is a face
\widetilde{F}^{\prime}
of
\partial\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
adjacent to
\widetilde{\psi}
from this side. Indeed, the only other option would be that there is a sequence of geodesics from
\widetilde{\lambda}
approaching
\widetilde{\psi}
from this side. As
\lambda
is minimal, we have
\widetilde{\lambda}\subset\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
, so all but finitely many of these geodesics belong to
\widetilde{F}\subset\Pi
. As
\Pi
is a supporting plane to
\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
, all these geodesics that are sufficiently close to
\widetilde{\psi}
can not be edges of
\partial\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
, which is a contradiction.

Since
\lambda
is irrational, Lemma
3.13
implies that
\widetilde{F}^{\prime}
belongs to
\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
. Moreover, it belongs to the same half-plane of
\Pi
with respect to
\widetilde{\psi}
as
\widetilde{v}
. As the distance from
\widetilde{v}
to
\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
is positive,
\widetilde{v}
is disjoint from the closure of
\widetilde{F}^{\prime}
. Thus, there must be a geodesic line
\widetilde{\chi}
that is a boundary component of
\widetilde{F}^{\prime}
and that separates
\widetilde{v}
from
\widetilde{\psi}
. It remains to show that the projection
\chi
of
\widetilde{\chi}
is not from a minimal irrational sub-lamination
\lambda^{\prime}
of
\lambda_{\overline{g}}
. Indeed, otherwise
\lambda^{\prime}\subset\partial M(g)
and there is a sequence of geodesics of its full preimage
\widetilde{\lambda}^{\prime}
approaching
\widetilde{\chi}
from the other side than
\widetilde{F}^{\prime}
. By the previous reasoning, all but finitely many of these geodesics belong to
\Pi
, which is a contradiction. Hence,
\chi
is a simple closed geodesic that separates
v
and
\psi
in
\overline{F}
.
∎

Corollary 3.17
.

The connected components of
\partial M(g)\cap\partial C(g)
are simple closed geodesics, which are edges of
\partial C(g)
, and compact hyperbolic surfaces with geodesic boundary.

Recall now Definition
1.11
of strictly and controllably polyhedral metrics on
M
. We can show

Corollary 3.18
.

Let
g
be a bent metric on
M
such that the induced cone-metric
d
on
\partial M
is balanced. Then
g
is controllably polyhedral.

Proof.

Suppose the converse, then
\partial M(g)\cap\partial C(\overline{g})
contains a compact hyperbolic surface with geodesic boundary. Pick a pair of pants
P
in this surface. Since
\cosp(d)<\delta(\sys(d))
, Corollary
2.8
implies that
V(d)\cap P
must be non-empty, which is a contradiction.
∎

Together with Theorem
B
, Corollary
3.18
implies Proposition
1.16
. Now we deal with strictly polyhedral metrics. As we can see, this property has strong implications on the boundary structure of
\partial M(g)
.

Corollary 3.19
.

Let
g
be a strictly polyhedral metric on
M
. Then each edge is a segment between vertices and the closures of all faces are isometric to compact hyperbolic polygons. Conversely, if the closures of all faces of a bent metric are compact hyperbolic polygons, then the metric is strictly polyhedral.

Proof.

Let
g
be a strictly polyhedral hyperbolic metric on
M
. The claim on the edges follows from our description of the edges of a bent metric. The closure of each face is isometric to a hyperbolic surface with convex boundary. As every edge ends at vertices, the boundary of a face is compact. If a face has more then one boundary component, then it contains a homotopically non-trivial geodesic loop. Lifting this loop to the universal cover we see that it goes to
\Lambda(\overline{g})
, hence it belongs to
\partial C(\overline{g})
, which is a contradiction.

In the other direction, suppose the converse. If
\partial M(g)\cap\partial C(\overline{g})
is non-empty, we saw that there is a face of
\partial M(g)
containing a simple closed geodesic either in its interior or in its boundary. The closure of this face can not be isometric to a compact hyperbolic polygon, which finishes the proof.
∎

We end this section by sketching a construction of non-polyhedral bent metrics with vertices.

Example 3.20
.

We assume that
N
admits a convex cocompact hyperbolic metric
\overline{g}
such that the bending lamination
\lambda_{\overline{g}}
contains an irrational component, and that there is a face
F
of
\partial C(\overline{g})
with boundary consisting of simple closed geodesics. (The works
[
17
,
52
]
of Bonahon–Otal and Lecuire imply that such
\overline{g}
exists on most manifolds under our consideration. In particular, it exists if
M
has an incompressible boundary component of genus at least three.)

Let
\psi\subset\partial\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
be a boundary edge of a lift of
F
. By
\Pi_{\psi}
we denote the supporting plane to
\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
at
\psi
that makes equal angles with the both extremal supporting planes to
\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
at
\psi
. We call a supporting plane
\Pi
to
\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})

bad
if it contains a boundary edge
\psi
of a lift
\widetilde{F}
of
F
and if the dihedral angle between
\Pi
and
\widetilde{F}
is smaller than the dihedral angle between
\Pi_{\psi}
and
\widetilde{F}
. Otherwise, we call a supporting plane
good
. By
\widetilde{A}
we denote the intersection in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
of all closed supporting half-spaces to
\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
bounded by good supporting planes. We note that the set
\widetilde{A}
is closed and
\rho_{\overline{g}}
-invariant, hence it projects to a closed set
A\subset N(\overline{g})
. We also remark that

•

every face of
\partial\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
that is not a lift of
F
belongs to
\partial\widetilde{A}
;

•

\widetilde{A}\backslash\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
is non-empty.

Pick a point
\widetilde{v}
in
\widetilde{A}\backslash\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
, consider its
\rho_{\overline{g}}
-orbit
\widetilde{V}
and take the closed convex hull of
\widetilde{V}
in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
. This determines a bent metric
g
on
M
with vertices. We have
\widetilde{M}(g)\subset\widetilde{A}
, hence
M(g)\subset A
and every face of
C(\overline{g})
except
F
belongs to
\partial M(g)
. In particular,
\partial M(g)
contains the irrational component of
\lambda_{\overline{g}}
, thus
g
is not polyhedral.

3.1.3
Every convex realization of a convex cone-metric is bent

In this subsection we prove Theorem
B
. We need some small preparations. In Section
1.3
we mentioned that Alexandrov gave a complete intrinsic characterization of the induced intrinsic metrics on the boundaries of general convex sets in
\mathbb{E}^{3}
. He similarly did this in other model spaces. For convex sets in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
such metrics are called
CBB(
-1
) metrics
. Again, we are not going to give a definition, but refer to
[
4
,
22
]
. Particularly, every convex hyperbolic cone-metric is CBB(
-1
). If
S
is a surface and
d
is a CBB(
-1
) metric, one can introduce two natural Borel measures:
the area

{\rm area}(.)
and
the intrinsic curvature

{\rm curv}_{I}(.)
. The former is the standard Hausdorff measure, while the latter is a non-smooth analogue of the Gaussian curvature measure. We define
the extrinsic curvature measure

{\rm curv}(.):={\rm curv}_{I}(.)+{\rm area}(.)
. If
d
is a convex hyperbolic cone-metric and
U\subset S
is a Borel set, then
{\rm curv}(U)
is equal to the sum of the curvatures of all cone-point belonging to
U
. The curvature of a cone-point
v
is equal to
2\pi
minus the total angle of
v
.

We use the duality between
\mathbb{H}^{3}
and
d\mathbb{S}^{3}
. If
C\subset\mathbb{H}^{3}
is a closed convex set, one can define its
dual

C^{*}\subset d\mathbb{S}^{3}
as the set of all planes in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
that do not separate
C
and are oriented outwards. If
C
has a non-empty interior and
U\subset\partial C
is a Borel set, its dual
U^{*}\subset\partial C^{*}
is defined as the set of all support planes to
G
at points of
U
. An analogue of the Gauss theorem in this setting states that
{\rm curv}(U)
is equal to the area of
U^{*}
. See
[
74
, Lemma 2.2.5]
for a proof. It is easy to deduce from this

Lemma 3.21

(
[
72
]
, Lemma 3.2.2)
.

Let
C\subset\mathbb{H}^{3}
be a compact convex set with non-empty interior and
\Pi
be a supporting plane such that the set
R:=\partial C\cap\Pi
has non-empty relative interior in
\partial C
. Define
R^{c}:=\partial C\backslash R
. Then
{\rm curv}(R^{c})>0
.

Now we can prove Theorem
B
.

Proof of Theorem
B
.

Consider
\widetilde{M}(g)
embedded in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
in the Klein model. By
\widetilde{V}
we denote the full preimage of
V(d)
. By
B
we denote
\widetilde{M}(g)\cup\Lambda(\overline{g})
, which is a compact convex set in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with non-empty interior. We recall few standard notions from convex geometry. A point
p\in\partial B
is called
extreme
if it does not belong to the relative interior of a segment with endpoints in
\partial B
. A point
p\in\partial B
is called
exposed
if there exists a plane
\Pi\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
such that
\Pi\cap B=\{p\}
. Obviously, exposed points are extreme, but not necessarily vice versa. All points from
\Lambda(\overline{g})
are exposed. It follows directly from the definition that
B
is the convex hull of all its extreme points.

The Straszewicz theorem
[
87
]
(see also
[
82
, Theorem 1.4.7]
) says that the set of extreme points is contained in the closure of the set of exposed points. We are going to show that if
p\in\partial B
is exposed, then
p\in\widetilde{V}\cup\Lambda(\overline{g})
. Since the latter set is closed, this also is true for every extreme point. In turn, this implies that every
p\in\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
such that
p\notin\widetilde{V}
belongs to the relative interior of a segment. Due to the equivariance of
\widetilde{M}(g)
, this proves that
g
is bent.

Suppose the converse that there is
p\in\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
,
p\notin\widetilde{V}
such that
p
is exposed. Then
p
has a neighborhood
U
in
\partial B
distinct from
\widetilde{V}\cup\Lambda(\overline{g})
. Let
\Pi
be a plane such that
\Pi\cap B=\{p\}
. Push
\Pi
slightly towards
B
such that the curve
\Pi\cap\partial B
stays in
U
, by
U^{\prime}
we denote the open set bounded by this curve. By the choice of
U
we have
{\rm curv}(U)=0
, thus also
{\rm curv}(U^{\prime})=0
. On the other hand, Lemma
3.21
implies
{\rm curv}(U^{\prime})>0
, which is a contradiction.
∎

3.2
Triangulations

For our further investigations we need a geodesic triangulation of
M(g)
, where
g
is polyhedral, with vertices at some subset of
\partial M(g)
containing
V(g)
(the examples of non-strictly polyhedral metrics show that just
V(g)
might be not enough). Unfortunately, we can not prove the existence of such a triangulation in full generality, but we can show two weaker things, which are enough for our purposes: (1) there exists such a decomposition of
M(g)
into convex hyperbolic polyhedra; (2) in a finite cover of
M(g)
this decomposition can be triangulated. This situation is very similar to the treatment of hyperbolic cusp-3-manifolds, where the existence of an ideal geodesic triangulation would also be desirable for many applications. In that case the analogue of (1) is the well-known Epstein–Penner decomposition
[
33
]
, and the analogue of (2) is shown in the work
[
54
]
of Luo–Schleimer–Tillmann. A similar treatment was considered by Schlenker in
[
80
]
for polyhedral hyperbolic 3-manifolds with ideal vertices. We will follow the mentioned works and will show that their approaches are also applicable in our situation. Note that the construction of the Epstein–Penner decomposition is itself very similar to a well-known convex-hull construction of the Delaunay decomposition of a point set in the Euclidean space via lifting the set to a surface in one dimension higher. In
[
30
]
DeBlois considers generalizations of this construction to discrete subsets of
\mathbb{H}^{n}
invariant with respect to torsion-free lattices of finite covolume.

In the above-mentioned situations the first step is to prove that the visible part of the convex hull in the Minkowski space has only space-like supporting planes. From this and from the discretness of the vertex set it follows that the convex hull provides a locally finite polyhedral decomposition. In our setting we can have also light-like and time-like supporting planes, so these conclusions become slightly more involved to make. Before we start, let us prove an auxiliary result.

Lemma 3.22
.

Let
g
be a polyhedral metric on
M
,
s\in\Lambda(\overline{g})
and
\Pi
be a plane passing through
s
, but not intersecting
\widetilde{M}(g)
. Then there exists a supporting plane
\Pi_{0}
to
\widetilde{M}(g)
passing through
s
and asymptotically parallel to
\Pi
. (By saying that a plane is supporting to
\widetilde{M}(g)
we particularly mean that it contains points of
\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
.)

Proof.

Consider the dual convex set
\widetilde{M}(g)^{*}\subset d\mathbb{S}^{3}
. Its boundary points can be divided in two types: (1) dual to supporting planes to
\widetilde{M}(g)
and (2) dual to planes
\Pi
such that
\Pi\cap\widetilde{M}(g)=\emptyset
, but
\partial_{\infty}\Pi\cap\Lambda(\overline{g})\neq\emptyset
.
We use the notions of exposed and extreme points from the previous section. We make a convention that
\partial\widetilde{M}(g)^{*}
means
\partial(\widetilde{M}(g)^{*})
. (Note that it is not the same as
(\partial\widetilde{M}(g))^{*}
. The latter is exactly the set of points of type (1).)

Let us show that points of type (2) cannot be exposed for
\widetilde{M}(g)^{*}
. Suppose that
\Pi^{*}\in\partial\widetilde{M}(g)^{*}
is such a point, dual to a plane
\Pi\subset\mathbb{H}^{3}
. Note that
\partial_{\infty}\Pi\cap\Lambda(\overline{g})
consists of a single point
s
. Indeed, otherwise
\Pi
contains a line between two points of
\Lambda(\overline{g})
, hence
\Pi\cap\widetilde{M}(g)\neq\emptyset
and
\Pi^{*}
is not of type (2). Assume that there is a plane
p^{*}\subset d\mathbb{S}^{3}
such that
p^{*}\cap\widetilde{M}(g)^{*}=\Pi^{*}
. Note that
p^{*}
cannot be time-like, thus the dual point
p
is in
\mathbb{H}^{3}\cup\partial_{\infty}\mathbb{H}^{3}
. If there is another plane in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
passing through
p
and not intersecting
\widetilde{M}(g)
, then
p^{*}
contains another point of
\widetilde{M}(g)^{*}
, which contradicts to our choice. If
p\neq s
, then because
\widetilde{M}(g)\cup\Lambda(\overline{g})
is closed in
\mathbb{H}^{3}\cup\partial_{\infty}\mathbb{H}^{3}
and
(\Pi\cup\partial_{\infty}\Pi)\cap(\widetilde{M}(g)\cup\Lambda(\overline{g}))=s
, we can rotate
\Pi
slightly around an axis in
\Pi
through
p
, but not through
s
, so that the obtained plane does not intersect
\widetilde{M}(g)\cup\Lambda(\overline{g})
. If
p=s
, then we can act on
\Pi
by a parabolic isometry preserving
s
and sending
\Pi
to an asymptotically parallel plane in the direction opposite from
\widetilde{M}(g)
. The resulting plane again does not intersect
\widetilde{M}(g)
. Hence,
\Pi^{*}
cannot be exposed.

One can see that when
g
is polyhedral, a point
\Pi^{*}\in\partial\widetilde{M}(g)^{*}
of type (1) is exposed if and only if the dual plane
\Pi
contains a regular point of
\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
. The set of such points
\Pi^{*}
is discrete. The Straszewicz theorem applied to
\widetilde{M}(g)^{*}\cup\Lambda(\overline{g})
says that the extreme points of
\widetilde{M}(g)^{*}\cup\Lambda(\overline{g})
are in the closure of the set of exposed points. Then all exposed points of
\widetilde{M}(g)^{*}
are extreme. In particular, no point of type (2) is extreme.

Now consider the situation in the statement of the lemma. The point
\Pi^{*}
is of the type (2). The segment from
s
to
\Pi^{*}
belongs to
\partial\widetilde{M}(g)^{*}
. Let us extend it behind
\Pi^{*}
. It can not be extended infinitely, thus it ends at a point
\Pi_{0}^{*}\in\partial\widetilde{M}(g)^{*}
. It remains to show that
\Pi_{0}^{*}
is of type (1). Indeed, otherwise it is not extreme and belongs to the relative interior of a segment
I
in
\partial\widetilde{M}(g)^{*}
such that each endpoint of
I
either is in
\Lambda(\overline{g})
, or is an extreme point of
\partial\widetilde{M}(g)^{*}
. If one of the endpoints is
s_{0}\in\Lambda(\overline{g})
, then by construction
s_{0}\neq s
, hence
\partial_{\infty}\Pi_{0}\cap\Lambda(\overline{g})
contains
s
and
s_{0}
, so
\Pi^{*}_{0}
is of type (1). If both endpoints are extreme, then they are of type (1). We will show that so is
\Pi^{*}_{0}
.

Denote the supporting planes to
\widetilde{M}(g)
, dual to the endpoints of
I
, by
\Pi_{1}
and
\Pi_{2}
. They must be distinct. Note that because they pass through
s
and are supporting to
\widetilde{M}(g)
, from Lemma
3.14
they contain points of
\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
, hence are supporting to it. Suppose that one of them, say
\Pi_{1}
, supports a face
\widetilde{F}
of
\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
such that there is no edge of this face ending in
s
. Then
s
is a limit point for points of
\Lambda(\overline{g})\cap\partial_{\infty}\Pi_{1}
from both sides. Thus, every geodesic ray emanating from
s
in
\Pi_{1}
contains points of
\widetilde{F}
. Then
\Pi_{1}
is the only supporting plane to
\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
through
s
, which is a contradiction with that
\Pi_{2}
is distinct from
\Pi_{1}
. Hence, there is an edge of
\partial\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
in
\Pi_{1}
adjacent to
s
. Similarly, there is an edge adjacent to
s
in
\Pi_{2}
. If these edges are distinct, then by Lemma
3.8
they belong to an irrational component of
\lambda_{\overline{g}}
, which is contained in
\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
. This contradicts to
g
being polyhedral. Thus, it is the same edge, i.e.,
\Pi_{1}
and
\Pi_{2}
intersects and the intersection line between
\Pi_{1}
and
\Pi_{2}
is an edge of
\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
. Hence,
I
consists of points of type (1), which finishes the proof.

∎

Lemma 3.23
.

Let
g
be a polyhedral hyperbolic metric on
M
.
Let
W\subset\partial M(g)
be a finite set of points containing
V(g)
and containing at least one point in each edge that is a closed geodesic. Then there exists a decomposition of
M(g)
into finitely many convex hyperbolic polyhedra with vertices in
W
.

Here by a decomposition we mean that every point in
M(g)
belongs to at least one polyhedron, the interiors of the polyhedra are disjoint, and if the intersection of two polyhedra is non-empty, then this intersection is a face of both polyhedra. In this section by a face of a polyhedron we sometimes mean a face of any dimension.

Proof.

Consider the universal cover
\widetilde{M}(g)\subset\mathbb{H}^{3}
in the hyperboloid model, let
\widetilde{W}
be the full preimage of
W
. It is easy to see that after we added to
W
a point on each edge that is a closed geodesic,
\widetilde{M}(g)
is the convex hull of
\widetilde{W}
in the usual sense, without taking the closure.
So each point of
\widetilde{M}(g)
is a convex combination of finitely many points of
\widetilde{W}
.

Let
C^{\prime}
be the convex hull of
\widetilde{W}
in
\mathbb{R}^{3,1}
and
C
be the set of points
p
of
\partial C^{\prime}
visible from the origin
o
, i.e., such that the segment
op
in
\mathbb{R}^{3,1}
does not intersect
C^{\prime}
except
p
. Take
p_{0}\in\widetilde{M}(g)
. As it is a convex combination of finitely many points of
\widetilde{W}
, the ray
op_{0}
intersects
C^{\prime}
. By definition, the first intersection point
p
belongs to
C
. One can see that
C
is homeomorphic to
\widetilde{M}(g)
via the central projection from the origin, and is closed in
\mathbb{R}^{3,1}
.

Consider a supporting plane
\Pi
to
C
that does not pass though the origin, let
H:=\Pi\cap\mathbb{H}^{3}
. Depending if
\Pi
is space-like, light-like or time-like,
H
is a sphere, a horosphere or a hypersphere respectively. We prove that in the latter two cases

Claim 3.23.1
.

\partial_{\infty}H\cap\Lambda(\overline{g})=\emptyset.

Note that as
\Pi\cap C\neq\emptyset
, we have
\Pi\cap\widetilde{W}\neq\emptyset
. The set
H
bounds an open convex set
H_{+}
, which does not contain points of
\widetilde{W}
since
\Pi
is a supporting plane for
C
.

In order to prove Claim
3.23.1
we need to introduce a special notion. A point
s\in\partial_{\infty}\mathbb{H}^{3}
is called a
conical limit point
for a Kleinian group
\Gamma
if for every geodesic line
\psi
through
s
and every
p\in\mathbb{H}^{3}
there exists a sequence
\gamma_{i}\in\Gamma
such that
\gamma_{i}(p)
converges to
s
and stays at a bounded distance from
\psi
. A theorem of Beardon–Maskit
[
7
]
says that if
\Gamma
is convex cocompact, then every limit point of
\Gamma
is conical. See also a discussion in
[
67
, Chapter 2]
, particularly Theorem 2.7.2 there.

Suppose that
H
is a horoball and that there is a point
s\in\partial_{\infty}H\cap\Lambda(\overline{g})
. We have a point
s\in\Lambda(\overline{g})
with an open horoball
H_{+}
centered at it, which does not contain points of
\widetilde{W}
. In particular it does not contain points from the
\rho_{\overline{g}}
-orbit of a point in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
. This contradicts to the claim that
s
is a conical limit point for
\rho_{\overline{g}}(\pi_{1}(M))
.

Suppose that
H
is a hypersphere and that there is a point
s\in\partial_{\infty}H\cap\Lambda(\overline{g})
. The set
H
is equidistant from a plane
H_{0}\subset H_{+}
with
\partial_{\infty}H=\partial_{\infty}H_{0}
. As
H_{+}
does not contain points of
\widetilde{W}
,
H_{0}
is disjoint from
\widetilde{M}(g)
. We remark that
\partial_{\infty}H\cap\Lambda(\overline{g})=s
. Indeed, if there were more than one point, then
H_{0}
would contain a line from
\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})\subset\widetilde{M}(g)
, which is a contradiction.

By Lemma
3.22
, there exists a a supporting plane
\Pi_{0}
to
\widetilde{M}(g)
passing through
s
and asymptotically parallel to
\Pi
. Because of the conditions on
\Pi_{0}
, the set
\Pi_{0}\cap\widetilde{M}(g)
is either an edge, or a face of
\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
.
In the first case denote the edge by
\widetilde{\psi}
. Since
g
is polyhedral,
\widetilde{\psi}
is the axis of a purely hyperbolic isometry
\gamma\in\rho_{\overline{g}}(\pi_{1}(M))
. Either
\gamma
or
\gamma^{-1}
sends
H
to
H_{+}
. This contradicts with
H_{+}\cap\widetilde{W}=\emptyset
.

Otherwise denote the face
\Pi_{0}\cap\widetilde{M}(g)
by
\widetilde{F}
and remark that
H\cap\Pi_{0}
is a horocycle based at
s
. Indeed, there exists a unique one-parametric parabolic subgroup of
{\rm Iso}^{+}(\mathbb{H}^{3})
fixing
s
and fixing
\Pi
and
\Pi_{0}
as sets. Thus, it also fixes as sets
H
and
H\cap\Pi_{0}
. Hence, the orbit of every point from
H\cap\Pi_{0}
is the whole
H\cap\Pi_{0}
. On the other hand, this orbit must be a horocycle.

Now consider a subgroup
\Gamma
of
\rho_{\overline{g}}(\pi_{1}(M))
consisting of all elements fixing
\Pi_{0}
. This is a convex cocompact Kleinian group, and
s
must be a conical limit point for
\Gamma
. Thus, the
\Gamma
-orbit of any point of
\widetilde{W}\cap\widetilde{F}
must enter
H_{+}\cap\widetilde{\Pi}_{0}
. This again contradicts with
H_{+}\cap\widetilde{W}=\emptyset
and finishes the proof of Claim
3.23.1
.

Now we claim that
\Pi\cap\widetilde{W}
is finite. If
\Pi
is space-like, then
H:=\Pi\cap\mathbb{H}^{3}
is compact, so the claim follows from the discreteness of
\widetilde{W}
in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
. If
\Pi
is time-like or light-like and
\Pi\cap\widetilde{W}
is infinite, then the discreteness of
\widetilde{W}
implies that there is
s\in\partial_{\infty}H\cap\Lambda(\overline{g})
, which is a contradiction with Claim
3.23.1
.

Let
p
be in the relative interior of
C
and
\Pi
be a support plane at
p
. The point
p
is a convex combination of at least two points of
\widetilde{W}
, which are contained in
\Pi
. From the previous argument,
\Pi\cap\widetilde{W}
is finite, so
\Pi\cap C
is the convex hull of finitely many points of
\widetilde{W}
. We claim that there exists a supporting plane through
p
containing at least four points of
\widetilde{W}
. Indeed, if it is less than four and it is not possible to rotate
\Pi
to increase
\Pi\cap\widetilde{W}
, then there exists a sequence
\{\widetilde{w}_{i}\}\subset\widetilde{W}
such that the distance from
\widetilde{w}_{i}
to
\Pi
tends to zero. Due to the discreteness of
\widetilde{W}
, up to taking a subsequence we assume that
\{\widetilde{w}_{i}\}
converges to
s\in\Lambda(\overline{g})
. It follows that
\Pi
is not space-like and
s\in\partial_{\infty}\Pi
, which is a contradiction.

We see that
C
decomposes into polyhedral 3-dimensional faces. We want to show that this decomposition is locally finite. It is enough to show that for each point
\widetilde{w}\in\widetilde{W}
there are only finitely many segments from
\widetilde{w}
to other points of
\widetilde{W}
belonging to
C
. Indeed, otherwise from the discreteness of
\widetilde{W}
and the closeness of
C
we obtain that there exists a light-like segment from
\widetilde{w}
belonging to
C
pointing to
\Lambda(\overline{g})
. A supporting plane
\Pi
containing this segment is light-like and for
H=\Pi\cap\mathbb{H}^{3}
we have
\partial_{\infty}H\cap\Lambda(\overline{g})\neq\emptyset
, which is a contradiction with Claim
3.23.1
.

By the central projection from the origin, this decomposition projects to a locally finite decomposition of
\widetilde{M}(g)
into convex hyperbolic polyhedra with vertices in
\widetilde{W}
. As
C
is invariant with respect to
\rho_{\overline{g}}
, so is the decomposition, and it produces a decomposition of
M(g)
into convex polyhedra with vertices in
W
as desired.

∎

Lemma 3.24
.

Let
g
be a polyhedral hyperbolic metric on
M
and let
M(g)
be decomposed into finitely many convex hyperbolic polyhedra with vertices in a set
W
such that
V(g)\subseteq W\subset\partial M(g)
. Then there exists a finite cover
M^{\prime}(g^{\prime})
of
M(g)
, where
g^{\prime}
is the lift of the metric
g
, such that the lift of the decomposition to
M^{\prime}(g^{\prime})
can be subdivided to a triangulation without adding new vertices, and the obtained triangulation is super-large.

Proof.

Claim 3.24.1
.

There exists a finite cover
M^{\prime}(g^{\prime})
of
M(g)
such that the polyhedral decomposition lifted to
M^{\prime}(g^{\prime})
is super-large.

Here the notion of super-largeness from Definition
2.30
is applied to polyhedral decompositions in a straightforward way.

Indeed, let
P_{1},\ldots,P_{r}
be an enumeration of all polyhedra of the decomposition of
M
. Choose a lift
\widetilde{P}_{i}
for each
P_{i}
. Consider the set
\Gamma
of all
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(M)
such that for some vertex
\widetilde{w}
of some
\widetilde{P}_{i}
, the vertex
\gamma\widetilde{w}
is a vertex of the same
\widetilde{P}_{i}
. As the stabilizer of each
\widetilde{w}
is trivial, the set
\Gamma
is finite. As
\pi_{1}(M)
is linear, it is residually finite
[
55
, Theorem III.7.11]
, and there exists a normal subgroup
S\triangleleft\pi_{1}(M)
of finite index not containing any element of
\Gamma
.

Consider the cover
M^{\prime}
of
M
corresponding to
S
. Suppose that it does not satisfy our claim. Then there exists
\widetilde{P}^{\prime}
of
\widetilde{M}(g)
, a vertex
\widetilde{w}^{\prime}
of
\widetilde{P}^{\prime}
and an element
\gamma^{\prime}\in\pi_{1}(M^{\prime})
such that
\gamma^{\prime}\widetilde{w}^{\prime}
is a vertex of
\widetilde{P}^{\prime}
. Note that
\widetilde{P}^{\prime}=\gamma\widetilde{P}_{i}
for some
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(M)
. Then
\widetilde{w}:=\gamma^{-1}\widetilde{w}^{\prime}
is a vertex of
\widetilde{P}_{i}
. Hence,
\gamma^{-1}\gamma^{\prime}\gamma\widetilde{w}
is again a vertex of
\widetilde{P}_{i}
. Thus,
\gamma^{-1}\gamma^{\prime}\gamma\in\Gamma
. On the other hand, as
S
is normal,
\gamma^{-1}\gamma^{\prime}\gamma\in S
, which contradicts our construction of
S
. This finishes the proof of the claim.

Now we consider the cover
M^{\prime}
of
M
as from Claim
3.24.1
. The lift of the polyhedral decomposition to
M^{\prime}
is super-large and it is well-known that any such decomposition is triangulable. We give a quick proof of this fact.

Let us construct a correspondence
f:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow W
from the set of polyhedra
\mathcal{C}
to the set of vertices with the following properties.

(1) For every
P
in
\mathcal{C}
,
f(P)
is a vertex of
P
.

(2) If
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
are adjacent by a 2-face and both
f(P_{1})
,
f(P_{2})
belongs to this 2-face, then
f(P_{1})=f(P_{2})
.

To construct this correspondence, start from
P_{1}
and let
f(P_{1})
be any its vertex
w
. Next, for each
P_{i}
adjacent to
w
set
f(P_{i})=w
. Now at each next step take
P_{j}
for which
f(P_{j})
is still not defined. Take any its vertex
w
that is not in the image of
f
and set
f(P_{i})=w
for each polyhedron
P_{i}
adjacent to
w
. Note that for some of them
f(P_{i})
could have been already defined, but we redefine it now. After finitely many steps we obtain the map
f
defined over all
\mathcal{C}
satisfying the required properties.

Now for each
P\in\mathcal{C}
consider all 2-faces of
P
adjacent to
f(P)
and triangulate them by coning from the vertex
f(P)
. Clearly, there are no contradictions due to the condition (2). Next, triangulate all remaining 2-faces arbitrarily. For each
P
the obtained triangulation of
\partial P
can be easily extended to a triangulation of
P
by coning it from
f(P)
. This provides the desired triangulation
\mathcal{T}
.
∎

3.3
Topology of the space of bent metrics

Let
V
be a finite set.
By
\mathcal{CH}(N,V)
denote the space of equivalence classes of pairs
(\overline{g},f)
, where
\overline{g}
is a convex cocompact hyperbolic metric on
N
,
{f:V\hookrightarrow N}
is an injective map, called
marking
, and two pairs are equivalent if they differ by an isometry preserving the marking and isotopic to identity. Here we do not assume that the isotopy should fix the marked points.
As in Section
2.4
, we similarly define the space
\mathcal{CH}_{h}(N,V)
with isotopy replaced by homotopy. We are going to endow
\mathcal{CH}(N,V)
with a natural smooth structure such that the forgetful map
\mathcal{CH}(N,V)\rightarrow\mathcal{CH}(N)
is a smooth bundle. This is very similar to the treatment of the space of metrics with marked oriented planes performed by us in
[
74
, Section 2.5]
.

Again, it is easier to define a smooth topology on
\mathcal{CH}_{h}(N,V)
. By
(\mathbb{H}^{3})^{V}
we denote the space of maps
\widetilde{f}:V\rightarrow\mathbb{H}^{3}
. Consider the direct product

\mathcal{R}(\pi_{1}(M),G)\times(\mathbb{H}^{3})^{V}.

Let
G
act on it from the left, by conjugation on the first factor and by isometries of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
on the second factor. Additionally,
\pi_{1}(M)
acts from the left on each fiber by
\gamma(\rho,\widetilde{f})=(\rho,\rho(\gamma)\widetilde{f})
, where
(\rho,\widetilde{f})\in\mathcal{R}(\pi_{1}(M),G)\times(\mathbb{H}^{3})^{V}
. One can check that these actions commute. By taking the quotient over both actions, we obtain a fibration over
\mathcal{X}(\pi_{1}(M),G)
. We denote the total space of the fibration by
\mathcal{X}(\pi_{1}(M),G,V)
. With the help of developing and holonomy maps the set
\mathcal{CH}_{h}(N,V)
injects in
\mathcal{X}(\pi_{1}(M),G,V)
as an open smooth subset so that the forgetful map
\mathcal{CH}_{h}(N,V)\rightarrow\mathcal{CH}_{h}(N)
is a smooth bundle with fibers homeomorphic to the space of injective maps
f:V\hookrightarrow N
. The group
MCG_{h}(M)
acts on
\mathcal{CH}(N)
freely and properly discontinuously determining a covering map
\mathcal{CH}(N)\rightarrow\mathcal{CH}_{h}(N).
We have the commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{CH}(N,V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{CH}_{h}(N,V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{CH}(N)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{CH}_{h}(N)}

from which we can locally pull back the topology on
\mathcal{CH}_{h}(N,V)
to the topology on
\mathcal{CH}(N,V)
preserving the bundle structure. This endows
\mathcal{CH}(N,V)
with the topology of a smooth manifold of dimension
3(n-k)
where
k
is the Euler characteristic of
\partial M
.

Assume that
V\subset\partial M
. By
\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
we denote the space of bent hyperbolic metrics
g
on
M
with
V(g)=V
considered up to isometries preserving
V
pointwise and isotopic to identity. We will frequently abuse the notation and write
g\in\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
meaning that
g
is a bent hyperbolic metric on
M
representing a class in
\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
.
A class in
\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
determines a class in
\mathcal{CH}(N,V)
and this defines an embedding of
\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
to
\mathcal{CH}(N,V)
, so we will consider
\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
as a subset of
\mathcal{CH}(N,V)
and endow it with the induced topology.

Lemma 3.25
.

\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
is open in
\mathcal{CH}(N,V)
.

Proof.

A pair
(\rho,\widetilde{f})\in\mathcal{R}(\pi_{1}(M),G)\times(\mathbb{H}^{3})^{V}
projects to an element of
\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
if
\rho
is a holonomy of a convex cocompact hyperbolic metric on
N
and if
\Lambda(\rho)
and
\widetilde{V}
, where
\widetilde{V}
is the
\rho
-orbit of
\widetilde{f}(V)
, are in a convex position. Take
g\in\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
and lift it to
(\rho,\widetilde{f})\in\mathcal{R}(\pi_{1}(M),G)\times(\mathbb{H}^{3})^{V}
. Lemma
2.34
says that when convex cocompact representations converge in
\mathcal{R}(\pi_{1}(M),G)
, their limit sets converge in the Hausdorff sense. Hence, if
g
does not have a neighborhood in
\mathcal{CH}(N,V)
belonging to
\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
, then for some
\widetilde{v}\in\widetilde{V}
there exists a sequence of 4-tuples
(p^{1}_{i},p^{2}_{i},p^{3}_{i},p^{4}_{i})
, where
p^{j}_{i}
is either in
\widetilde{V}
or in
\Lambda(\rho)
, such that the distance from
\widetilde{v}
to the convex span of the
i
-th 4-tuple tends to zero. Up to passing to subsequences, we can assume that all four sequences converge to points
p^{1},p^{2},p^{3},p^{4}\in\Lambda(\rho)
. Then
\widetilde{v}
belongs to the convex span of
p^{1},p^{2},p^{3},p^{4}
, which is a contradiction with that
(\rho,\widetilde{f})
projects to
\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
.
∎

By
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,V)
we denote the space of bent hyperbolic metrics
g
on
M
with
V(g)\subseteq V
considered up to isometries preserving
V
pointwise and isotopic to identity. Similarly to
\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
, the set
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,V)
embeds in
\mathcal{CH}(N,V)
and is endowed with the induced topology. It is easy to see that
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,V)
is exactly the closure of
\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
in
\mathcal{CH}(N,V)
.
From the description of the topology on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,V)
it follows that if a sequence
\{g_{i}\}
converges to
g
, then one can choose the embeddings of
\widetilde{M}(g_{i})
and
\widetilde{M}(g)
to
\mathbb{H}^{3}
such that the respective holonomy maps
\{\rho_{\overline{g}_{i}}\}
converge to
\rho_{\overline{g}}
.

Lemma 3.26
.

Let a sequence
\{g_{i}\}
converge to
g
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,V)
. Choose the embeddings of
\widetilde{M}(g_{i})
and
\widetilde{M}(g)
to
\mathbb{H}^{3}
as above. Then
\{\widetilde{M}(g_{i})\}
converges to
\widetilde{M}(g)
in the Hausdorff sense.

Proof.

Let
\widetilde{V}_{i}
and
\widetilde{V}
be the lifts of
V
in
\widetilde{M}(g_{i})
and
\widetilde{M}(g)
to
\mathbb{H}^{3}
. From the description of the topology on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,V)
the discrete sets
\widetilde{V}(g_{i})
converge to
\widetilde{V}
in the Hausdorff sense. Consider the Klein model of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
. Recall that the Hausdorff convergence of closed subsets of a metric space depends only on the topology of the space. By Lemma
2.34
,
\Lambda(\overline{g}_{i})
converge to
\Lambda(\overline{g})
in the Hausdorff sense. As
\widetilde{M}(g_{i})\cup\Lambda(\overline{g}_{i})={\rm conv}(\Lambda(\overline{g}_{i})\cup\widetilde{V}_{i})
and
\widetilde{M}(g)\cup\Lambda(\overline{g})={\rm conv}(\Lambda(\overline{g})\cup\widetilde{V})
, we get that the closed subsets
\widetilde{M}(g_{i})\cup\Lambda(\overline{g}_{i})
of the unit ball converge in the Hausdorff sense to
\widetilde{M}(g)\cup\Lambda(\overline{g})
. It is easy to check that then the same do their intersections with the interior of the unit ball, i.e.,
\widetilde{M}(g_{i})
converge in the Hausdorff sense to
\widetilde{M}(g)
.
∎

It is easy to conclude from this

Corollary 3.27
.

Let a sequence
\{g_{i}\}
converge to
g
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,V)
. Choose the embeddings of
\widetilde{M}(g_{i})
and
\widetilde{M}(g)
to
\mathbb{H}^{3}
as above. Then
\{\partial\widetilde{M}(g_{i})\}
converges to
\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
in the Hausdorff sense.

We will make use from the following standard fact

Lemma 3.28

(
[
2
]
, Theorem 4)
.

Let
S_{i}\subset\mathbb{H}^{3}
be a sequence of complete convex surfaces converging to a complete convex surface
S\subset\mathbb{H}^{3}
in the Hausdorff sense, and
p_{i},q_{i}\in S_{i}
be two sequences of points converging to
p,q\in S
. Then the intrinsic distances between
p_{i}
and
q_{i}
on
S_{i}
converge to the intrinsic distance between
p
and
q
on
S
.

For every
g\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,V)
the induced intrinsic metric on
\partial M(g)
is a convex hyperbolic cone-metric
d
with
V(d)\subseteq V
defined up to isometries preserving
V
pointwise and isotopic to identity. Hence, this defines the realization map

\mathcal{I}_{V}:\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,V)\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{c}(\partial M,V).

(note that in Section
1.2
in order to simplify the exposition we reduced the domain and the codomain of
\mathcal{I}_{V}
). We can deduce

Lemma 3.29
.

The map
{\mathcal{I}}_{V}
is continuous.

Proof.

Let
\{g_{i}\}\subset\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,V)
be a sequence converging to
g\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,V)
. We note that here by
g_{i}
and
g
we mean some concrete metrics on
M
such that their classes converge in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,V)
. Choose the embeddings of
\widetilde{M}(g_{i})
and
\widetilde{M}(g)
to
\mathbb{H}^{3}
such that
\rho_{\overline{g}_{i}}
converge to
\rho_{\overline{g}}
. Corollary
3.27
implies that
\partial\widetilde{M}(g_{i})
converges to
\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
in the Hausdorff sense. Let
d_{i}
and
d
be the induced intrinsic metrics on
\partial M(g_{i})
and
\partial M(g)
.

We claim that there exists a finite set
W\subset\partial M
,
W\supseteq V
, and a geodesic triangulation
\mathcal{T}
of
(\partial M,W,d)
such that each edge is the unique shortest arc between its endpoints. Indeed, first we claim that there exists a finite set
W^{\prime}\subset\partial M
,
W^{\prime}\supseteq V
and a geodesic triangulation
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
of
(\partial M,W^{\prime},d)
such that each edge is a shortest arc between its endpoints, but possibly not unique. To this purpose it is enough to start with any geodesic triangulation of
(\partial M,V,d)
and do the barycentric subdivision sufficiently many times. Next, it is easy to see that every proper sub-arc of a shortest arc is the unique shortest arc between the endpoints. Thus, if we divide every triangle of
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
into six triangles by drawing all the medians, we will obtain the desired set
W
and the triangulation
\mathcal{T}
.

By
\widetilde{W}_{i}
and
\widetilde{W}
we denote the full preimages of
W
in
\partial M(g_{i})
and
\partial M(g)
respectively. We make an isotopy of
g_{i}
(and respectively of
d_{i}
) fixing
V
such that for each
w\in W
the orbit of
w
under
\rho_{\overline{g}_{i}}
converge to the orbit under
\rho_{\overline{g}}
.

We prove that after this the classes of
\{d_{i}\}
converge to the class
d
in
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{c}(\partial M,W)
, which implies also that their classes converge in
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{c}(\partial M,V)
and proves the lemma. To this purpose we show that for all sufficiently large
i
the triangulation
\mathcal{T}
is realized in
d_{i}
and that the lengths of the edges converge to those in
d
. Let
e
be an edge of
\mathcal{T}
of length
l_{e}
in
d
,
\widetilde{e}
be a lift of
e
with endpoints
\widetilde{v},\widetilde{w}\in\widetilde{W}
, let
\widetilde{v}_{i},\widetilde{w}_{i}\in\widetilde{W}_{i}
be the respective points of
\widetilde{W}_{i}
converging to
\widetilde{v}
,
\widetilde{w}
. Lemma
3.28
shows that the distance between
\widetilde{v}_{i}
and
\widetilde{w}_{i}
converges to the distance between
\widetilde{v}
and
\widetilde{w}
. Connect
\widetilde{v}_{i}
and
\widetilde{w}_{i}
by an intrinsic shortest arc
\widetilde{e}_{i}
in
\partial\widetilde{M}(g_{i})
, so the lengths of
\widetilde{e}_{i}
converge to the length of
\widetilde{e}
. Parametrize the curves
\widetilde{e}_{i}
and
\widetilde{e}
by the arc length. We claim that
\widetilde{e}_{i}
converge to
\widetilde{e}
as parametrized curves. By
[
4
, Chapter II.1, Theorems 4 and 5]
from any subsequence of
\{\widetilde{e}_{i}\}
one can extract a further subsequence converging to a curve
\widetilde{e}^{\prime}\subset\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
of length at most
l_{e}
. Because
e
is the unique shortest arc between its endpoints, the same is true for
\widetilde{e}
, and we get that every arc
\widetilde{e}^{\prime}
obtained this way is actually
\widetilde{e}
. In particular, this argument implies that a subsequence of
\{\widetilde{e}_{i}\}
converges to
\widetilde{e}
. If, however,
\{\widetilde{e}_{i}\}
does not converge to
\widetilde{e}
itself, by the Hausdorff maximal principle we can choose a maximal subsequence
\{\widetilde{e}_{i_{j}}\}
of
\{\widetilde{e}_{i}\}
converging to
\widetilde{e}
. Then the complement to
\{\widetilde{e}_{i_{j}}\}
in
\{\widetilde{e}_{i}\}
should be infinite, but this means that we can again extract from the complement a subsequence converging to
\widetilde{e}
, which contradicts to the maximality of
\{\widetilde{e}_{i_{j}}\}
.

Let
e_{i}
be the projection of
\widetilde{e}_{i}
to
\partial M
. It follows from the construction that for all sufficiently large
i
the arc
e_{i}
belongs to the same isotopy class of arcs in
\partial M\backslash W
as
e
. Doing the described procedure for all edges of
\mathcal{T}
we obtain a collection of intrinsic geodesic arcs in
d_{i}
constituting a triangulation equivalent to
\mathcal{T}
for all sufficiently large
i
. The lengths of the respective arcs converge to the lengths of the arcs of
\mathcal{T}
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Denote the space of classes of controllably polyhedral metrics by
\mathcal{P}_{cp}(M,V)
and the space of classes of strictly polyhedral metrics by
\mathcal{P}_{sp}(M,V)
. We have

\mathcal{P}_{sp}(M,V)\subset\mathcal{P}_{cp}(M,V)\subset\mathcal{P}_{p}(M,V)\subset\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V).

We also denote by
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{p}(M,V)
,
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{cp}(M,V)
,
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{sp}(M,V)
the respective subsets of metrics of
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,V)
.

Definition 3.30
.

A hyperbolic metric
g
on
M
in
\mathcal{P}_{cp}(M,V)\backslash\mathcal{P}_{sp}(M,V)
is called
peculiar
.

Lemma 3.31
.

\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{sp}(M,V)
is open in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,V)
.

Proof.

One needs to check that the function
g(\partial M(g),\partial C(\overline{g}))=d_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(\partial\widetilde{M}(g),\partial\widetilde{C}(\overline{g}))
is continuous at zero over
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,V)
.
Consider
g\in\mathcal{\overline{}}{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,V)
such that

g(\partial M(g),\partial C(\overline{g}))=0.

Hence, there is a common point
p\in\partial\widetilde{M}(g)\cap\partial\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
. Let
\{g_{i}\}
be a sequence converging to
g
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,V)
. Due to Corollary
3.27
, we can choose the embeddings of
\widetilde{M}(g_{i})
and
\widetilde{M}(g)
to
\mathbb{H}^{3}
such that
\{\partial\widetilde{M}(g_{i})\}
converges to
\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
in the Hausdorff sense. Corollary
3.27
also implies
\partial\widetilde{C}(\overline{g}_{i})
converges to
\partial\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
in the Hausdorff sense. Hence, there exists a sequence of points
\{p_{i}\in\partial\widetilde{M}(g_{i})\}
and
\{p^{\prime}_{i}\in\partial\widetilde{C}(\overline{g}_{i})\}
converging to
p
. Thus,

g_{i}(\partial M(g_{i}),\partial C(\overline{g}_{i}))\rightarrow 0.

∎

Lemma 3.32
.

\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{cp}(M,V)
is open in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,V)
.

Proof.

Suppose the converse that there exists
g\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{cp}(M,V)
and a sequence

\{g_{i}\}\subset(\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,V)\backslash\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{cp}(M,V))

converging to
g
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,V)
. By
d_{i}
and
d
we denote the induced intrinsic metrics on
\partial M(g_{i})
and
\partial M(g)
. As
{\mathcal{I}}_{V}
is continuous, the metrics
d_{i}
converge to
d
in the weak Lipschitz sense. Then Lemma
2.18
implies that the systoles of
d_{i}
are uniformly bounded from below by some constant
\Delta>0
.

As
g_{i}\notin\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{cp}(M,V)
, Corollary
3.17
implies that
\partial M(g_{i})\cap\partial C(\overline{g}_{i})
contains at least one component, which is a hyperbolic surface with geodesic boundary. We can pick a hyperbolic pair of pants
P_{i}
in this component. The lengths of the boundaries are bounded from below by
\Delta
. Hence, Corollary
2.8
implies that there exists
\delta>0
and a point
p_{i}
in
P_{i}
such that the intrinsic distance from
p_{i}
to
\partial P_{i}
is at least
\delta
. We use this to choose an intrinsic geodesic triangle
T_{i}\subset P_{i}
containing
p_{i}
with the areas of all
T_{i}
uniformly bounded from below and the diameters uniformly bounded from above.

Consider the embeddings of
\widetilde{M}(g_{i})
and
\widetilde{M}(g)
to
\mathbb{H}^{3}
as in Corollary
3.27
, so
\{\partial\widetilde{M}(g_{i})\}
converges to
\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
in the Hausdorff sense and
\{\partial\widetilde{C}(\overline{g}_{i})\}
converges to
\partial\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
in the Hausdorff sense. We can choose lifts
\widetilde{p}_{i}\in\partial\widetilde{M}(g_{i})
of
p_{i}
such that the sequence
\{\widetilde{p}_{i}\}
is bounded in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
. Hence, up to passing to a subsequence we can assume that
\widetilde{p}_{i}
converge to a point
\widetilde{p}\in\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
. Now let
\widetilde{T}_{i}\subset(\partial\widetilde{M}(g_{i})\cap\partial\widetilde{C}(\overline{g}_{i}))
be the lift of
T_{i}
containing
\widetilde{p}_{i}
. As
\widetilde{T}_{i}
are compact subsets of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
staying in a bounded domain, up to taking a subsequence they must converge in the Hausdorff sense to a compact connected subset
\widetilde{T}
of
\partial\widetilde{M}(g)\cap\partial\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
. The latter consists only of disjoint geodesic lines, hence
\widetilde{T}
is a compact geodesic segment (possibly a point). However, Alexandrov showed that because
\widetilde{T}_{i}
and
\widetilde{T}
belong to complete convex surfaces
\partial\widetilde{M}(g_{i})
and
\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
, the areas of
\widetilde{T}_{i}
must converge to the area of
\widetilde{T}
, which is zero. In the Euclidean case this is
[
4
, Chapter X.2, Theorem 2]
, the changes for the hyperbolic case are discussed in
[
4
, Chapter XII.1]
. This contradicts to the uniform lower bound on the areas of
\widetilde{T}_{i}
.
∎

Note that Lemmas
3.31
and
3.32
particularly imply that
\mathcal{P}_{sp}(M,V)
and
\mathcal{P}_{cp}(M,V)
are open in
\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
.

3.4
Differentiability of the realization map

Our current aim is to study the differentiability properties of the realization map
\mathcal{I}_{V}
. In full generality this is a complicated task. We will restrict ourselves to controllably polyhedral metrics, and will prove that
\mathcal{I}_{V}
is
C^{1}
at strictly polyhedral metrics and differentiable at peculiar metrics.

3.4.1
Differentiability for strictly polyhedral metrics

Lemma 3.33
.

The realization map
\mathcal{I}_{V}
is
C^{1}
over
\mathcal{P}_{sp}(M,V)
.

Proof.

Pick
g\in\mathcal{P}_{sp}(M,V)
and its neighborhood
U
in
\mathcal{P}_{sp}(M,V)
. Lift
g
to
(\rho,\widetilde{f})\in\mathcal{R}(\pi_{1}(M),G)\times(\mathbb{H}^{3})^{V}
. Provided
U
is small enough, there exists a neighborhood
\widetilde{U}
of
(\rho,\widetilde{f})
projecting onto
U
such that the action of
\pi_{1}(M)
sends
\widetilde{U}
to disjoint domains. The action of
G
on
\widetilde{U}
defines a smooth submersion
\widetilde{U}\rightarrow U
. We will show that
\mathcal{I}_{V}
is
C^{1}
as a function over
\widetilde{U}
.

If there is an edge of
\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
between
\widetilde{v},\widetilde{w}\in\widetilde{V}(g)
, one can see that they are connected by an edge for all
(\rho^{\prime},\widetilde{f}^{\prime})
in
\widetilde{U}
provided that
\widetilde{U}
is sufficiently small. Indeed, the key is that the distance from any point of the edge to
\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
is positive and that the limit sets of converging representations converge in the Hausdorff sense. If there exists a sequence
(\rho_{i},\widetilde{f}_{i})
converging to
(\rho,\widetilde{f})
such that
\widetilde{v}
and
\widetilde{w}
are not connected by an edge in all
\partial\widetilde{M}(g_{i})
, then there exists a sequence of 4-tuples of points
(p^{1}_{i},p^{2}_{i},p^{3}_{i},p^{4}_{i})
, where all
p^{j}_{i}
are from
(\Lambda(\rho)\cup\widetilde{V}(g))\backslash\{\widetilde{v},\widetilde{w}\}
, such that the distance from the convex hull of each 4-tuple to the midpoint of the segment between
\widetilde{v}
and
\widetilde{w}
tends to zero. By passing to a subsequence we assume that each
p^{j}_{i}
converge to
p^{j}\in\Lambda(\rho)
, which means that the distance from the midpoint to
\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
is zero, which is a contradiction. Note that the length of such an edge is a smooth function in
\widetilde{U}
.

As
g\in\mathcal{P}_{sp}(M,V)
, all edges of
\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
are between pairs of vertices and all faces are convex compact hyperbolic polygons. In particular, the face decomposition of all
(\rho^{\prime},\widetilde{f}^{\prime})
in a sufficiently small neighborhood of
(\rho,\widetilde{f})
is a subdivision of the face decomposition of
\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
. Let
d
be the induced intrinsic metric on
\partial M(g)
. Consider a geodesic triangulation
\mathcal{T}
of
\partial M(g)
subdividing the face decomposition. As
\mathcal{T}
is realized in
(d,V)
, we have
d\in\mathcal{D}(V,\mathcal{T})
. The lengths of all edges of
\mathcal{T}
that are edges of
g
are smooth functions around
g
. We need to show that the lengths of the other edges of
\mathcal{T}
are
C^{1}
-functions.

Consider a non-triangular face
Q
of
\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
, let
V_{Q}
be the set of its vertices and
e
be a geodesic segment in the relative interior of
Q
corresponding to an edge of
\mathcal{T}
that is not an edge of
g
. When we move the vertices of
V_{Q}
,
{\rm conv}(V_{Q})
may become a convex polyhedron. If we move them sufficiently close to the initial positions, all boundary edges of
Q
remain among the edges of
{\rm conv}(V_{Q})
. They divide
\partial{\rm conv}(V_{Q})
into two parts. One of them, determined with the help of the orientation of
Q
with respect to
\widetilde{M}(g)
, is considered as a deformation of
Q
. We call this part
upper
. We also require that we perturb the vertices slightly enough so that the intrinsic angles of all the vertices in the upper part stay less than
\pi
. The segment
e
may become a broken line (i.e., after we move the vertices, we identify
e
with the intrinsic shortest arc in the upper part between the vertices of
Q
). Consider a small neighborhood
U_{Q}\subset(\mathbb{H}^{3})^{V_{Q}}
of the initial position of
V_{Q}
for which the boundary edges of
Q
remain to be in the boundary of
{\rm conv}(V_{Q})
and for which the intrinsic angles of all the vertices in the upper part stay less than
\pi
. We can consider the length
l_{e}
as a continuous function over
U_{Q}
. To finish the proof of the lemma, it is enough to show

Claim 3.33.1
.

The function
l_{e}
is
C^{1}
over
U_{Q}
and its differential in the initial position coincides with the differential of the
\mathbb{H}^{3}
-distance between the endpoints of
e
.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{T}_{r}
be all triangulations of
Q
. Pick one of them,
\mathcal{T}_{i}
, and now, as we move the vertices, consider the deformations of
Q
as of a surface triangulated with
\mathcal{T}_{i}
(i.e., the edges of
\mathcal{T}_{i}
remain geodesic segments of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
). It may become a non-convex surface, but near the initial position the edge
e
is still defined in the obtained surface as the intrinsic shortest arc between its endpoints. Its length
l_{i,e}
is a smooth function because the edge lengths of
\mathcal{T}_{i}
change smoothly and the length of
e
is a smooth function of them. We assume that
U_{Q}
is small enough so that for all such polyhedral surfaces the intrinsic angles of all vertices stay less than
\pi
. Then
e
is defined in all these surfaces over the whole
U_{Q}
. Let
U_{i}
be the subset of
U_{Q}
corresponding to the configurations where
\mathcal{T}_{i}
subdivides the face decomposition of the upper part of
{\rm conv}(V_{Q})
is triangulated with
\mathcal{T}_{i}
. One can see that this produces a decomposition of
U_{Q}
into cells
U_{1},\ldots,U_{r}
with non-empty disjoint interiors and piecewise-analytic boundaries. The function
l_{e}
is equal to
l_{i,e}
over
U_{i}
. In particular, it is smooth in the interiors of
U_{i}
. We need to check that if
U_{i}
,
U_{j}
have a common boundary, then the first derivatives of
l_{i,e}
,
l_{j,e}
coincide there.

Let
\Pi
be the plane containing
Q
, we orient it arbitrarily. Set a local coordinate system in
U_{Q}
: for each
v\in V_{Q}
choose two coordinates that are coordinates of the projections of
v
to
\Pi
and the third coordinate is the oriented distance from
v
to
\Pi
. Call the first two coordinates
horizontal
and the third
vertical
. Note that in the initial moment all the horizontal derivatives of all
l_{i,e}
coincide and are equal to the respective derivatives of the distance between the endpoints of
e
.

We now claim that all the vertical derivatives are zero. Indeed, let
ABC
be a hyperbolic triangle with the corresponding sides equal to
a,b,c
and with the right angle at
A
. Then we have

\cosh(a)=\cosh(b)\cosh(c).

Hence, for fixed
b>0
as we move the vertex
B
along the line
AB
we have
\partial a/\partial c=0
at
c=0
. This means that when we consider the bending of the polyhedral surface triangulated along
\mathcal{T}_{i}
, the vertical derivatives of all the edge lengths of
\mathcal{T}_{i}
are zero at the initial moment. Hence, so are the vertical derivatives of
l_{i,e}
. This implies that all first derivatives of all
l_{i,e}
coincide at the initial moment. The same argument implies that all first derivatives of any two
l_{i,e}
and
l_{j,e}
coincide at any point of the common boundary of
U_{i}
and
U_{j}
. This shows that
l_{e}
is
C^{1}
in
U_{Q}
. In turn this finishes the proof.
∎

3.4.2
Deformations of hyperbolic strips

In order to investigate the differentiability of the realization map for controllably polyhedral metrics, we need first to restrict ourselves to the case of closed solid hyperbolic tori with convex polyhedral boundary.

Only in this subsection by
M
we denote the closed solid torus and by
N
we denote its interior. It is easy to see that the space
\mathcal{CH}(N)
of convex cocompact hyperbolic metrics on
N
up to isotopy is in one-to-one correspondence with the set of loxodromic isometries of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
up to conjugation. Any such isometry
\gamma
is determined up to conjugacy by two parameters: the shift
a>0
along the geodesic line
\widetilde{\psi}\subset\mathbb{H}^{3}
fixed by
\gamma
and the rotation angle
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}
around
\widetilde{\psi}
, measured clockwise to the shift direction. We make the convention that
\widetilde{\psi}
is oriented in the shift direction. Let
\Gamma<{\rm Iso}^{+}(\mathbb{H}^{3})
be the cyclic subgroup generated by
\gamma
. The quotient hyperbolic manifold
\mathbb{H}^{3}/\Gamma
is convex cocompact and homeomorphic to
N
. Its convex core is the closed geodesic
\psi
that is the image of
\widetilde{\psi}
. Note that for every
\overline{g}\in\mathcal{CH}(N)
the hyperbolic manifold
N(\overline{g})
has a connected two-dimensional isometry group. Hence, for
V\neq\emptyset
the space
\mathcal{CH}(N,V)
has dimension
3n
, and so is the dimension of
\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
. It is evident that every bent hyperbolic metric on
M
is controllably polyhedral, so
\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)=\mathcal{P}_{p}(M,V)=\mathcal{P}_{cp}(M,V)
.

Let
g_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{cp}(M,V)
. We are interested in the case when
g_{0}
is peculiar. Clearly, this is equivalent to the situation when all points of
\widetilde{V}(g_{0})\subset\mathbb{H}^{3}
belong to a closed half-space containing
\widetilde{\psi}
in its boundary plane. Particularly,
\alpha_{0}=0
, where
\alpha_{0}
is the rotation parameter for
g_{0}
. Also, if
\alpha_{0}=0
and
\overline{\rm conv}(V)
is homeomorphic to
M
, one can see that
n=|V|\geq 2
. By
\Sigma
we denote the union of faces whose closures contain
\psi
(there are either two or one of them), and by
\widetilde{\Sigma}\subset\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
denote its universal cover. In this section we study deformations of the induced metric on
\Sigma
when we vary
g_{0}
, so we expel from
V
all vertices except the vertices of
\Sigma
. Of special interest are the deformations when
\alpha
becomes nonzero.
Our aim is to show that the induced metric is differentiable at
g_{0}
and that the partial derivative of the metric by
\alpha
is zero. Fix an intrinsically geodesic triangulation
\mathcal{T}
of
\Sigma
. There are two types of edges in
\mathcal{T}
: some connect vertices from different components of
\partial\Sigma
and some connect vertices from the same component. In order to consider less cases we assume that all interior edges of
\Sigma
are of the first type. It is easy to see that such
\mathcal{T}
exists. Let
e
be an interior edge of
\mathcal{T}
.

At the initial moment all vertices of
\widetilde{\Sigma}
belong to two half-planes intersecting at
\widetilde{\psi}
, which we denote by
\Pi^{1}
and
\Pi^{2}
. We denote the vertices with the lifts belonging to
\Pi^{1}
by
v^{1}_{1},\ldots,v^{1}_{n_{1}}
and to
\Pi^{2}
by
v^{2}_{1},\ldots,v^{2}_{n_{2}},
where
n_{1}+n_{2}=n
. For each
v^{j}_{i}
, where
j=1,2
, we fix a lift
\widetilde{v}^{j}_{i}\in\partial\widetilde{\Sigma}
. By
u^{j}_{i}
we denote the projection of
\widetilde{v}^{j}_{i}
to
\widetilde{\psi}
. By
x^{j}_{i,0}
we denote the distance from
\widetilde{v}^{j}_{i}
to
\widetilde{\psi}
. By
y^{j}_{i,0}
we denote the oriented distance from
u^{1}_{1}
to
u^{j}_{i}
. We assume that
v^{1}_{1}
and
v^{2}_{1}
are the endpoints of
e
and consider its lift
\widetilde{e}
, which is the unique intrinsic shortest arc in
\widetilde{\Sigma}
between
\widetilde{v}^{1}_{1}
and
\widetilde{v}^{2}_{1}
. By possibly changing the labels, we assume that
\widetilde{\psi}
is oriented in the direction from
u^{1}_{1}
to
u^{2}_{1}
. By
\phi_{0}\geq\pi
we denote the angle from
\Pi^{1}
to
\Pi^{2}
clockwise.

Note that when we vary
g_{0}
so that
\alpha
becomes nonzero, the strip
\Sigma
becomes triangulated. There are infinitely many triangulations of
\Sigma
realized by metrics in any neighborhood of
g_{0}
. This is the reason, why we can not really apply the methods of Section
3.4.1
to show the differentiability of the boundary metric, and have to look for another way.

Take
g\in\mathcal{P}_{cp}(M,V)
sufficiently close to
g_{0}
so that all edges of
g_{0}
except possibly
\psi
remain to be edges of
g
. Then the boundary edges of
\Sigma
divide
\partial M(g)
into two strips. One of them is a deformation of
\Sigma
and we will continue to denote it
\Sigma
. We assume that
g
is sufficiently close to
g_{0}
so that the intrinsic angles of the vertices in
\Sigma
remain to be less than
\pi
.
We choose the same lifts of all
v^{j}_{i}
, so
u^{j}_{i}
remain defined also for
g
, and we define
x^{j}_{i}
and
y^{j}_{i}
in a similar fashion to
x^{j}_{i,0}
and
y^{j}_{i,0}
. Hence,
x^{j}_{i}
and
y^{j}_{i}
are smooth functions in
\mathcal{P}_{cp}(M,V)
. By
\Pi^{j}_{i}
we denote the half-plane spanned by
\widetilde{\psi}
and
\widetilde{v}^{j}_{i}
.
By
\phi^{j}_{i}
we denote the angle between the segments
u^{j}_{i}\widetilde{v}^{j}_{i}
and the half-plane
\Pi^{1}_{1}
. Since for
g_{0}
we have
\phi^{1}_{i,0}=0
,
\phi^{2}_{i,0}=\phi_{0}
, we consider
\phi^{1}_{i}
as a real number valuing in a neighborhood of 0, and
\phi^{2}_{i}
as a real number valuing in a neighborhood of
\phi_{0}
.
One can see that the
3n
variables

x^{1}_{1},\ldots,x^{1}_{n_{1}},x^{2}_{1},\ldots,x^{2}_{n_{2}},y^{1}_{2},\ldots,y^{1}_{n_{1}},y^{2}_{1},\ldots,y^{2}_{n_{2}},\phi^{1}_{2},\ldots,\phi^{1}_{n_{1}},\phi^{2}_{1},\ldots,\phi^{2}_{n_{2}},a,\alpha

provide local coordinates in a neighborhood of
g_{0}
in
\mathcal{P}_{cp}(M,V)
. Particularly we use these coordinates to consider a neighborhood of
g_{0}
as a domain in a
(3n)
-dimensional real vector space, and by
\|.\|
we denote the standard Euclidean norm there.

Since now and until the end of this section we are going to work only in the universal cover embedded in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
. To simplify the reading, until the end of this section we drop tilde in
\widetilde{M}(g)
,
\widetilde{\psi}
,
\widetilde{\Sigma}
,
\widetilde{v}^{j}_{i}
, writing just
M(g)
,
\psi
,
\Sigma
,
v^{j}_{i}
instead.

We will continue to denote the intrinsic shortest arc in
\Sigma
with the metric induced by
g
between
v^{1}_{1}
and
v^{2}_{1}
by
e
. By
l_{e}
we denote its length, so we consider
l_{e}
as a function in a neighborhood of
g_{0}
, particularly a function of our
3n
variables. The results of Section
3.4.1
apply also to the situation we have now provided that
\alpha\neq 0
. Hence,
l_{e}
is
C^{1}
when
\alpha\neq 0
. By
e^{\prime}
we denote the intrinsic shortest arc between
v^{1}_{1}
and
v^{2}_{1}
in
\Pi^{1}_{1}\cup\Pi^{2}_{1}
. By
l^{\prime}_{e}
we denote the length of
e^{\prime}
. The function
l^{\prime}_{e}
is a smooth function of three variables:
x^{1}_{1}
,
x^{2}_{1}
,
y^{2}_{1}
. Our aim is to show

Lemma 3.34
.

For every
0<\mu<1
the difference
l_{e}-l^{\prime}_{e}
is
O(\|\Delta g\|^{1+\mu})
at
g_{0}
.

Here
\Delta g=g-g_{0}
is an abstract vector in our coordinates.

Corollary 3.35
.

The function
l_{e}
is differentiable at
g_{0}
. Moreover, its differential coincides with the differential of the function
l^{\prime}_{e}
. In particular, its derivatives with respect to
\phi^{j}_{i}
and to
\alpha
are zero.

We have to consider separately the cases
\phi_{0}>\pi
and
\phi_{0}=\pi
. In the case
\phi_{0}>\pi
we assume that the deformation is small enough so that all differences
\phi^{2}_{i_{2}}-\phi^{1}_{i_{1}}
are greater than
\pi
. Then the realization of
e
in
\partial M(g)
belongs to the concave part of
\mathbb{H}^{3}\backslash(\Pi^{1}_{1}\cup\Pi^{2}_{1})
, so by the Busemann–Feller lemma we have
l^{\prime}_{e}\leq l_{e}
and the non-trivial part of Lemma
3.34
is to provide an upper bound on
l_{e}-l^{\prime}_{e}
. The case
\phi=\pi
is slightly more involved, but the upper bound still remains the main issue of the proof.

Let
p\in\partial M(g)
and
q
be its projection to
\psi
. By
\phi_{p}\in\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}
we denote the angle between the segment
pq
and
\Pi^{1}_{1}
clockwise. By
t_{p}
we denote the signed distance
u^{1}_{1}q
. This provides a parametrization of
\partial M(g)
. However, we are more interested in the coordinate
\phi_{p}
extended to the universal cover of
\partial M(g)
(recall that
\partial M(g)
is topologically a cylinder). That is, we consider the universal cover of
\partial M
, fix a lift
\widehat{v}^{1}_{1}
of
v^{1}_{1}
and for
p\in\partial M(g)
by
\theta_{p}\in\mathbb{R}
we denote the extended oriented angle from
\widehat{v}^{1}_{1}
to the lift
\widehat{p}
of
p
such that
\widehat{p}
belongs to the same lift of
\Sigma
or of
\partial M(g)\backslash\Sigma
as
\widehat{v}^{1}_{1}
.

Fix
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
such that
0<\mu<1
and suppose that
\phi_{0}>\pi
. Set
\mu^{\prime}=(1+\mu)/2.
Consider in
\Sigma
the curve
e^{\prime\prime}
consisting of the following three arcs. Denote the intersection point of
e^{\prime}
with
\psi
by
u
. Let
p_{1}\in\Sigma
be a point such that

(1) the plane spanned by
v^{1}_{1}
,
u
and
p_{1}
is orthogonal to
\Pi^{1}_{1}
;

(2)
\theta_{p_{1}}=\max_{1\leq i\leq n_{1}}\phi^{1}_{i}+|\alpha|^{\mu^{\prime}}

Similarly, let
p_{2}
be the point such that

(1) the plane spanned by
v^{2}_{1}
,
u
and
p_{2}
is orthogonal to
\Pi^{2}_{1}
;

(2)
\theta_{p_{2}}=\min_{1\leq i\leq n_{2}}\phi^{2}_{i}-|\alpha|^{\mu^{\prime}}
.

Now let
e^{\prime\prime}_{1}
be the curve connecting
v^{1}_{1}
with
p_{1}
in the intersection of
\partial M(g)
with the plane
v^{1}_{1}up_{1}
in the clockwise direction. Similarly, let
e^{\prime\prime}_{2}
be the curve connecting
v^{2}_{1}
with
p_{2}
in the intersection of
\partial M(g)
with the plane
v^{2}_{1}up_{2}
in the counterclockwise direction. Finally, let
e^{\prime\prime}_{3}
be the curve connecting
p_{1}
with
p_{2}
in the intersection of
\partial M(g)
with the plane spanned by
u,p_{1},p_{2}
in the clockwise direction. Altogether the arcs
e^{\prime\prime}_{1}
,
e^{\prime\prime}_{3}
,
e^{\prime\prime}_{2}
form a curve in
\Sigma
connecting
v^{1}_{1}
with
v^{2}_{1}
. By
l^{\prime\prime}_{e}
we denote its length. We have
l^{\prime\prime}_{e}\geq l_{e}\geq l^{\prime}_{e}
. Thus, Lemma
3.34
for
\phi_{0}>\pi
follows from

Lemma 3.36
.

Let
\phi_{0}>\pi
. For every
0<\mu<1
the difference
l^{\prime\prime}_{e}-l^{\prime}_{e}
is
O(\|\Delta g\|^{1+\mu})
at
g_{0}
.

Now consider the case
\phi_{0}=\pi
. In this case we propose a different construction of the auxiliary curve
e^{\prime\prime}
. Emit a geodesic ray from
u
such that it is orthogonal to
\psi
, directed towards
\Sigma
, and the plane spanned by this ray and
\psi
bisects the dihedral angle between
\Pi^{1}_{1}
and
\Pi^{2}_{1}
. Let
p_{0}
be the intersection point of this ray with
\Sigma
. Let
e^{\prime\prime}_{1}
be the curve connecting
v^{1}_{1}
with
p_{0}
in the intersection of
\partial M(g)
with the plane
v^{1}_{1}up_{0}
in the clockwise direction, and
e^{\prime\prime}_{2}
be the curve connecting
p_{0}
with
v^{2}_{1}
in the intersection of
\partial M(g)
with the plane
v^{2}_{1}up_{0}
. Altogether the arcs
e^{\prime\prime}_{1}
and
e^{\prime\prime}_{2}
form a curve in
\Sigma
connecting
v^{1}_{1}
with
v^{2}_{1}
, which we again denote by
e^{\prime\prime}
. By
l^{\prime\prime}_{e}
we denote its length. We have
l^{\prime\prime}_{e}\geq l_{e}
. We will similarly show

Lemma 3.37
.

Let
\phi_{0}=\pi
and
\phi^{2}_{1}\geq\pi
. For every
0<\mu<1
the difference
l^{\prime\prime}_{e}-l^{\prime}_{e}
is
O(\|\Delta g\|^{1+\mu})
at
g_{0}
.

In the case
\phi^{2}_{1}\geq\pi
, by the Busemann–Feller lemma we have
l_{e}\geq l^{\prime}_{e}
and Lemma
3.34
follows from Lemma
3.37
. When
\phi^{2}_{1}<\pi
, a simple additional argument is required, which we provide below.

Denote the hyperbolic metric of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
by
d_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}
. To prove our claims we need to make the following estimates on the geometry of
\Sigma
.

Lemma 3.38
.

Let
\phi_{0}>\pi
,
\alpha\neq 0
. For every
0<\mu<1
and every
T>0
we have

\sup\{d_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(p,\psi):p\in\Sigma,-T\leq t_{p}\leq T,\max_{1\leq i\leq n_{1}}\phi^{1}_{i}+|\alpha|^{\mu}\leq\theta_{p}\leq\min_{1\leq i\leq n_{2}}\phi^{2}_{i}-|\alpha|^{\mu}\}=O(\|\Delta g\|^{2}).

Lemma 3.39
.

Let
\alpha\neq 0
. For every
0<\mu<1
and every
T>0
we have
