\sup\{d_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(p,\Pi^{1}_{1}):p\in\Sigma,-T\leq t_{p}\leq T,0\leq\theta_{p}\leq\frac{\pi}{2}\}=O(\|\Delta g\|^{\mu}),

\sup\{d_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(p,\Pi^{2}_{1}):p\in\Sigma,-T\leq t_{p}\leq T,\phi^{2}_{1}-\frac{\pi}{2}\leq\theta_{p}\leq\phi^{2}_{1}\}=O(\|\Delta g\|^{\mu}).

We postpone proofs of Lemmas
3.38
–
3.39
and first deduce Lemmas
3.36
and
3.37
from them. To this purpose we also need few less involved estimates

Lemma 3.40
.

Fix
0<\mu\leq 1
and let
\xi>0
be a parameter. Consider the points
p,p^{\prime}\in\mathbb{H}^{2}
connected by a geodesic segment
\psi
. Let
\chi
and
\chi^{\prime}
be geodesic rays from
p
and
p^{\prime}
at the same side of
\psi
, and
\sigma
be a convex arc starting at
p
tangent to
\chi
and ending at a point
p_{0}\in\chi^{\prime}
. By
r_{0}
denote the foot of the perpendicular from
p_{0}
to
\psi
. Assume that

(1) the angle between
\psi
and
\chi
is
O(\xi^{\mu})
;

(2) the angle between
\psi
and
\chi^{\prime}
is
O(\xi^{\mu})
;

(3) the length of
\psi
is
O(1)
.

Here all asymptotics are as
\xi
tends to zero. Then

{\rm len}(\sigma)-pr_{0}=O(\xi^{2\mu}).

Here and in what follows we will abuse the notation and sometimes write
pr_{0}
for the length of the geodesic segment
pr_{0}
.

Proof.

We have
{\rm len}(\sigma)-pr_{0}\geq 0
. Clearly, the rays
\chi
and
\chi^{\prime}
intersect provided that
\xi
is sufficiently small, denote the intersection point by
p^{\prime\prime}
. Let
\sigma^{\prime\prime}
be the broken line consisting of the segments
pp^{\prime\prime}
and
p^{\prime\prime}p_{0}
. By the Busemann–Feller lemma we have
{\rm len}(\sigma^{\prime\prime})\geq{\rm len}(\sigma)
. Thus, it is enough to show that
{\rm len}(\sigma^{\prime\prime})-pr_{0}=O(\xi^{2\mu}).

Let
r
be the foot of the perpendicular from
p^{\prime\prime}
to
\psi
. We have

\tanh(pr)=\tanh(pp^{\prime\prime})\cos(\angle p^{\prime\prime}pr).

As
\xi\rightarrow 0
we get from (1)

1-\cos(\angle p^{\prime\prime}pr)=O(\xi^{2\mu}).

Also clearly
pp^{\prime\prime}=O(1)
,
pr=O(1)
. Thus, we can estimate

0\leq pp^{\prime\prime}-pr\leq\sinh(pp^{\prime\prime}-pr)=\cosh(pp^{\prime\prime})\cosh(pr)(\tanh(pp^{\prime\prime})-\tanh(pr))=\\
=O(1)\tanh(pp^{\prime\prime})(1-\cos(\angle p^{\prime\prime}pr))=O(\xi^{2}).

(3.1)

Now for the trapezoid
p^{\prime\prime}p_{0}r_{0}r
we have from Lemma
2.3

\frac{\sinh(p^{\prime\prime}p_{0})}{\sinh(rr_{0})}=\frac{\cosh(p^{\prime\prime}r)}{\sin({\angle}p^{\prime\prime}p_{0}r_{0})}.

(3.2)

From

\tan({\angle}p_{0}p^{\prime}r_{0})\cosh(p_{0}p^{\prime})=\cot({\angle}p^{\prime}p_{0}r_{0})=\tan\left({\angle}p^{\prime\prime}p_{0}r_{0}-\frac{\pi}{2}\right)

we get
0\leq{\angle}p^{\prime\prime}p_{0}r_{0}-\frac{\pi}{2}=O(\xi^{\mu}).
Note that

\cosh(p^{\prime\prime}r)-1=O(\xi^{2\mu}),~~~~~1-\cos(\angle p_{0}p^{\prime}r_{0})=O(\xi^{2\mu}).

Using this and (
3.2
) we obtain

0\leq p^{\prime\prime}p_{0}-rr_{0}\leq 2\sinh\left(\frac{p^{\prime\prime}p_{0}-rr_{0}}{2}\right)\leq 2\sinh\left(\frac{p^{\prime\prime}p_{0}-rr_{0}}{2}\right)\cosh\left(\frac{p^{\prime\prime}p_{0}+rr_{0}}{2}\right)=\\
=\sinh(p^{\prime\prime}p_{0})-\sinh(rr_{0})=\sinh(p^{\prime\prime}p_{0})\left(1-\cos\left({\angle}p^{\prime\prime}p_{0}r_{0}-\frac{\pi}{2}\right)\right)+\\
+\sinh(rr_{0})(\cosh(p^{\prime\prime}r)-1)=O(\xi^{2\mu}).

(3.3)

Altogether (
3.1
) and (
3.3
) give us

0\leq{\rm len}(\sigma^{\prime\prime})-pr_{0}=pp^{\prime\prime}+p^{\prime}p^{\prime\prime}-pr-rr_{0}=O(\xi^{2\mu}).

∎

Lemma 3.41
.

Fix
0<\mu<1
and let
\xi>0
be a parameter. Let
\psi
be a geodesic line in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
,
C
be the surface consisting of all points at distance
x
from
\psi
where
x=O(\xi^{2})
as
\xi
tends to zero. Consider two points
p,p^{\prime}\in C
, let
q,q^{\prime}
be their projections to
\psi
and
\sigma\subset C
be an intrinsic shortest arc connecting them. Assume that
qq^{\prime}=O(1)
. Then

0\leq{\rm len}(\sigma)-qq^{\prime}=O(\xi^{2}).

Proof.

Let
p^{\prime\prime}
be the point at the intersection of
C
, the plane spanned by
p
and
\psi
and of the plane orthogonal to
\psi
at
q^{\prime}
. Let
\sigma^{\prime\prime}\subset C
be the intrinsic shortest arc connecting
p
to
p^{\prime\prime}
and
\sigma^{\prime}\subset C
be the intrinsic shortest arc connecting
p^{\prime}
to
p^{\prime\prime}
. We then have
{\rm len}(\sigma^{\prime\prime})=qq^{\prime}\cosh(x)
. Thus,
0\leq{\rm len}(\sigma^{\prime\prime})-qq^{\prime}=O(\xi^{2})
. Also
\sigma^{\prime}
is an arc of a circle centered at
q^{\prime}
with radius
x
, hence
{\rm len}(\sigma^{\prime})=O(\xi^{2})
. Finally,

{\rm len}(\sigma)-qq^{\prime}\leq{\rm len}(\sigma^{\prime})+{\rm len}(\sigma^{\prime\prime})-qq^{\prime}=O(\xi^{2}).

∎

Now we are ready to prove Lemma
3.36
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\alpha\neq 0
. Consider the arc
e^{\prime\prime}_{1}
. It belongs to the orthogonal plane to
\Pi^{1}_{1}
passing through
v^{1}_{1}
and
u
. Let
r_{1}
be the foot of the perpendicular from
p_{1}
to
v^{1}_{1}u
and
q_{1}
be the foot of the perpendicular from
p_{1}
to
\psi
. We want to apply Lemma
3.40
to
e^{\prime\prime}_{1}
and to the segment
v^{1}_{1}u
. Considering the spherical link at the point
u
we get

\tan({\angle}p_{1}uv^{1}_{1})=\tan(\theta_{p_{1}})\sin({\angle}q_{1}uv^{1}_{1}).

Thus, from
\theta_{p_{1}}=O(\|\Delta g\|^{\mu^{\prime}})
we get
{\angle}p_{1}uv^{1}_{1}=O(\|\Delta g\|^{\mu^{\prime}})
. It remains to show that the angle between the tangent ray to
e^{\prime\prime}_{1}
and
v^{1}_{1}u
at
v^{1}_{1}
is
O(\|\Delta g\|^{\mu^{\prime}})
. Denote this angle by
\eta_{1}
.

Let
v,w
be the neighbouring vertices of
v^{1}_{1}
in
\partial\Sigma
. By
z_{1}
denote the intrinsic distance from
v^{1}_{1}
to the intrinsic geodesic segment
vw
in
\Sigma
. Let
p^{\prime}_{1}
be the intersection point of
e^{\prime\prime}_{2}
with
vw
. Note that the part of
e^{\prime\prime}_{1}
between
v^{1}_{1}
and
p^{\prime}_{1}
is a geodesic segment in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
and
v^{1}_{1}p^{\prime}_{1}\geq z_{1}
. Due to Lemma
3.39
, we have
d_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(p^{\prime}_{1},\Pi^{1}_{1})=O(|g|^{\mu^{\prime}})
. Thus, from

\sinh(d_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(p^{\prime}_{1},\Pi^{1}_{1}))=\sin(\eta_{1})\sinh(v^{1}_{1}p^{\prime}_{1})

we get
\eta_{1}=O(\|\Delta g\|^{\mu^{\prime}})
.

Now we can apply Lemma
3.40
. We obtain

{\rm len}(e^{\prime\prime}_{1})-v^{1}_{1}r_{1}=O(\|\Delta g\|^{2\mu^{\prime}})=O(\|\Delta g\|^{1+\mu}).

We deal just the same with the arc
e^{\prime\prime}_{2}
of
e^{\prime\prime}
between
v^{2}_{1}
and
p_{2}
. Let
r_{2}
be the foot of the perpendicular from
p_{2}
to
v^{2}_{1}u
. We get

{\rm len}(e^{\prime\prime}_{2})-v^{2}_{1}r_{2}=O(\|\Delta g\|^{1+\mu}).

Now we need to bound
{\rm len}(e^{\prime\prime}_{3})-r_{1}u-r_{2}u
. By
u_{1}
,
u_{2}
denote the projections of
r_{1}
,
r_{2}
to
\psi
. We have
r_{1}u\geq u_{1}u
,
r_{2}u\geq u_{2}u
.
Lemma
3.39
shows that

\sup_{p\in e^{\prime\prime}_{3}}d_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(p,\psi)=O(\|\Delta g\|^{2}).

Hence we can choose a radius
x=O(\|\Delta g\|^{2})
such that
e^{\prime\prime}_{3}
belongs to the set of points at distance at most
x
from
\psi
. Let
C
be the boundary of this set,
p^{\prime\prime}_{1}
and
p^{\prime\prime}_{2}
be the intersections of
C
with the rays
u_{1}p_{1}
,
u_{2}p_{2}
, let
\sigma\subset C
be the intrinsic shortest arc connecting
p^{\prime\prime}_{1}
and
p^{\prime\prime}_{2}
. Lemma
3.41
implies that
{\rm len}(\sigma)-u_{1}u_{2}=O(\|\Delta g\|^{2})
. The Busemann–Feller lemma shows that

{\rm len}(e^{\prime\prime}_{3})\leq p_{1}p^{\prime\prime}_{1}+{\rm len}(\sigma)+p^{\prime\prime}_{2}p_{2}={\rm len}(\sigma)+O(\|\Delta g\|^{2}).

Thus,
{\rm len}(e^{\prime\prime}_{3})-u_{1}u_{2}=O(\|\Delta g\|^{2}).

Hence, also
{\rm len}(e^{\prime\prime}_{3})-r_{1}u-r_{2}u=O(\|\Delta g\|^{2}).

Summing this up we get

l^{\prime\prime}_{e}-l^{\prime}_{e}=O(\|\Delta g\|^{1+\mu}).

Now suppose that
\alpha=0
. Then the arc
e^{\prime\prime}_{3}
is the union of two geodesic segments,
p_{1}u
and
p_{2}u
. Denote similarly the points
p^{\prime}_{1}
,
p^{\prime}_{2}
and the angles
\eta_{1}
,
\eta_{2}
. We have

d_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(p^{\prime}_{1},\Pi^{1}_{1})\leq\sinh(d_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(p^{\prime}_{1},\Pi^{1}_{1}))\leq\max\{\sin(|\phi^{1}_{i}|)\}\max\{\sinh(x^{1}_{i})\}=O(\|\Delta g\|).

Hence, also
\eta_{1}=O(\|\Delta g\|)
and from Lemma
3.40
we get

{\rm len}(e^{\prime\prime}_{1})+p_{1}u-v^{1}_{1}u=O(\|\Delta g\|^{2}).

Similarly,

{\rm len}(e^{\prime\prime}_{2})+p_{2}u-v^{2}_{1}u=O(\|\Delta g\|^{2})

and altogether

l^{\prime\prime}_{e}-l^{\prime}_{e}={\rm len}(e^{\prime\prime}_{1})+p_{1}u+p_{2}u+{\rm len}(e^{\prime\prime}_{2})-v^{1}_{1}u-v^{2}_{1}u=O(\|\Delta g\|^{2}),

which finishes the proof.
∎

For Lemma
3.37
we also require

Lemma 3.42
.

Fix
0<\mu\leq 1
and let
\xi>0
be a parameter. Let
p_{1}p_{0}p_{2}q
be a hyperbolic quadrilateral with
{\angle}p_{1}qp_{2}\geq\pi
, but with the other angles
<\pi
, such that

(1) the angle
{\angle}p_{1}qp_{2}-\pi=O(\xi)
;

(2) the angles
{\angle}p_{0}p_{1}q
and
{\angle}p_{0}p_{2}q
are
O(\xi^{\mu})
;

(3) the lengths
p_{1}q
and
p_{2}q
are
O(1)
.

Here all asymptotics are as
\xi
tends to zero. Let
\sigma
be a convex arc connecting
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
such that
q
belongs to the convex side. Then

{\rm len}(\sigma)-p_{1}q-p_{2}q=O(\xi^{2\mu}).

Proof.

We have
{\rm len}(\sigma)-p_{1}q-p_{2}q\geq 0
. Let
\sigma^{\prime}
be the broken line consisting of the segments
p_{1}p_{0}
and
p_{2}p_{0}
. By the Busemann–Feller lemma we have
{\rm len}(\sigma^{\prime})\geq{\rm len}(\sigma)
. Thus, it is enough to show

{\rm len}(\sigma^{\prime})-p_{1}q-p_{2}q=p_{1}p_{0}+p_{2}p_{0}-p_{1}q-p_{2}q=O(\xi^{2\mu}).

Let the ray
p_{1}q
intersect the segment
p_{0}p_{2}
in a point
p^{\prime}_{0}
. It is easy to check that

{\angle}p_{0}p^{\prime}_{0}p_{1}=O(\xi^{\mu}).

Applying Lemma
3.40
to the triangle
p_{1}p_{0}p^{\prime}_{0}
we get

p_{1}p_{0}+p_{0}p^{\prime}_{0}-p^{\prime}_{0}p_{1}=O(\xi^{2\mu}).

Applying Lemma
3.40
to the triangle
p_{2}qp^{\prime}_{0}
we obtain

qp^{\prime}_{0}+p^{\prime}_{0}p_{2}-p_{2}q=O(\xi^{2\mu}).

Altogether this gives

p_{1}p_{0}+p_{2}p_{0}-p_{1}q-p_{2}q=O(\xi^{2\mu}).

∎

Now we prove Lemma
3.37
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\alpha\neq 0
. Let
\eta_{1}
be the angle between the tangent ray to
e^{\prime\prime}_{1}
and
v^{1}_{1}u
at
v^{1}_{1}
,
\eta_{2}
be the angle between the tangent ray to
e^{\prime\prime}_{2}
and
v^{2}_{1}u
at
v^{2}_{1}
. Similarly to the proof of Lemma
3.36
, from Lemma
3.39
we get that
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
are
O(\|\Delta g\|^{\mu^{\prime}})
. Consider the union of the half-planes of the planes
v^{1}_{1}p_{0}u
and
v^{2}_{1}p_{0}u
containing the arc
e^{\prime\prime}_{1}
. Develop them to
\mathbb{H}^{2}
. Clearly, when
\|\Delta g\|
is sufficiently small, the tangent rays to
e^{\prime\prime}_{2}
at its endpoints intersect in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
. Let
p^{\prime}_{0}
be the intersection point. Our result follows from applying Lemma
3.42
to the quadrilateral
v^{1}_{1}p^{\prime}_{0}v^{2}_{1}u
and the arc
e^{\prime\prime}_{2}
.

For
\alpha=0
we argue like in the proof of Lemma
3.36
to obtain that
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
are
O(\|\Delta g\|)
. Then Lemma
3.42
gives us

l^{\prime\prime}_{e}-l^{\prime}_{e}=O(\|\Delta g\|^{2}),

which finishes the proof.
∎

We can finish the proof of Lemma
3.34
.

Proof of Lemma
3.34
.

Due to the discussion above, Lemma
3.34
follows from Lemmas
3.36
and
3.37
except the case
\phi_{0}=0
,
\phi^{2}_{1}<\pi
, so we deal with this case here. Suppose that
\alpha\neq 0
. Let
\Pi
be the plane containing the half-plane
\Pi^{1}_{1}
and
v_{0}
be the orthogonal projection of
v^{2}_{1}
to
\Pi
. By
e_{0}
we denote the union of the segments
v^{1}_{1}u
and
uv_{0}
, by
l_{0}
we denote its length. We act now similarly to the proof of Lemma
3.37
. By
\eta_{1}
,
\eta_{2}
we denote the angles between the tangent rays to
e^{\prime\prime}_{2}
at its endpoints and between the segments connecting the endpoints with
u
. We get that
\eta_{1}
,
\eta_{2}
are
O(\|\Delta g\|^{\mu^{\prime}})
. Since the dihedral angle between
\Pi^{2}_{1}
and
\Pi
is
O(\|\Delta g\|)
, it is easy to see that when
\|\Delta g\|
is small enough, the extension of the tangent ray to
e^{\prime\prime}_{2}
at
v^{2}_{1}
in the converse direction intersects
\Pi
and the intersection angle is
O(\|\Delta g\|^{\mu^{\prime}})
. Applying Lemma
3.40
we get

l^{\prime\prime}_{e}-l_{0}=O(\|\Delta g\|^{2\mu^{\prime}}).

By
l^{\prime}_{0}
we denote the length
v^{1}_{1}v_{0}
. It is easy to check that since the dihedral angle between
\Pi^{2}_{1}
and
\Pi
is
O(\|\Delta g\|)
, we get
l^{\prime}_{0}-l_{0}=O(\|\Delta g\|^{2})
and
l^{\prime}_{e}-l^{\prime}_{0}=O(\|\Delta g\|^{2})
. Thus,
l^{\prime\prime}_{e}-l^{\prime}_{0}=O(\|\Delta g\|^{2\mu^{\prime}}).
Since
l^{\prime\prime}_{e}\geq l_{e}\geq l^{\prime}_{0}
, we obtain
l_{e}-l^{\prime}_{0}=O(\|\Delta g\|^{2\mu^{\prime}})
, and, finally,

l_{e}-l^{\prime}_{e}=O(\|\Delta g\|^{2\mu^{\prime}}).

For
\alpha=0
, similarly to the proofs of Lemmas
3.36
,
3.37
we can obtain that
\eta_{1}
,
\eta_{2}
are
O(\|\Delta g\|)
. The remaining part is identical to the argument above.
∎

Now we start to prove Lemmas
3.38
–
3.39
. To this purpose we introduce an auxiliary surface. Since now we assume
\alpha\neq 0
. Let
\Gamma^{*}<{\rm Iso}^{+}(\mathbb{H}^{3})
be the smooth 1-parametric subgroup consisting of isometries with the oriented shift
t\in\mathbb{R}
along
\psi
in the positive direction and rotating by
\frac{\alpha t}{a}
clockwise. Note that
\Gamma<\Gamma^{*}
. By
\tau^{j}_{i}
we denote the
\Gamma^{*}
-orbit of
v^{j}_{i}
, which is a smooth curve, by
M^{*}(g)
we denote the closed convex hull of all
\tau^{j}_{i}
. We have
M(g)\subset M^{*}(g)
.

Lemma 3.43
.

If
g
is sufficiently close to
g_{0}
, then the boundary
\partial M^{*}(g)
contains a curve
\tau^{1}_{i_{1}}
for some
i_{1}
and a curve
\tau^{2}_{i_{2}}
for some
i_{2}
.

Proof.

Clearly
\partial M^{*}(g)
must contain at least one curve
\tau^{j}_{i}
. Suppose that there exists a sequence
g_{k}
converging to
g
in
\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
such that
\partial M^{*}(g)
does not contain curves of the type
\tau^{2}_{i}
. Similarly to Corollary
3.27
, one can see that
\partial M^{*}(g_{k})
must converge in the Hausdorff sense to
\partial M^{*}(g)
. However, it is easy to see that
\overline{\rm conv}(\{\tau^{1}_{i}\})
converge in the Hausdorff sense to a subset of
\Pi^{1}
bounded by
\psi
and by a curve
\tau^{1}_{i_{0}}\subset\Pi^{1}
at the greatest distance from
\psi
in
g_{0}
.
∎

The union of the curves
\tau^{j}_{i}
belonging to
\partial M^{*}(g)
divide
\partial M^{*}(g)
into strips. When
g
is sufficiently close to
g_{0}
, there are exactly two strips bounded by a curve
\tau^{1}_{i_{1}}
for some
i_{1}
and a curve
\tau^{2}_{i_{2}}
for some
i_{2}
. We choose the one, for which the curve
\tau^{2}_{i_{2}}
is clockwise with respect to the curve
\tau^{1}_{i_{1}}
. We denote this strip by
\Sigma^{*}
. From now on we denote the boundary curves of
\Sigma^{*}
just by
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
. Similarly, we denote
v^{1}_{i_{1}}
,
v^{2}_{i_{2}}
,
x^{1}_{i_{1}}
,
x^{2}_{i_{2}}
,
y^{1}_{i_{1}}
,
y^{2}_{i_{2}}
,
\phi^{1}_{i_{1}}
,
\phi^{2}_{i_{2}}
just by
v_{1}
,
v_{2}
,
x_{1}
,
x_{2}
,
y_{1}
,
y_{2}
,
\phi_{1}
,
\phi_{2}
. Define
y:=y_{2}-y_{1}
,
\phi:=\phi_{2}-\phi_{1}
.

The surface
\Sigma^{*}
is invariant with respect to
\Gamma^{*}
. Each point
p\in\Sigma^{*}\backslash(\tau_{1}\cup\tau_{2})
either belongs to a segment contained in
\Sigma^{*}
with vertices on
\tau_{1}
,
\tau_{2}
, or belongs to a triangle contained in
\Sigma^{*}
with vertices on
\tau_{1}
,
\tau_{2}
. As there is no plane invariant with respect to
\Gamma^{*}
, the second option is not possible. Thus,
\Sigma^{*}
is a ruled surface decomposing into the union of segments so that the decomposition is
\Gamma^{*}
-invariant.

The main step is to prove analogues of Lemmas
3.38
and
3.39
for the surface
\Sigma^{*}
.

Lemma 3.44
.

Let
\phi_{0}>\pi
. For every
0<\mu<1
and every
T>0
we have

\sup\{d_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(p,\psi):p\in\Sigma^{*},-T\leq t_{p}\leq T,\max_{1\leq i\leq n_{1}}\phi^{1}_{i}+|\alpha|^{\mu}\leq\theta_{p}\leq\min_{1\leq i\leq n_{2}}\phi^{2}_{i}-|\alpha|^{\mu}\}=O(\|\Delta g\|^{2}).

Lemma 3.45
.

For every
0<\mu<1
and every
T>0
we have

\sup\{d_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(p,\Pi^{1}_{1}):p\in\Sigma^{*},-T\leq t_{p}\leq T,\max_{1\leq i\leq n_{1}}\phi^{1}_{i}+|\alpha|^{\mu}\leq\theta_{p}\leq\frac{\pi}{2}\}=O(\|\Delta g\|^{\mu}),

\sup\{d_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(p,\Pi^{2}_{1}):p\in\Sigma^{*},-T\leq t_{p}\leq T,\phi^{2}_{1}-\frac{\pi}{2}\leq\theta_{p}\leq\min_{1\leq i\leq n_{2}}\phi^{2}_{i}-|\alpha|^{\mu}\}=O(\|\Delta g\|^{\mu}).

Note that there is a slight difference in the bounds on
\theta_{p}
between Lemma
3.39
and Lemma
3.45
. This is done on purpose to simplify our argument.
Since
M(g)\subset M^{*}(g)
, Lemmas
3.38
and
3.39
follow from Lemmas
3.44
and
3.45
quite easily:

Proof of Lemma
3.38
.

Note that if
p\in\tau^{j}_{i}
, then

\theta_{p}=\phi^{j}_{i}+\frac{\alpha}{a}(t_{p}-y^{j}_{i}).

Thus, for every
T>0
we have

\sup\{\theta_{p}:p\in\tau^{1}_{i},1\leq i\leq n_{1},-T\leq t_{p}\leq T\}\leq\max_{1\leq i\leq n_{1}}\phi^{1}_{i}+O(|\alpha|),

\inf\{\theta_{p}:p\in\tau^{2}_{i},1\leq i\leq n_{2},-T\leq t_{p}\leq T\}\geq\min_{1\leq i\leq n_{2}}\phi^{2}_{i}+O(|\alpha|).

Let
p\in\Sigma
and
p^{*}\in\partial M^{*}(g)
be the point with the same coordinates
(\theta_{p},t_{p})
.
Due to the bounds above, for sufficiently small
\alpha
, if
p\in\Sigma
,
-T\leq t_{p}\leq T
and

\max_{1\leq i\leq n_{1}}\phi^{1}_{i}+|\alpha|^{\mu}\leq\theta_{p}\leq\min_{1\leq i\leq n_{2}}\phi^{2}_{i}-|\alpha|^{\mu},

then
p^{*}\in\Sigma^{*}
. Thus, Lemma
3.38
follows from Lemma
3.44
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.39
.

As in the proof of Lemma
3.38
, for
p\in\Sigma
let
p^{*}\in\partial M^{*}(g)
be the point with the same coordinates
(\theta_{p},t_{p})
. For sufficiently small
\alpha
, if
p\in\Sigma
,
-T\leq t_{p}\leq T
and

\max_{1\leq i\leq n_{1}}\phi^{1}_{i}+|\alpha|^{\mu}\leq\theta_{p}\leq\min_{1\leq i\leq n_{2}}\phi^{2}_{i}-|\alpha|^{\mu},

then
p^{*}\in\Sigma^{*}
, and for such
p
Lemma
3.39
follows from Lemma
3.45
.

But for
p\in\Sigma
satisfying
0\leq\theta_{p}\leq\max_{1\leq i\leq n_{1}}\phi^{1}_{i}+|\alpha|^{\mu}
we have

d_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(p,\Pi^{1}_{1})\leq\sinh(d_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(p,\Pi^{1}_{1}))=\sin(\theta_{p})\sinh(d_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(p,\psi))\leq\theta_{p}\sinh(\max(x^{j}_{i}))=O(\|\Delta g\|^{\mu}).

Similarly for
p
such that
\min_{1\leq i\leq n_{2}}\phi^{2}_{i}-|\alpha|^{\mu}\leq\theta_{p}\leq\phi^{2}_{1}
we have

d_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(p,\Pi^{2}_{1})\leq\sinh(d_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(p,\Pi^{2}_{1}))=\sin(\phi^{2}_{1}-\theta_{p})\sinh(d_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(p,\psi))\leq

\leq(\phi^{2}_{1}-\theta_{p})\sinh(\max(x^{j}_{i}))=O(\|\Delta g\|^{\mu}).

This finishes the proof.
∎

Now we are going to prove Lemmas
3.44
–
3.45
. We will need the following preliminaries

Lemma 3.46
.

Let
p_{1}p_{2}q_{1}q_{2}
be a hyperbolic quadrilateral, possibly self-intersecting, such that
{\angle}p_{1}q_{1}q_{2}={\angle}p_{2}q_{2}q_{1}=\pi/2
. Consider a point
p\in p_{1}p_{2}
, let
q\in q_{1}q_{2}
be its orthogonal projection to the line
q_{1}q_{2}
. Then

pq\leq 2\max_{j=1,2}\{p_{j}q_{j}\exp(-qq_{j})\}.

Proof.

Let
q^{\prime}
be the projective intersection point of the lines
p_{1}p_{2}
and
q_{1}q_{2}
, possibly ideal or hyperideal. Assume that
q
lies between
q^{\prime}
and
q_{1}
. We will show that

pq\leq 2p_{1}q_{1}\exp(-qq_{1}).

If
q^{\prime}
is not hyperideal, we can rotate the line
p_{1}p_{2}
around the point
p_{1}
until we make
q^{\prime}
hyperideal so that
pq
increases and
q
stays between
q^{\prime}
and
q_{1}
. Here we mean that the point
q
stays fixed and the point
p
is defined as the intersection point of the new line with the perpendicular to
q_{1}q_{2}
at
q
. Hence, we can assume that
q^{\prime}
is hyperideal, so the lines
p_{1}p_{2}
and
q_{1}q_{2}
are ultra-parallel. Let
p_{0}
,
q_{0}
be the respective closest points on these lines. By construction, the point
q
lies between
q_{0}
and
q_{1}
. From Lemmas
2.2
and
2.3
we get

\tanh(p_{0}q_{0})=\frac{\tanh(p_{1}q_{1})}{\cosh(q_{0}q_{1})}.

And also

\tanh(pq)=\tanh(p_{0}q_{0})\cosh(qq_{0})=\frac{\tanh(p_{1}q_{1})\cosh(qq_{0})}{\cosh(q_{0}q_{1})}=

=\tanh(p_{1}q_{1})\exp(-qq_{1})\frac{1+\exp(-2qq_{0})}{1+\exp(-2q_{0}q_{1})}\leq 2\tanh(p_{1}q_{1})\exp(-qq_{1}).

It remains to note that
pq\leq p_{1}q_{1}
, thus
pq\leq\frac{p_{1}q_{1}}{\tanh(p_{1}q_{1})}\tanh(pq).

∎

Lemma 3.47
.

Let
\alpha\neq 0
, let
(\phi-\pi)a-\alpha y>0
and let
p_{1}p_{2}
be a generatrix of
\Sigma^{*}
, where
p_{1}\in\tau_{1}
,
p_{2}\in\tau_{2}
. Then
\sgn(t_{p_{1}}-t_{p_{2}})=\sgn(\alpha)
,
0<\theta_{p_{2}}-\theta_{p_{1}}<\pi
and

\left|\frac{t_{p_{1}}-t_{p_{2}}}{\theta_{p_{2}}-\theta_{p_{1}}}\right|\geq\left|\frac{(\phi-\pi)a-\alpha y}{\alpha\pi}\right|.

Proof.

It is clear that
-\pi<\theta_{p_{2}}-\theta_{p_{1}}<\pi
. Let
p^{\prime}_{1}\in\tau_{1}
be a point with
\theta_{p^{\prime}_{1}}=\theta_{p_{1}}-\pi
. Then we have
\sgn(t_{p^{\prime}_{1}}-t_{p_{2}})=\sgn(\alpha)
. Hence, the same holds for
\sgn(t_{p_{1}}-t_{p_{2}})
.

We now show that
\theta_{p_{2}}>\theta_{p_{1}}
. Consider the equidistant surfaces from
\psi
. The induced intrinsic metrics on such surfaces are locally Euclidean. Consider now the supporting plane
\Pi
to
\Sigma^{*}
containing the generatrix
p_{1}p_{2}
. Its intersection with each equidistant surface to
\psi
is an ellipse in the intrinsic metric of this surface. In
\Pi
all such intersections are concentric figures around a point
p_{0}\in\Pi
, which is the closest point from
\Pi
to
\psi
. For each such ellipse we call its “left side” all the points
p
with
\theta_{p}<\theta_{p_{0}}
and its “right side” the set of points with
\theta_{p}>\theta_{p_{0}}
. It is easy to see that by the choice of
\Sigma^{*}
the curve
\tau_{1}
is tangent to the corresponding ellipse in its left side, and
\tau_{2}
tangents the corresponding ellipse in its right side. Hence,
\theta_{p_{1}}>\theta_{p_{0}}>\theta_{p_{2}}
.

Due to the
\Gamma^{*}
-invariance of
\Sigma^{*}
, we can assume that
p_{2}=v_{2}
, so
t_{p_{2}}=y_{2}
and
\theta_{p_{2}}=\phi_{2}
. As above, let
p^{\prime}_{1}\in\tau_{1}
be the point with
\theta_{p_{2}}-\theta_{p_{1}^{\prime}}=\pi
. We get
\theta_{p^{\prime}_{1}}=\phi_{2}-\pi
. Hence,
t_{p_{1}^{\prime}}-t_{p_{2}}=\frac{(\phi-\pi)a}{\alpha}-y
. The chord
p^{\prime}_{1}p_{2}
is tangent to
\psi
, and in order for the chord
p_{1}p_{2}
to be in the right location with respect to
\psi
, the following bound must hold

\left|\frac{t_{p_{1}}-t_{p_{2}}}{\theta_{p_{2}}-\theta_{p_{1}}}\right|\geq\left|\frac{t_{p^{\prime}_{1}}-t_{p_{2}}}{\theta_{p_{2}}-\theta_{p^{\prime}_{1}}}\right|.

Our last claim follows.
∎

Lemma 3.48
.

Let
\alpha\neq 0
, let
(\phi-\pi)a-\alpha y>0
and let
p\in\Sigma^{*}
be such that

-T\leq t_{p}\leq T
,
p_{1}\in\tau_{1}
,
p_{2}\in\tau_{2}
be the endpoints of the generatrix containing
p
. Then there are constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0,

c_{3},c_{4}\geq 0
such that in a neighborhood of
g_{0}
we have

|t_{p_{1}}-t_{p}|\geq\frac{c_{1}(\theta_{p}-\phi_{1})}{|\alpha|}-c_{2},

|t_{p}-t_{p_{2}}|\geq\frac{c_{3}(\phi_{2}-\theta_{p})}{|\alpha|}-c_{4}.

Proof.

We have
\theta_{p_{1}}=\phi_{1}+\frac{\alpha(t_{p_{1}}-y_{1})}{a}
. On the other hand, from Lemma
3.47
we get

|t_{p_{1}}-t_{p}|\geq(\theta_{p}-\theta_{p_{1}})\frac{(\phi-\pi)a-\alpha y}{\pi|\alpha|}.

Substituting the first into the second and rearranging we get

|t_{p_{1}}-t_{p}|\frac{\phi a-\alpha y}{\pi a}\geq(\theta_{p}-\phi_{1})\frac{(\phi-\pi)a-\alpha y}{\pi|\alpha|}-\sgn(\alpha)(t_{p}-y_{1})\frac{(\phi-\pi)a-\alpha y}{\pi a},

from which we obtain

|t_{p_{1}}-t_{p}|\geq\frac{a(\theta_{p}-\phi_{1})}{|\alpha|}\cdot\frac{(\phi-\pi)a-\alpha y}{\phi a-\alpha y}-(T+y_{1})\cdot\frac{(\phi-\pi)a-\alpha y}{\phi a-\alpha y}.

The first claim follows.

Similarly we have

\theta_{p_{2}}=\phi_{2}+\frac{\alpha(t_{p_{2}}-y_{2})}{a}

and from Lemma
3.47

|t_{p}-t_{p_{2}}|\geq(\theta_{p_{2}}-\theta_{p})\frac{(\phi-\pi)a-\alpha y}{\pi|\alpha|}.

From this we get

|t_{p}-t_{p_{2}}|\frac{\phi a-\alpha y}{\pi a}\geq(\phi_{2}-\theta_{p})\frac{(\phi-\pi)a-\alpha y}{\pi|\alpha|}+\sgn(\alpha)(t_{p}-y_{2})\frac{(\phi-\pi)a-\alpha y}{\pi a}

and in turn

|t_{p}-t_{p_{2}}|\geq\frac{a(\phi_{2}-\theta_{p})}{|\alpha|}\cdot\frac{(\phi-\pi)a-\alpha y}{\phi a-\alpha y}-(T+y_{2})\cdot\frac{(\phi-\pi)a-\alpha y}{\phi a-\alpha y}.

The second claim follows.
∎

Now we can prove Lemma
3.44
.

Proof of Lemma
3.44
.

We may assume that
\|\Delta g\|
is small enough so that
\phi>\pi
and

(\phi-\pi)a-\alpha y>0.

Let
p
be a point satisfying the conditions of the lemma. It belongs to a generatrix of
\Sigma^{*}
, with endpoints
p_{1}\in\tau_{1}
,
p_{2}\in\tau_{2}
. Let
q_{1},q_{2},q
be the orthogonal projections of
p_{1},p_{2},p
to
\psi
. Due to the restrictions on
\theta_{p}
, from Lemma
3.48
we get that in a neighborhood of
g_{0}
, there exist constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that

q_{1}q=|t_{p_{1}}-t_{p}|\geq\frac{c_{1}}{|\alpha|^{1-\mu}},

qq_{2}=|t_{p}-t_{p_{2}}|\geq\frac{c_{2}}{|\alpha|^{1-\mu}}.

Consider the tetrahedron
p_{1}p_{2}q_{2}q_{1}
. Rotate the segment
p_{2}q_{2}
in the plane orthogonal to
q_{1}q_{2}
. We keep
q
fixed and by
p
we mean the point at
p_{1}p_{2}
such that its orthogonal projection to
q_{1}q_{2}
is
q
. We see that the length
pq
maximizes when the tetrahedron degenerates to a planar trapezoid. Indeed, the trajectory of
p
is a circle in the plane orthogonal to
q_{1}q_{2}
at
q
centered at the intersection of this plane with the line
p_{1}q_{2}
. The farthest point from this circle to
q
corresponds exactly to the situation when
pq
becomes coplanar to
p_{1}q_{2}
and lying on the same side of
\psi
.

Let
p^{\prime}_{1}p^{\prime}_{2}q^{\prime}_{2}q^{\prime}_{1}
be the trapezoid with
p^{\prime}_{1}q^{\prime}_{1}=p_{1}q_{1}
,
p^{\prime}_{2}q^{\prime}_{2}=p_{2}q_{2}
and
q_{1}q_{2}=q^{\prime}_{1}q^{\prime}_{2}
,
p^{\prime}
be the point at the segment
p^{\prime}_{1}p^{\prime}_{2}
with the orthogonal projection
q
to the line
q^{\prime}_{1}q^{\prime}_{2}
such that
q^{\prime}q^{\prime}_{1}=qq_{1}
,
q^{\prime}q^{\prime}_{2}=qq_{2}
. From the argument above we have
pq\leq p^{\prime}q^{\prime}
. Applying Lemma
3.46
we get

p^{\prime}q^{\prime}\leq 2\max_{j=1,2}\left\{x_{j}\exp\left(-\frac{c_{j}}{|\alpha|^{1-\mu}}\right)\right\}\leq 2\max_{j=1,2}\left\{\frac{x_{j}}{c_{j}^{\frac{2}{1-\mu}}}\right\}|\alpha|^{2}=O(\|\Delta g\|^{2}).

∎

Proof of Lemma
3.45
.

Consider the first claim of the lemma. Let
p
be a point satisfying the conditions of the claim. We treat separately the cases
\phi_{0}>\pi
and
\phi_{0}=\pi
. Suppose that
\phi_{0}>\pi
.
We may assume that
\|\Delta g\|
is small enough so that
\phi>\pi
and

(\phi-\pi)a-\alpha y>0.

We have

d_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(p,\Pi^{1}_{1})\leq\sinh(d_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(p,\Pi^{1}_{1}))=\sin(\theta_{p})\sinh(d_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(p,\psi))\leq

\leq\max_{j=1,2}\left\{\frac{\sinh(x_{j})}{x_{j}}\right\}\theta_{p}d_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(p,\psi).

The point
p
belongs to a generatrix of
\Sigma^{*}
, with endpoints
p_{1}\in\tau_{1}
,
p_{2}\in\tau_{2}
. Let
q_{1},q_{2},q
be the orthogonal projections of
p_{1},p_{2},p
to
\psi
. From Lemma
3.48
we get that in a neighborhood of
g_{0}
there exist constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that

q_{1}q=|t_{p_{1}}-t_{p}|\geq\frac{c_{1}}{|\alpha|^{1-\mu}},

qq_{2}=|t_{p}-t_{p_{2}}|\geq\frac{c_{2}}{|\alpha|^{1-\mu}}.

Thus, similarly to the proof of Lemma
3.44
, from Lemma
3.40
in this case we can bound

d_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(p,\psi)=O(\|\Delta g\|^{2}),~~~~~d_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(p,\Pi^{1}_{1})=O(\|\Delta g\|^{2}).

Now we deal with the case
\phi_{0}=\pi
. Let
\Pi
be the plane containing the half-plane
\Pi^{1}_{1}
, and
r_{1}
,
r_{2}
,
r
be the orthogonal projections of
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
,
p
to
\Pi
. From Lemma
3.46
we get

d_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(p,\Pi)=pr\leq 2\max_{j=1,2}\{p_{j}r_{j}\exp(-rr_{j})\}.

(3.4)

Suppose that
rr_{j}\geq|\alpha|^{\mu-1}
. Then

p_{j}r_{j}\exp(-rr_{j})\leq x_{j}|\alpha|^{2}=O(|\alpha|^{2}).

Suppose that
rr_{j}\leq|\alpha|^{\mu-1}
. Then also
qq_{j}\leq|\alpha|^{\mu-1}
and

|t_{p_{j}}-y_{j}|\leq T+qq_{j}+|y_{j}|\leq T+|\alpha|^{\mu-1}+|y_{j}|\leq c|\alpha|^{\mu-1}

for some constant
c>1
. We have

\theta_{p_{j}}=\phi_{j}+\frac{\alpha(t_{p_{j}}-y_{j})}{a}.

In the case
j=1
we get

|\theta_{p_{1}}|\leq|\phi_{1}|+\frac{c|\alpha|^{\mu}}{a}=O(\|\Delta g\|^{\mu}),

p_{1}r_{1}=d_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(p_{1},\Pi)\leq\sinh(p_{1}r_{1})=\sin(|\theta_{p_{1}}|)\sinh(p_{1}q_{1})\leq

\leq|\theta_{p_{1}}|\sinh(x_{1})=O(\|\Delta g\|^{\mu}).

In the case
j=2
we get

|\theta_{p_{2}}-\pi|\leq|\phi_{2}-\pi|+\frac{c|\alpha|^{\mu}}{a}=O(\|\Delta g\|^{\mu}),

p_{2}r_{2}=d_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(p_{2},\Pi)\leq\sinh(p_{2}r_{2})=\sin(|\theta_{p_{2}}-\pi|)\sinh(p_{2}q_{2})\leq

\leq|\theta_{p_{2}}-\pi|\sinh(x_{2})=O(\|\Delta g\|^{\mu}).

In any case we see that

p_{j}r_{j}\exp(-qq_{j})=O(\|\Delta g\|^{\mu}).

Due to (
3.4
), this finishes the proof. The second claim of the lemma is obtained similarly.
∎

3.4.3
Differentiability for controllably polyhedral metrics

We use the results of the previous section to show

Lemma 3.49
.

Let
g\in\mathcal{P}_{cp}(M,V)
. Then
\mathcal{I}_{V}
is differentiable at
g
.

Proof.

We start as in the proof of Lemma
3.33
. Pick
g\in\mathcal{P}_{cp}(M,V)
, lift it to
(\rho,\widetilde{f})\in\mathcal{R}(\pi_{1}(M),G)\times(\mathbb{H}^{3})^{V}
, choose a neighborhood
\widetilde{U}
of
(\rho,\widetilde{f})
submersing smoothly onto a neighborhood
U
of
g
in
\mathcal{P}_{cp}(M,V)
and consider
\mathcal{I}_{V}
as a function over
\widetilde{U}
. Let
d
be the induced intrinsic metric on
\partial M(g)
.

Let
\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{r}
be all components of
\partial M(g)\cap\partial C(\overline{g})
. If
\psi_{i}
is an edge of
g
, then by
\Sigma_{i}
we denote the union of
\psi_{i}
and the two faces adjacent to
\psi_{i}
. If
\psi_{i}
is not an edge of
g
, then by
\Sigma_{i}
we denote the face containing
\psi_{i}
. Pick any triangulation
\mathcal{T}
realized in
(d,V)
such that (1) every edge of
g
that is not one of
\psi_{i}
is an edge of
\mathcal{T}
; (2) all
\Sigma_{i}
are subdivided so that all the interior edges of
\mathcal{T}
in
\Sigma_{i}
connect two different boundary components of
\Sigma_{i}
. Then if
U
is small enough, we get
\mathcal{I}_{V}(U)\subset\mathcal{D}_{c}(V,\mathcal{T})
.

The lengths of the edges of
\mathcal{T}
that are edges of
g
are smooth functions around
g
. For each face of
g
that is a convex hyperbolic polygon,
\mathcal{T}
subdivides it, and the lengths of the edges of
\mathcal{T}
in the interior of the faces are
C^{1}
-functions around
g
by the same argument as in Lemma
3.33
. The only remaining edges are the edges of
\mathcal{T}
belonging to interiors of
\Sigma_{i}
. For every such edge Corollary
3.35
shows that its length is a differentiable function at
g
. This finishes the proof.
∎

3.4.4
Differentiability for convex cores

Let
V=\emptyset
, so for each
g\in\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
the set
M(g)
coincides with
C(\overline{g})
. The set
\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
is identified with a subset of
\mathcal{CH}(N)
corresponding to non-Fuchsian metrics on
N
(recall that it is the whole
\mathcal{CH}(N)
except the case when
M
is an interval bundle over a surface). The map
\mathcal{I}_{V}
coincides with a map
\mathcal{I}
defined in the introduction, which naturally extends to the whole
\mathcal{CH}(N)
when there are Fuchsian metrics.
It was shown by Bonahon in
[
14
]

Lemma 3.50
.

The map
\mathcal{I}
is continuously differentiable over
\mathcal{CH}(N)
.

We will need some additional knowledge on the differential of
\mathcal{I}
.
We recall from the introduction that there is another map
\mathcal{I}^{*}:\mathcal{CH}(N)\rightarrow\mathcal{ML}(\partial M)
sending a convex cocompact metric on
N
to the bending lamination of its convex core. The space
\mathcal{ML}(\partial M)
does not carry a natural smooth structure, but carries a PL-structure. In
[
14
]
Bonahon proposed a natural relaxation of the notion of differentiability for PL-structures. Let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a domain and
f:U\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{k}
be a map.
The tangent map
to
f
at
x\in U
, which we denote
df_{x}
, is a map
\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{k}
such that for every continuous curve
\tau:[0,\varepsilon]\rightarrow U
with
\tau(0)=x
, differentiable at
0
with
\tau^{\prime}(0)=v
, the curve
f\circ\tau
is differentiable at zero and
df_{x}(v)=(f\circ\tau)^{\prime}(0)
. We say that
f
is
tangentiable
at
x
if it admits a tangent map at
x
, and
f
is tangetiable over
U
if it is tangentiable at every
x\in U
.

By the same construction as for differential manifolds, PL-manifolds admit tangent spaces at each point, which, however, lack the addition structure on the tangent vectors, and allow only the multiplication by non-negative scalars. For an
n
-dimensional PL-manifold, the tangent space at a point is isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
endowed with such a structure. One can naturally speak about tangentiable maps between PL-manifolds. For a bit more information we refer to
[
14
, Section 1]
. Bonahon proved in
[
14
]
that the map
\mathcal{I}^{*}
is tangentiable.

Let us recall from Section
2.4
that
\mathcal{CH}(N)
can be thought as a covering space over a smooth open subset of the character space
\mathcal{X}(\pi_{1}(M),G)
, where
G={\rm Iso}^{+}(\mathbb{H}^{3})
. We need to slightly expand this construction. Let
S_{i}
,
{i=1\ldots r}
, be the components of
\partial M
. Define
\mathcal{R}(\pi_{1}(S_{i}),G)
and
\mathcal{X}(\pi_{1}(S_{i}),G)
as in Section
2.4
and denote
\prod_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{X}(\pi_{1}(S_{i}),G)
by
\mathcal{X}(\partial M,G)
. Let
[\rho_{i}]\in\mathcal{X}(\pi_{1}(S_{i}),G)
be the characters induced by a representation
\rho_{\overline{g}}\in\mathcal{R}(\pi_{1}(M),G)
for
\overline{g}\in\mathcal{CH}(N)
. It is known that even when the boundary of
M
is compressible,
[\rho_{i}]
belongs to the smooth part of
\mathcal{X}(\pi_{1}(S_{i}),G)
, which is locally a complex manifold of complex dimension
-3\chi(S_{i})
, see, e.g.,
[
42
]
. Hence, also
\mathcal{X}(\partial M,G)
is locally a complex manifold around the induced characters, and the smooth part of
\mathcal{X}(\pi_{1}(M),G)
is immersed to
\mathcal{X}(\partial M,G)
as a complex submanifold of half dimension.

Thurston proposed a well-known parametrization of the space of complex projective structures, which defines a local homeomorphism

\mathcal{J}:\mathcal{T}(\partial M)\times\mathcal{ML}(\partial M)\rightarrow\mathcal{X}(\partial M,G),

see details in
[
47
]
. The points of
\mathcal{X}(\partial M,G)
coming from convex cocompact metrics on
M
are in the image of
\mathcal{J}
, and
\mathcal{I}\times\mathcal{I}^{*}
coincides with a local inverse of
\mathcal{J}
on such points. Bonahon showed in
[
14
]
that
\mathcal{J}
is tangentiable and that its tangent map is an isomorphism of the tangent spaces at each point. Moreover, he proposed a convenient description of the tangent map.

We need to introduce few definitions from another paper
[
13
]
of Bonahon. An
(abstract) pleated surface
with topological type
S
(for a closed connected surface
S
) is a pair
(\widetilde{\iota},\rho)
, where
\widetilde{\iota}:\widetilde{S}\rightarrow\mathbb{H}^{3}
is a map from the universal cover
\widetilde{S}
of
S
and
\rho:\pi_{1}(S)\rightarrow G
is a homomorphism, such that

(1) for every
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(S)
we have
\widetilde{\iota}\circ\gamma=\rho(\gamma)\circ\widetilde{\iota}
;

(2) the pull-back of the intrinsic metric on
\widetilde{\iota}(\widetilde{S})
induces a hyperbolic metric
d
on
S
;

(3) there exists a geodesic lamination
\lambda
in metric
d
such that
\widetilde{\iota}
sends each edge of its preimage
\widetilde{\lambda}
in
\widetilde{S}
to a geodesic in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
and
\widetilde{\iota}
is totally geodesic on the complement to
\widetilde{\lambda}
.

This notion is different from the common notion of a pleating surface in that the holonomy representation
\rho
is not required to be faithful or to act freely or properly discontinuously on
\mathbb{H}^{3}
.

Now let us step back and consider a geodesic lamination on a hyperbolic surface
(S,d)
. An
A
-valued transverse cocycle
for
\lambda
, where
A
is an abelian group, is an
A
-valued transverse finitely additive measure for
\lambda
. By
\mathcal{H}_{A}(S,\lambda)
we denote the space of transverse cocycles for
\lambda
. For
A=\mathbb{R}
this is a finite-dimensional vector space. In particular, measured laminations and tangent vectors to
\mathcal{ML}(\partial M)
with the supports contained in
\lambda
are
\mathbb{R}
-valued transverse cocycles for
\lambda
. (For the description of tangent vectors to
\mathcal{ML}(\partial M)
we refer to
[
12
,
70
]
.) In
[
13
]
Bonahon showed that one can associate to a pleated surface an
\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}
-valued transverse cocycle measuring the bending of the surface. Moreover, he described a converse process of constructing a pleated surface from a hyperbolic surface
(S,d)
together with an
\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}
-valued transverse cocycle for
\lambda
. This determines a map

\mathcal{T}(S)\times\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{R}}(S,\lambda)\rightarrow\mathcal{X}(S,G).

(Here we associate to
\mathbb{R}
-valued transverse cocycles their
\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}
-reductions.) The space
\mathcal{T}(S)\times\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{R}}(S,\lambda)
can be endowed with a complex structure such that
iT\mathcal{T}(S)=T\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{R}}(S,\lambda)
, where
T\mathcal{T}(S)
and
T\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{R}}(S,\lambda)
are considered as sub-bundles of the tangent bundle to
\mathcal{T}(S)\times\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{R}}(S,\lambda)
.
Bonahon showed in
[
13
]
that when
\lambda
is maximal, the map above is a local biholomorphic homeomorphism. We consider the product of these maps for different components of
\partial M

\mathcal{J}_{\lambda}:\mathcal{T}(\partial M)\times\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{R}}(\partial M,\lambda)\rightarrow\mathcal{X}(\partial M,G).

In
[
14
]
Bonahon proved

Lemma 3.51
.

Let
(d,\mu)\in\mathcal{T}(\partial M)\times\mathcal{ML}(\partial M)
,
\dot{d}\in T_{d}\mathcal{T}(\partial M)
,
\dot{\mu}\in T_{\mu}\mathcal{ML}(\partial M)
and let
\lambda
be a maximal geodesic lamination containing the supports of
\mu
and
\dot{\mu}
. Then

(d\mathcal{J})_{(d,\mu)}(\dot{d},\dot{\mu})=(d\mathcal{J}_{\lambda})_{(d,\mu)}(\dot{d},\dot{\mu}).

4
Infinitesimal rigidity

This section is devoted to a proof of the following infinitesimal rigidity result.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let either
g\in\mathcal{P}_{cp}(M,V)
or
V=\emptyset
and
g\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(M,V)
. Then
d\mathcal{I}_{V}
has the full rank at
g
.

We now show that it implies the corresponding local rigidity results

Corollary 4.2
.

Let
g\in{\mathcal{P}}_{cp}(M,V)
. Then
\mathcal{I}_{V}
is a local homeomorphism at
g
.

Proof.

The manifolds
\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
and
\mathcal{D}_{c}(\partial M,V)
have the same dimension
3(n-k)
and
\mathcal{P}_{cp}(M,V)
is an open subset of the former. Since
\mathcal{I}_{V}
is differentiable over
\mathcal{P}_{cp}(M,V)
and the differential is everywhere non-degenerate, by the inverse function theorem for differentiable maps (see, e.g.,
[
76
]
) we get that
\mathcal{I}_{V}
is locally injective. By the Brower invariance of the domain theorem,
\mathcal{I}_{V}
is a local homeomorphism around
g
.
∎

Corollary 4.3
.

Let
V=\emptyset
and
g\in{\mathcal{P}}_{p}(M,V)
. Then
\mathcal{I}_{V}
is a local
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism at
g
.

Proof.

The proof is almost the same, but here we use that
\mathcal{I}_{V}
is
C^{1}
over
\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
. Hence, it is non-degenerate in a neighborhood of
g
and we can use the standard inverse function theorem to make the desired conclusion.
∎

Particularly, we get the first claim of Part (1) of Corollary
C
. We can also deduce Part (2).

Corollary 4.4
.

Let
\overline{g}\in\mathcal{CH}(N)
have polyhedral convex core. Then the tangent map
d\mathcal{I}^{*}_{\overline{g}^{\prime}}
is an isomorphism for every
\overline{g}^{\prime}
sufficiently close to
\overline{g}
.

Proof.

Due to the invariance of domain, it is enough to show that
d\mathcal{I}^{*}_{\overline{g}^{\prime}}
is injective. We will rely only on that
d\mathcal{I}_{\overline{g}^{\prime}}
is non-degenerate, which is true in a neighborhood of
\overline{g}
since
\mathcal{I}
is
C^{1}
. Thus, it is enough to prove the lemma for
\overline{g}^{\prime}=\overline{g}
. In what follows we won’t be using the subscript
{}_{\overline{g}}
for tangent maps meaning that this is the case.

Denote
\mathcal{I}\times\mathcal{I}^{*}(\overline{g})
by
(d,\mu)
. First let us show that
d\mathcal{I}^{*}
is non-degenerate.
Suppose the converse, that there exists
\dot{\overline{g}}\neq 0
such that
d\mathcal{I}^{*}(\dot{\overline{g}})=0
.
Define

P:=d\mathcal{J}(T_{d}\mathcal{T}(\partial M)\times\{0\})\subset T_{\overline{g}}\mathcal{X}(\partial M,G).

Due to Lemma
3.51
,
P
is a linear subspace and
iP
is transversal to
P
. We have
\dot{\overline{g}}\in P
. Let
\mathcal{J}^{-1}
be a local inverse for
\mathcal{J}
sending
\overline{g}
to
(d,\mu)
. Then
d\mathcal{J}^{-1}(i\dot{\overline{g}})\in\{0\}\times T_{\mu}\mathcal{ML}(\partial M)
. As
\mathcal{X}(\pi_{1}(M),G)
is a complex submanifold of
\mathcal{X}(\partial M,G)
, we have
i\dot{\overline{g}}\in T_{\overline{g}}\mathcal{X}(\pi_{1}(M),G)
. We get
d\mathcal{I}(i\dot{\overline{g}})=0
, which contradicts to Corollary
4.3
.

Now suppose that for
\dot{\overline{g}}_{1},\dot{\overline{g}}_{2}\in T_{\overline{g}}\mathcal{CH}(N)
we have
d\mathcal{I}^{*}(\dot{\overline{g}}_{1})=d\mathcal{I}^{*}(\dot{\overline{g}}_{2})=\dot{\mu}
. Let
\lambda
be a maximal geodesic lamination containing the supports of
\mu
and
\dot{\mu}
, and let
\mathcal{J}_{\lambda}^{-1}
be a local inverse for the map
\mathcal{J}_{\lambda}
sending
\overline{g}
to
(d,\mu)
. Due to Lemma
3.51
, we have
d\mathcal{J}_{\lambda}^{-1}(\dot{\overline{g}}_{1})=(\dot{d}_{1},\dot{\mu})
,
d\mathcal{J}_{\lambda}^{-1}(\dot{\overline{g}}_{2})=(\dot{d}_{2},\dot{\mu})
for
\dot{d}_{1},\dot{d}_{2}\in T_{d}\mathcal{T}(\partial M)
. Hence,
d\mathcal{J}_{\lambda}^{-1}(\dot{\overline{g}}_{1}-\dot{\overline{g}}_{2})=(\dot{d}_{1}-\dot{d}_{2},0)
. Due to Lemma
3.51
, we get
d\mathcal{I}^{*}(\dot{\overline{g}}_{1}-\dot{\overline{g}}_{2})=0
, which contradicts to our first claim.
∎

In order to pass from local rigidity to global rigidity we will need the following

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
g_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
be such that
\mathcal{I}_{V}
is a local homeomorphism at
g_{0}
. Let
V\subseteq W
,
g\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,W)
be a lift of
g_{0}
and let
d:=\mathcal{I}_{W}(g)
. Then
g
is isolated in
\mathcal{I}_{W}^{-1}(d)
.

Proof.

If
V=W
, then the claim is straightforward, so suppose that
V\subsetneq W
. By
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,V,W)
we denote the subset of
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,W)
of metrics
g^{\prime}
with
V(g^{\prime})=V
, by
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{c}(M,V,W)
we denote the subset of
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{c}(M,W)
of metrics
d
with
V(d)=V
. Consider the commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,V,W)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{c}(M,V,W)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}}_{cp}(M,V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}_{cp}(M,V)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}_{W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}_{V}}

Since the bottom arrow is a local homeomorphism around
g_{0}
, which is the vertical image of
g
, and by definition
\mathcal{I}_{W}
is a homeomorphism when restricted to a fiber of the vertical map, it is easy to deduce that the restriction of
\mathcal{I}_{W}
to
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,V,W)
is locally injective. Because
\mathcal{I}_{W}^{-1}(d)\subset\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,V,W)
, the claim follows.
∎

4.1
Infinitesimal substitution

Let
g\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(M,V)
and
\Psi
be the set of all edges
\psi\subset\partial M(g)
that are simple closed geodesics (note that there are finitely many of them). We orient all these geodesics arbitrarily. Let
V^{\prime}
be a finite set in bijection with
\Psi
. Consider a smooth section
\sigma
of the bundle
\mathcal{CH}(N,V^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathcal{CH}(N)
such that for every
\overline{g}\in\mathcal{CH}(N)
and every
v\in V^{\prime}
the point marked by
v
in
N(\overline{g})
belongs to the closed geodesic homotopic to
\psi
, where
\psi
is the element of
\Psi
corresponding to
v
. It is not hard to see that such a section indeed can be chosen smooth since for every
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(M)
the axis fixed by
\rho(\gamma)
varies smoothly as
\rho
varies in
\mathcal{R}(\pi_{1}(M),G)
. By
W
we denote the union
V\cup V^{\prime}
.

By Lemmas
3.23
and
3.24
there exists a finite cover
M^{c}(g^{c})
of
M(g)
admitting a geodesic triangulation
\mathcal{T}
with vertices in
W^{c}
, where
W^{c}
is the preimage of
W
.
Taking a cover provides a smooth immersion of
\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
to
\mathcal{P}_{b}(M^{c},V^{c})
, and if we take
\dot{g}\in T_{g}\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
and consider the corresponding vector
\dot{g}^{c}\in T_{g^{c}}\mathcal{P}_{b}(M^{c},V^{c})
, then
d\mathcal{I}_{V}(\dot{g})=0
is equivalent to
d\mathcal{I}_{V^{c}}(\dot{g}^{c})=0
. Hence, if we show that
d\mathcal{I}_{V^{c}}(\dot{g}^{c})
implies
\dot{g}^{c}=0
, we also obtain that
d\mathcal{I}_{V}(\dot{g})
implies
\dot{g}=0
. We extend the section
\sigma
to a smooth section
\sigma^{c}
of
\mathcal{CH}(N^{c},W^{c})\rightarrow\mathcal{CH}(N^{c})
with the same property that every point marked by an element of
W^{c}\backslash V^{c}
belongs to the corresponding geodesic. Until the end of this section we will work with
M^{c}(g^{c})
, so to simplify the reading we now omit to write
c
and assume that
M(g)
has a large triangulation
\mathcal{T}
with vertices in
W
(now there can be several new vertices at each
\psi\in\Psi
, but we continue to denote by
V^{\prime}
the difference
W\backslash V
). We write
E(\mathcal{T})
for the set of edges of
\mathcal{T}
and divide them in two groups:
E_{in}(\mathcal{T})
are the interior edges and
E_{bd}(\mathcal{T})
are the boundary edges.

We take the direct product
\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)\times\mathbb{R}^{V^{\prime}}
and immerse it in
\mathcal{CH}(N,W)
as follows. Denote the elements of of
\mathbb{R}^{V^{\prime}}
by
z
and their components by
z_{v}
for
v\in V^{\prime}
. For
\overline{g}\in\mathcal{CH}(N)
denote
\sigma(\overline{g})\in\mathcal{CH}(N,V^{\prime})
by
[g_{\sigma(\overline{g})},f_{\sigma(\overline{g})}].
For every
([g,f],z)\in\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)\times\mathbb{R}^{V^{\prime}}
we map it to
[g^{\prime},f^{\prime}]\in\mathcal{CH}(N,W)
such that
[g^{\prime},f^{\prime}]
is a lift of
[g,f]
and for every
v\in V^{\prime}
the marked point
f^{\prime}(v)
is the point on the corresponding geodesic at oriented distance
t_{v}
from
f_{\sigma(\overline{g})}(v)
. We identify
\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)\times\mathbb{R}^{V^{\prime}}
with its image and write for simplicity its elements as
(g,z)
. For each
(g,z)\in\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)\times\mathbb{R}^{V^{\prime}}
and for each edge
e
of
\mathcal{T}
we consider the length of the unique geodesic segment in
M(g)
with the same endpoints as
e
and in the same homotopy class (with fixed endpoints). This defines a smooth map

l:\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)\times\mathbb{R}^{V^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{E(\mathcal{T})}.

We now return to our initial metric
g
and consider the differential
dl
of the above map at the point
(g,0)
.

Lemma 4.6
.

If there exists
\dot{z}\in T_{0}\mathbb{R}^{V^{\prime}}
such that
dl(\dot{g},\dot{z})=0
, then
\dot{g}=0
.

Proof.

It is easy to see that
dl(\dot{g},\dot{z})=0
implies that the induced deformation of
\overline{g}
is trivial. Indeed, pick arbitrary
v\in W
. Every element
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(M)
can be represented by a simplicial loop based at
v
in the 1-skeleton of
\mathcal{T}
. Pick a cyclic sequence of tetrahedra along this loop based on a tetrahedron
T
containing
v
so that any two subsequent tetrahedra have a 2-face in common, and develop this sequence in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
. Let
\widetilde{v}_{1},\widetilde{v}_{2}\in\mathbb{H}^{3}
be the lifts of
v
that are the endpoints of the lifted loop,
\widetilde{T}_{1}
and
\widetilde{T}_{2}
be the starting and the ending lifts of
T
so that
\widetilde{T}_{i}
contains
\widetilde{v}_{i}
. The image
\rho_{\overline{g}}(\gamma)
can be reconstructed by its action on any point in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
together with a basis in the tangent space of this point. Take
\widetilde{v}_{1}
and the basis given by the edges of
\widetilde{T}_{1}
. As
dl(\dot{g},\dot{z})=0
, we get that
\widetilde{v}_{2}
and
\widetilde{T}_{2}
infinitesimally do not move and so the induced change on
\rho_{\overline{g}}(\gamma)
is trivial.

Hence,
\dot{g}
is vertical with respect to the fibration
\mathcal{CH}(N,V)\rightarrow\mathcal{CH}(N)
. Thus, it represents an infinitesimal change of the locations of the points of
V
. Consider the universal cover
\widetilde{M}(g)\subset\mathbb{H}^{3}
. Then
\dot{g}
can be represented by a vector field on
\widetilde{V}=\widetilde{V}(g)
equivariant with respect to
\rho_{\overline{g}}
. We extend this vector field to the full preimage
\widetilde{W}
of
W
using
\dot{z}
. As
dl(\dot{g},\dot{z})=0
, it extends to a Killing field on each 2-face of
\mathcal{T}
belonging to the boundary of
\widetilde{M}(g)
such that the Killing fields coincide on the edges. Moreover, as
dl(\dot{g},\dot{z})=0
, this vector field preserves the link of each vertex, thus it is the same Killing field on every connected component of
\partial\widetilde{M}(g)
. Because it needs to be equivariant, it must be zero. Hence,
\dot{g}=0
.
∎

We now show

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
g\in\mathcal{P}_{cp}(M,V)
. If
d\mathcal{I}_{V}(\dot{g})=0
, then there exists
\dot{z}\in T_{0}\mathbb{R}^{V^{\prime}}
such that for each
e\in E_{bd}(\mathcal{T})
we have
dl_{e}(\dot{g},\dot{z})=0

Proof.

If both endpoints of
e
are in
V
and
e
is an edge of
g
, then the statement is clearly true for any
\dot{z}
. If
e
is not an edge and belongs to a simply connected face, the statement is again true for any
\dot{z}
, which follows from Claim
3.33.1
that the differential of the length of the realization of
e
in the intrinsic metric of the boundary coincides with the differential of the extrinsic distance between its endpoints.

Consider a simple closed geodesic
\psi\in\partial M(g)
, let
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(M)
be a generating element for a subgroup of
\pi_{1}(M)
corresponding to
\psi
, let
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
be two faces of
\partial M(g)
adjacent to
\psi
(possibly degenerating to one) and let
\Sigma
be their union. Develop the universal cover
\widetilde{\Sigma}
of
\Sigma
to
\mathbb{H}^{3}
and consider the coordinate system from Subsection
3.4.2
. By
\widetilde{\psi}
denote the development of
\psi
. Let
g_{t}
,
t\in[0,\varepsilon]
for some small
\varepsilon>0
, be a path tangent to
\dot{g}
. We consider a corresponding path of
\rho_{\overline{g}_{t}}(\gamma)
and the trajectories of the vertices adjacent to
\Sigma
and develop them in our coordinate system. By
V^{\prime}_{\psi}
denote the subset of
V^{\prime}
belonging to
\psi
. For each
v\in V^{\prime}_{\psi}
choose a lift
\widetilde{v}\in\widetilde{\psi}
and by
T_{\widetilde{\psi}}
denote the subspace of
\Pi_{v\in V^{\prime}_{\psi}}T_{\widetilde{v}}\mathbb{H}^{3}
consisting of tuples of tangent vectors parallel to
\widetilde{\psi}
. The section
\sigma
determines trajectories for points
\widetilde{v}
. Considering the derivatives at
t=0
we obtain an element of
T_{\widetilde{\psi}}
. It is easy to see that
T_{0}\mathbb{R}^{V^{\prime}_{\psi}}
is in one-to-one correspondence with
T_{\widetilde{\psi}}
(more exactly, there is a natural non-degenerate affine map between them). Let
\dot{z}_{\psi}\in T_{0}\mathbb{R}^{V^{\prime}_{\psi}}
be the preimage of the zero vector.

The deformation
\dot{g}
induces the deformation of the initial coordinates such that the deformation of the induced metric on
\Sigma
is zero. Corollary
3.35
shows that if we set all
\dot{\phi}^{j}_{i}=0
and
\dot{\alpha}=0
, then the obtained deformation of
\Sigma
is still zero. But this implies that all
\dot{x}^{j}_{i}=0
, all
\dot{y}^{j}_{i}=0
and
\dot{a}=0
. Together with zero vectors chosen at each
\widetilde{v}
, this induces zero infinitesimal deformation on every geodesic segment of
\mathcal{T}
in
\Sigma
. Let
\dot{z}\in T_{0}\mathbb{R}^{V^{\prime}}
be the vector obtained from gluing all
\dot{z}_{\psi}
. We have
dl_{e}(\dot{g},\dot{z})=0
for all
e\in E_{bd}(\mathcal{T})
.
∎

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
V=\emptyset
and
g\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(M,V)
. If
d\mathcal{I}_{V}(\dot{g})=0
, then there exists
\dot{z}\in T_{0}\mathbb{R}^{V^{\prime}}
such that for each
e\in E_{bd}(\mathcal{T})
we have
dl_{e}(\dot{g},\dot{z})=0
.

Proof.

As described in Section
3.4.4
, there is an immersion from
\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
to the smooth part of
\mathcal{X}(\partial M,G)
and we identify
g
with its image. Let
S_{i}
,
i=1\ldots r
, be the components of
\partial M
. By
V^{\prime}_{i}
we denote the subset of
V^{\prime}
corresponding to geodesics belonging to
S_{i}
in
M(g)
. As in Section
3.1.2
, we define the fibrations

\mathcal{X}(\pi_{1}(S_{i}),G,V^{\prime}_{i})\rightarrow\mathcal{X}(\pi_{1}(S_{i}),G)

and by
\mathcal{X}(\partial M,G,V^{\prime})
we denote the product
\prod_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{X}(\pi_{1}(S_{i}),G,V^{\prime}_{i})
, which fibers over
\mathcal{X}(\partial M,G)
. Let
U_{0}
be a small enough neighborhood of
g
in
\mathcal{X}(\partial M,G)
and
U^{\prime}_{0}
be its preimage in
\mathcal{X}(\partial M,G,V^{\prime})
. We consider a smooth section
\sigma^{\prime}
of the fibration
U^{\prime}_{0}\rightarrow U_{0}
extending the section
\sigma
such that again the marked points belong to the respective geodesics. We consider the product
U_{0}\times\mathbb{R}^{V^{\prime}}
and use
\sigma^{\prime}
to immerse it in
U^{\prime}_{0}
in the same way as above we immersed
\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)\times\mathbb{R}^{V^{\prime}}
in
\mathcal{CH}(N,W)
. We also define a map
l_{bd}:U_{0}\times\mathbb{R}^{V^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{E_{bd}(\mathcal{T})}
in the same way as we defined it above, though now we care only about the boundary edges of
\mathcal{T}
.

We now use the maps
\mathcal{J}
and
\mathcal{J}_{\lambda}
from Section
3.4.4
. Let
\mathcal{J}^{-1}
be a local inverse to
\mathcal{J}
defined on
U_{0}
, provided that
U_{0}
is sufficiently small, and coinciding with
\mathcal{I}\times\mathcal{I}^{*}
on
U_{0}\cap\mathcal{X}(\pi_{1}(M),G)
. Define

(d,\mu):=\mathcal{J}^{-1}(g)\in\mathcal{T}(\partial M)\times\mathcal{ML}(\partial M)
. As
d\mathcal{J}
at
(d,\mu)
is an isomorphism of the tangent spaces and
d\mathcal{I}_{V}(\dot{g})=0
, we have

d\mathcal{J}^{-1}(\dot{g})\in\{0\}\times T_{\mu}\mathcal{ML}(\partial M).

We are going to prove that for every
\dot{\mu}\in T_{\mu}\mathcal{ML}(\partial M)
there exists
\dot{z}\in T_{0}\mathbb{R}^{V^{\prime}}
such that
dl_{bd}(d\mathcal{J}(0,\dot{\mu}),\dot{z})=0
. This will imply the claim of the lemma.

We first suppose that the support of
\dot{\mu}
is rational. Let
\lambda
be a maximal rational geodesic lamination containing the supports of
\mu
and
\dot{\mu}
. Consider a local inverse
\mathcal{J}_{\lambda}^{-1}
to the map
\mathcal{J}_{\lambda}
defined over
U_{0}
and sending
g
to
(d,\mu)
.
We identify the vector space
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{R}}(\partial M,\lambda)
with its tangent space and consider a curve
\mu+t\dot{\mu}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{R}}(\partial M,\lambda)
for
t\in[0,\varepsilon]
and some
\varepsilon>0
.
Take a component
S_{i}
endowed with the hyperbolic metric
d
induced from
M(g)
, we consider it now as abstract hyperbolic surface triangulated by the restriction of
\mathcal{T}
to
S_{i}
.

Look at the pleated surfaces
(\widetilde{\iota}_{t},\rho_{t})
in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
obtained from
(S_{i},d)
by pairs
(d,\mu+t\dot{\mu})
. The starting surface is a boundary component of
\partial\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
. Let us fix a base point on it, together with a basis in the tangent space to the base point. We can choose
(\widetilde{\iota}_{t},\rho_{t})
so that they fix the base point and the chosen basis. Since
\lambda
is rational, it is easy to understand what happens with it: the surface starts to bend along the geodesics in
\widetilde{\iota}_{0}(\widetilde{\lambda})
, which is a discrete set, with the speeds of the bending angles prescribed by
\dot{\mu}
.

Pick an edge
e
of
\mathcal{T}
in
S_{i}
, let
\widetilde{e}
be its lift to
(\widetilde{S}_{i},d)
and
p_{0},\ldots,p_{s}
be the intersection points of
\widetilde{e}
with
\widetilde{\lambda}
including the endpoints. The curves
\widetilde{\iota}_{t}(p_{j})
are differentiable at zero. We claim that the derivative of the hyperbolic distance between
\widetilde{\iota}_{t}(p_{1})
and
\widetilde{\iota}_{t}(p_{s})
is zero at
t=0
. Indeed, let
x_{j}
be the tangent vector at
\widetilde{\iota}_{0}(p_{j})
to its trajectory, and
x^{k}_{j}
be the result of the parallel translation of
x_{j}
to
\widetilde{\iota}_{0}(p_{k})
along
\widetilde{\iota}_{0}(\widetilde{e})
with respect to the metric connection of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
. Since the length of each segment between
\widetilde{\iota}_{0}(p_{j})
and
\widetilde{\iota}_{0}(p_{j+1})
does not change infinitesimally, we have that
x^{j+1}_{j}-x_{j}
is orthogonal to
\widetilde{\iota}_{0}(\widetilde{e})
. Hence
x^{s}_{1}-x_{s}
is orthogonal to
\widetilde{\iota}_{0}(\widetilde{e})
and the claim follows.

The trajectories
\widetilde{\iota}_{t}(p_{1})
and
\widetilde{\iota}_{t}(p_{s})
determine tangent vectors
\dot{z}_{v_{1}},\dot{z}_{v_{2}}\in T_{0}\mathbb{R}^{V^{\prime}}
in the components corresponding to the endpoints
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
of
e
. The obtained vectors are independent on the choice of
\widetilde{e}
or
e
. By determining the tangent vector for every element of
V^{\prime}
we obtain a tangent vector
\dot{z}\in T_{0}\mathbb{R}^{V^{\prime}}
. For each edge
e\in E_{bd}(\mathcal{T})
the deformation
dl_{bd,e}(d\mathcal{J}_{\lambda}(0,\dot{\mu}),\dot{z})
coincides with the deformation of the distance between
\widetilde{\iota}_{0}(p_{1})
and
\widetilde{\iota}_{0}(p_{s})
in the construction above. Hence,
dl_{bd}(d\mathcal{J}_{\lambda}(0,\dot{\mu}),\dot{z})=0
. By Lemma
3.51
we get
dl_{bd}(d\mathcal{J}(0,\dot{\mu}),\dot{z})=0
as desired.

If the support of
\dot{\mu}
is irrational, we can approximate it by tangent vectors
\dot{\mu}_{j}
whose supports are rational. By the previous argument, for every
j
there exists
z_{j}\in T_{0}\mathbb{R}^{V^{\prime}}
such that
dl_{bd}(d\mathcal{J}(0,\dot{\mu}_{j}),\dot{z}_{j})=0
. This means that for every
\dot{z}\in T_{0}\mathbb{R}^{V^{\prime}}
the vector
dl_{bd}(d\mathcal{J}(0,\dot{\mu}_{j}),\dot{z})
belongs to the subspace
dl_{bd}(\{0\}\times T_{0}\mathbb{R}^{V^{\prime}})
. By continuity of the tangent map, the same is true for
dl_{bd}(d\mathcal{J}(0,\dot{\mu}),\dot{z})
. Hence, there exists
\dot{z}\in T_{0}\mathbb{R}^{V^{\prime}}
such that
dl_{bd}(d\mathcal{J}(0,\dot{\mu}),\dot{z})=0
. This finishes the proof.
∎

There is a neighborhood of
l=l(g,0)
in
\mathbb{R}^{E(\mathcal{T})}
where the edge lengths induce a hyperbolic cone-3-manifold structure on
M
triangulated with
\mathcal{T}
. Recall that for each edge
e\in E(\mathcal{T})
by
\nu_{e}
we denote the total dihedral angle of
e
. Clearly, every
\dot{g}\in T_{g}\mathcal{P}(M,V)
and
\dot{z}\in T_{0}\mathbb{R}^{V^{\prime}}
induce a deformation
\dot{l}=dl(\dot{g},\dot{z})
such that for every
e\in E_{in}(\mathcal{T})
the infinitesimal change
d\nu_{e}(\dot{l},\dot{z})=0
. Now our aim is to show

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
\dot{l}\in T_{l}\mathbb{R}^{E(\mathcal{T})}
be such that for each
e\in E_{bd}(\mathcal{T})
we have
\dot{l}_{e}=0
and for each
e\in E_{in}(\mathcal{T})
we have
d\nu_{e}(\dot{l})=0
. Then
\dot{l}=0
.

It is clear that the combination of Lemmas
4.6
–
4.9
imply Lemma
4.1
. We prove Lemma
4.9
in the next subsections.

4.2
Affine automorphic vector fields

Consider the Klein model of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
and denote by
AF
the set of affine vector fields restricted to
\mathbb{H}^{3}
. For every non-degenerate tetrahedron
T
with a vertex set
V(T)
there is a canonical isomorphism

J_{T}:\prod_{v\in V(T)}T_{v}\mathbb{H}^{3}\rightarrow AF,

mapping four tangent vectors at each vertex of
T
to the unique affine vector field coinciding with these vectors at
V(T)
. This isomorphism is preserved under isometries of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
.
Note that
AF
has dimension 12 and contains the 6-dimensional subspace
KF
of Killing fields.

We look at the action of
AF
on the edge lengths of
T
. Consider the 6-tuple of its edge lengths as an element
l\in\mathbb{R}^{E(T)}
. There is a natural linear map
AF\rightarrow T_{l}\mathbb{R}^{E(T)}
sending a vector field to the induced infinitesimal change of the edge lengths. As hyperbolic tetrahedra are infinitesimally rigid, the kernel of this map exactly equals to
KF
, hence the map is surjective and produces an isomorphism

T_{l}\mathbb{R}^{E(T)}\cong AF/KF.

(4.1)

Consider now a (possibly non-convex) polyhedron
P\subset\mathbb{H}^{3}
with a geodesic triangulation
\mathcal{T}
(without new vertices). Denote all the tetrahedra of
\mathcal{T}
by
T_{1},\ldots,T_{r}
. Pick an open cover of
P
by sets
U_{1},\ldots,U_{r}
such that for each
i
we have
T_{i}\subset U_{i}
and
U_{i}
does not contain vertices of
P
except the vertices of
T_{i}
. Choose a partition of unity over
P
with respect to
U_{i}
, i.e., smooth functions
\lambda_{i}:\mathbb{H}^{3}\rightarrow[0,1]
,
i=1,\ldots,r
, such that the support of
\lambda_{i}
is in
U_{i}
and for every
p\in P
we have
\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}(p)=1
. We denote the set of pairs
(U_{i},\lambda_{i})
by
\mathcal{U}
. By
AF(P,\mathcal{U})
we denote the space of vector fields of the form
\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}X_{i}
, where
X_{i}\in AF
and if
T_{i}
and
T_{j}
are two tetrahedra adjacent by a 2-face, then the restriction of
X_{i}
to the plane containing this face coincides with the restriction of
X_{j}
. We call such fields
affine fields on
P
with respect to
\mathcal{U}
. The meaning of this construction is that we construct a vector field on
P
by assigning an affine field
X_{i}
to each tetrahedron
T_{i}
so that the assigned fields coincide on the common faces. However, the resulting field will be continuous, but not smooth, so we smooth it out with the help of a partition of unity. We do it in a way that the values of the smoothed vector field does not change at the vertices of
P
.

If
V
is the set of vertices of
P
, there is a natural isomorphism

J_{P}:\prod_{v\in V}T_{v}\mathbb{H}^{3}\rightarrow AF(P,\mathcal{U})

defined by

J_{P}(Y)=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}J_{T_{i}}(Y|_{V(T_{i})}),

where
Y\in\prod_{v\in V}T_{v}\mathbb{H}^{3}
and
V(T_{i})
is the vertex set of
T_{i}
. Note that for every
v\in V
and
Y\in\prod_{v\in V}T_{v}\mathbb{H}^{3}
we have
J_{P}(Y)|_{v}=Y|_{v}
. There is the 6-dimensional subspace
KF(P,\mathcal{U})\subset AF(P,\mathcal{U})
consisting of the fields
\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}X_{i}
such that all
X_{i}
are equal to the same Killing field
X
, so the restriction of an element of
KF(P,\mathcal{U})
to
P
is exactly
X
.

Similarly to the consideration above, we regard the tuple of edge lengths of
\mathcal{T}
as a tuple
l\in\mathbb{R}^{E(\mathcal{T})}
and look at the natural map
AF(P,\mathcal{U})\rightarrow T_{l}\mathbb{R}^{E(\mathcal{T})}
.
Again, its kernel is equal
KF(P,\mathcal{U})
.
We now consider cone structures on
P
induced by deformations of
l
in
\mathbb{R}^{E(\mathcal{T})}
. Denote the set of the interior edges of
\mathcal{T}
by
E_{in}(\mathcal{T})
. For every
e\in E_{in}(\mathcal{T})
the total dihedral angle
\nu_{e}
is an analytic function in a neighborhood of
l
in
\mathbb{R}^{E(\mathcal{T})}
. We denote

T^{P}_{l}\mathbb{R}^{E(\mathcal{T})}=\left\{\bigcap_{e\in E_{in}(\mathcal{T})}\ker d\nu_{e}\right\}.

Clearly, the image of
AF(P,\mathcal{U})
belongs to
T^{P}_{l}\mathbb{R}^{E(\mathcal{T})}
. We now prove

Lemma 4.10
.

T^{P}_{l}\mathbb{R}^{E(\mathcal{T})}\cong AF(P,\mathcal{T})/KF(P,\mathcal{U}).

Proof.

We need to show that
T^{P}_{l}\mathbb{R}^{E(\mathcal{T})}
is the image of our map. Pick
\dot{l}\in T_{l}^{P}\mathbb{R}^{E(\mathcal{T})}
. Start from an arbitrary tetrahedron
T_{1}
of
\mathcal{T}
. As described above, there is always an affine field inducing the restriction of
\dot{l}
to
T_{1}
, we pick one of them. Now let
T_{2}
be a tetrahedron adjacent to
T_{1}
by a 2-face. We try to find a tangent vector at the fourth vertex
v
of
T_{2}
such that the induced infinitesimal lengths-change is the restriction of
\dot{l}
to
T_{2}
. Consider the hyperboloid model of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
in
\mathbb{R}^{3,1}
. Denote the other three vertices of
T_{2}
by
v_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,3
, and denote the edge from
v_{i}
to
v
by
e_{i}
. We have the system of equations

\langle\dot{v},v_{i}\rangle+\langle\dot{v}_{i},v\rangle=-\dot{l}_{e_{i}}\cosh l_{e_{i}},~~~~~i=1,\ldots,3.

Here we identify
v_{i}
,
v
with their radius-vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{3,1}
,
\dot{v}_{i}\in T_{v_{i}}\mathbb{H}^{3}
are the chosen tangent vectors to
v_{i}
and
\dot{v}
is a vector that we want to find in
T_{v}\mathbb{H}^{3}
. Suppose that there is no solution. Then from linearity there exists a non-trivial solution to the system

\langle\dot{v},v_{i}\rangle=0,~~~~~i=1,\ldots,3.

Due to the infinitesimal rigidity of tetrahedra, such a solution
\dot{v}\in T_{v}\mathbb{H}^{3}
together with the assignment of zero vectors in
T_{v_{i}}\mathbb{H}^{3}
is the restriction of a global Killing field of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
, but clearly such a non-zero Killing field does not exist.

We continue this procedure. It remains to check what happens when we close a cycle of tetrahedra around an interior edge
e
of
\mathcal{T}
. Let
T
be the last tetrahedron to close the chain and
e^{\prime}
be its last edge. The previous choices of tangent vectors at the vertices of
T
automatically induce an affine vector field on
T
, which induces an infinitesimal change of the length
l_{e^{\prime}}
. We need to see that it coincides with the one from
\dot{l}
. But this follows from
\frac{\partial\nu_{e}}{\partial l_{e^{\prime}}}\neq 0
, as this implies that for any assignment of infinitesimal lengths-changes to all edges of tetrahedra adjacent to
e
except
e^{\prime}
there exists a unique choice of
\dot{l}_{e^{\prime}}
such that
\dot{\nu}_{e}=0
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Now let
(K,\Sigma,\mathcal{T})
be a closed triangulated hyperbolic cone-3-manifold,
L
be its subset obtained by deleting from
K
the singular locus
\Sigma
. By
E_{0}(\mathcal{T})
denote the set of edges not belonging to
\Sigma
, so with
\nu_{e}=2\pi
. Let
T_{1},\ldots,T_{r}
be the tetrahedra of
\mathcal{T}
. We need to show that there exists some special open cover of
L
.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be large. There exists an open cover of
L
by sets
U_{1},\ldots,U_{r}
such that

•

each
U_{i}
is simply-connected;

•

U_{i}\supset(T_{i}\cap L)
;

•

the closure of each
U_{i}
in
K
does not contain vertices of
\mathcal{T}
except the vertices of
T_{i}
.

Proof.

Pick a tetrahedron
T_{i}
and develop it to
\mathbb{H}^{3}
, we denote the image by
\widehat{T}
and the set of its vertices by
\widehat{V}
. Let us call the edges of
\widehat{T}
coming from an edge in
E_{0}(\mathcal{T})

important
. For each important edge of
\widehat{T}
develop all tetrahedra adjacent to the preimage of this edge. For every 2-face of
\widehat{T}
not adjacent to important edges develop also the other tetrahedron adjacent to its preimage. Enumerate the obtained tetrahedra except
\widehat{T}
by
\widehat{T}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{T}_{s}
and denote their set by
\widehat{\mathcal{T}}
. Note that as
\mathcal{T}
is large, among the tetrahedra of
\widehat{\mathcal{T}}
there is no other copy of
T_{i}
, but some other tetrahedra of
\mathcal{T}
could be developed more than once. Denote the barycenter of
\widehat{T}_{j}
by
p_{j}
. Also for each 2-face of
\widehat{T}_{j}
that is not a 2-face of
\widehat{T}
, but contains an important edge, mark the barycenter of this face and denote the set of marked points by
\widehat{S}
. In each
\widehat{T}_{j}
by
\widehat{U}_{j}
we denote the interior of the convex hull of
p_{j}
,
\widehat{S}\cap\widehat{T}_{j}
and
\widehat{V}\cap T_{j}
. We are now constructing the set
\widehat{U}
as follows

•

add all
\widehat{U}_{j}
to
\widehat{U}
;

•

for each face
F
that is not a face of
\widehat{T}
, but is adjacent to an important edge, we add to
\widehat{U}
the relative interior of the triangle formed by
\widehat{S}\cap F
and
\widehat{V}\cap F
;

•

we add to
\widehat{U}
the relative interior of every important edge;

•

we add to
\widehat{U}
the interior of
\widehat{T}
.

By
U_{i}\subset L
we denote the preimage of the set
\widehat{U}
. It is not hard to see from the construction that
U_{i}
is simply-connected and open in
L
. Doing this for every
T_{i}
we obtain an open cover of
L
satisfying the desired properties.
∎

Take an open cover
U_{1},\ldots,U_{r}
of
L
from Lemma
4.11
and fix a partition of unity of
L
with respect to this cover. Denote the corresponding partition functions by
\lambda_{i}:L\rightarrow[0,1]
. We denote the set of pairs
(U_{i},\lambda_{i})
by
\mathcal{U}
, but we will also abuse the notation and sometimes will say just
U_{i}\in\mathcal{U}
. Consider a developing map
dev:\widetilde{L}\rightarrow\mathbb{H}^{3}
. Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}
be the partition of unity of
\widetilde{L}
that is the lift of
\mathcal{U}
. By
I
we denote a countable set indexing elements of
\mathcal{U}
. By
AF(\widetilde{L},\widetilde{\mathcal{U}})
we denote the space of vector fields on
\widetilde{L}
of the form
\sum_{i\in I}\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}X_{i}
where
\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}
are the partition functions and
X_{i}
are pull-backs on
\widetilde{L}
of affine vector fields on
\mathbb{H}^{3}
via
dev
such that for every two tetrahedra
\widetilde{T}_{i}
and
\widetilde{T}_{j}
in
\widetilde{L}
adjacent by a 2-face the fields
X_{i}
and
X_{j}
coincide on this face. We call such fields
affine vector fields on
\widetilde{L}
with respect to
\mathcal{U}
. It is easy to see that
AF(\widetilde{L},\widetilde{\mathcal{U}})
is independent on the choice of
dev
. By
KF(\widetilde{L})
we denote the space of the Killing fields on
\widetilde{L}
. Note that all vector fields in
KF(\widetilde{L})
are pull-backs of Killing fields on
\mathbb{H}^{3}
via
dev
and
KF(\widetilde{L})\subset AF(\widetilde{L},\mathcal{U})
as any such field can be represented as
\sum_{i\in I}\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}X_{i}
where all
X_{i}
are the same Killing field on
\widetilde{L}
.

As previously, we consider the tuple of edge lengths of
\mathcal{T}
as
l\in\mathbb{R}^{E(\mathcal{T})}
and by
T^{L}_{l}\mathbb{R}^{E(\mathcal{T})}
we denote the intersection of kernels of
d\nu_{e}
for all edges
e\in E_{0}(\mathcal{T})
. We want to establish an isomorphism of
T^{L}_{l}\mathbb{R}^{E(\mathcal{T})}
with a subspace of
AF(\widetilde{L},\widetilde{\mathcal{U}})
modulo
KF(\widetilde{L})
.
We need to understand which vector fields from
AF(\widetilde{L},\mathcal{U})
well-define variations of the edge lengths of
\mathcal{T}
. These are so-called
automorphic
ones.

This notion applies to a slightly more general context and is heavily connected with variations of a hyperbolic structure on manifolds.
For the moment, let
L
be any open hyperbolic 3-manifold and
\mathcal{HM}(L)
be the set of hyperbolic metrics on
L
up to isotopies and thickenings. (For the notion of thickening, see, e.g.,
[
24
, Chapter I.1.6]
. This is a simple equivalence relation for incomplete hyperbolic metrics on open manifolds.) We note that the space of Killing vector fields on
\widetilde{L}
always has dimension exactly 6: it is easy to see it from any developing map to
\mathbb{H}^{3}
. Consider a smooth path of hyperbolic metrics on
L
. It gives rise to a smooth path of developing maps from
\widetilde{L}
to
\mathbb{H}^{3}
defined up to a path of global isometries of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
. In the first order it produces a vector field
X
on
\widetilde{L}
defined up to Killing fields.
It is easy to see that
X
satisfies the
automorphic property
: for every
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(L)
considered as a deck transformation
\gamma:\widetilde{L}\rightarrow\widetilde{L}
the vector field
X-d\gamma^{-1}(X)

is a Killing field (various for various
\gamma
). If the path of metrics is trivial, e.g., coming from the pull-back of an isotopy, then
X
is the sum of an equivariant vector field and a Killing vector field, i.e., the vector field above is the same for all
\gamma
.

Now we return to our setting and by
AF_{a}(\widetilde{L},\mathcal{U})
denote the space of automorphic affine vector fields on
\widetilde{L}
with respect to
\mathcal{U}
. Note that
KF(\widetilde{L})\subset AF_{a}(\widetilde{L},\mathcal{U})
. However, we are going to see that the space
AF_{a}(\widetilde{L},\mathcal{U})
is larger and to prove

Lemma 4.12
.

T^{L}_{l}\mathbb{R}^{E(\mathcal{T})}\cong AF_{a}(\widetilde{L},\mathcal{U})/KF(\widetilde{L}).

Proof.

Let
X\in AF_{a}(\widetilde{L},\mathcal{U})
and take
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(L)
. As
X-d\gamma^{-1}(X)
is a Killing field, we see that for every tetrahedron
T
of
\mathcal{T}
and every two its lifts
\widetilde{T}_{1}
and
\widetilde{T}_{2}
in
\widetilde{L}
that differ by
\gamma
, the edge-length change induced by
X
is the same. By construction,
X
does not change infinitesimally the dihedral angles of the edges from
E_{0}(\mathcal{T})
. Hence, all lengths changes of tetrahedra in
\widetilde{L}
project to a length change
\dot{l}\in T^{L}_{l}\mathbb{R}^{E(\mathcal{T})}
. This defines a linear map

AF_{a}(\widetilde{L},\mathcal{T})\rightarrow T^{L}_{l}\mathbb{R}^{E(\mathcal{T})}

with
KF(\widetilde{L})
equal to its kernel.

Conversely, let
\dot{l}\in\mathbb{R}^{E(\mathcal{T})}
. Similarly to the proof of Lemma
4.10
, we produce inductively an affine vector field
X
on
\widetilde{L}
with respect to
\mathcal{U}
inducing
\dot{l}
. We need to show that it is automorphic.

Pick a tetrahedron
\widetilde{T}_{1}
of
\mathcal{T}
in
\widetilde{L}
and
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(L)
. Let
\widetilde{T}^{\prime}_{1}
be the tetrahedron
\gamma(\widetilde{T}_{1})
, and let
X_{1}
and
X^{\prime}_{1}
be the components of
X
corresponding to the tetrahedra
\widetilde{T}_{1}
and
\widetilde{T}^{\prime}_{1}
respectively. As
X_{1}
and
d\gamma^{-1}(X^{\prime}_{1})
produce the same length change on the tetrahedron
\widetilde{T}_{1}
, we see that the difference
X_{1}-d\gamma^{-1}(X^{\prime}_{1})
is a Killing field on the tetrahedron
\widetilde{T}_{1}
.

We need to show that for any other tetrahedron
\widetilde{T}_{2}
if
\widetilde{T}^{\prime}_{2}:=\gamma(\widetilde{T}_{2})
and the corresponding components of
X
are
X_{2}
and
X^{\prime}_{2}
, then the vector field
X_{2}-d\gamma^{-1}(X^{\prime}_{2})
is the same Killing field as
X_{1}-d\gamma^{-1}(X^{\prime}_{1})
. Assume that
\widetilde{T}_{2}
is adjacent to
\widetilde{T}_{1}
by a 2-face and that a developing map
dev:\widetilde{L}\rightarrow\mathbb{H}^{3}
is fixed, so we treat the components of
X
as elements of
AF
. The deformations of the edge lengths of
\widetilde{T}_{1}
and
\widetilde{T}_{2}
induced by
\dot{l}
together with the isomorphism (
4.1
) define respectively two affine 6-dimensional spaces
A_{1},A_{2}\subset AF
parallel to
KF
and a map

A_{21}:A_{2}\rightarrow A_{1}

mapping an affine field
X_{2}\in A_{2}
to the unique affine field
X_{1}\in A_{1}
such that the restrictions of
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
to the common face of
\widetilde{T}_{1}
and
\widetilde{T}_{2}
coincide. We extend
A_{21}
linearly to the linear span of
A_{2}
. The latter contains
KF
and
A_{21}
preserves
KF
pointwise.

Similarly we get affine subspaces
A^{\prime}_{1},A^{\prime}_{2}\subset AF
defined by the restriction of
\dot{l}
to the edge lengths of
\widetilde{T}^{\prime}_{1}
and
\widetilde{T}^{\prime}_{2}
and get an affine map

A^{\prime}_{21}:A^{\prime}_{2}\rightarrow A^{\prime}_{1},

which we extend to the linear spans of
A^{\prime}_{1}
,
A^{\prime}_{2}
. We have

A^{\prime}_{21}=d\gamma\circ A_{21}\circ d\gamma^{-1},

d\gamma(X_{1})-X^{\prime}_{1}=d\gamma\circ A_{21}(X_{2})-A^{\prime}_{21}(X^{\prime}_{2})=d\gamma\circ A_{21}(X_{2}-d\gamma^{-1}(X^{\prime}_{2})).

X_{1}-d\gamma^{-1}(X^{\prime}_{1})=A_{21}(X_{2}-d\gamma^{-1}(X^{\prime}_{2})).

It follows that
X_{1}-d\gamma^{-1}(X^{\prime}_{1})
and
X_{2}-d\gamma^{-1}(X^{\prime}_{2})
is the same Killing field. By induction we show that for every component
X_{i}
of
X
,
X_{i}-d\gamma^{-1}(X_{i})
is the same Killing field on the whole
\widetilde{L}
. This finishes the proof.
∎

We now describe affine equivariant vector fields on
\widetilde{L}
with respect to
\mathcal{U}
.

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
X\in AF(\widetilde{L},\mathcal{U})
be equivariant and
\widetilde{T}
be a tetrahedron of
\mathcal{T}
in
\widetilde{L}
. Consider a developing map
dev:\widetilde{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{H}^{3}
, identify
\widetilde{T}
with the image and extend
X
to the vertices. Then
X
is zero at each vertex that has valence greater than 2 in
\Sigma
. For vertices of valence 2,
X
is parallel to (the
dev
-image) of the singular locus.

Proof.

Indeed, suppose that for a vertex
v
of
\widetilde{T}
having valence at least 3 in
\Sigma
the extension of
X
is non-zero. Then there exists an axis of
\Sigma
that, after developing to
\mathbb{H}^{3}
, is not parallel to
X
at
v
. Consider a chain of tetrahedra adjacent by 2-faces, starting at
\widetilde{T}
and looping around this axis. We develop it to
\mathbb{H}^{3}
. Due to the equivariance, the extension of
X
to
v
in the last tetrahedron of the chain should differ from the extension in
\widetilde{T}
. On the other hand, since all tetrahedra in the chain are adjacent by 2-faces, these extensions must coincide. This is a contradiction.

This argument similarly shows that if
v
has valence 2 in
\Sigma
, then the extension of
X
must be parallel to (the
{\rm dev}
-image of) the singular axis of
\Sigma
passing through
X
.
∎

4.3
Bundle-valued differential forms

Recall that
G=PSL(2,\mathbb{C})
,
\widetilde{G}=SL(2,\mathbb{C})
. By
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{C})
we denote the Lie algebra of
\widetilde{G}
. By a result of Culler
[
29
]
, every holonomy map
\pi_{1}(L)\rightarrow G
lifts to a holonomy map
\pi_{1}(L)\rightarrow\widetilde{G}
. Denote the latter by
\rho
. Consider the trivial bundle
\widetilde{E}\rightarrow\widetilde{L}
, where
\widetilde{E}=\widetilde{L}\times\mathfrak{g}
. It is naturally equipped with a flat connection coming from the trivialization. Consider the action of
\pi_{1}(L)
on
\widetilde{E}
where the action on
\widetilde{L}
is given by the deck transformations, and the action on
\mathfrak{g}
is given via
Ad\circ\rho
, where
Ad
is the adjoint representation of
\widetilde{G}
on
\mathfrak{g}
. By taking the quotient, we obtain a flat bundle
E\rightarrow L
, which is the bundle of germs of Killing fields on
L
. Let
\Omega^{*}(L,E)
be the de Rham complex of
E
-valued differential forms on
L
, where the exterior derivative
d
is induced by the flat connection on
E
. This complex is naturally isomorphic to the complex
\Omega^{*}(\widetilde{L},\widetilde{E},Ad\circ\rho)
consisting of
\widetilde{E}
-valued differential forms on
\widetilde{L}
that are equivariant with respect to the action
Ad\circ\rho
. This construction goes back to Weil
[
95
]
, see a general exposition in the book
[
75
]
of Raghunathan, and papers
[
45
,
96
,
97
]
for the applications to the infinitesimal theory of hyperbolic cone-3-manifolds.

Let
e
be an edge of
\Sigma
and
\gamma_{e}
be a meridian around
e
. In
[
97
, Theorem 3.15]
Weiss showed that

Theorem 4.14
.

Let
\omega\in\Omega^{1}(L,E)
belong to
\ker(d\tr(\gamma_{e}))
for all
e\in\Sigma
. Then
\omega
is cohomologous to zero.

For an open hyperbolic 3-manifold
L
there are two main ways to describe an infinitesimal deformation of the hyperbolic structure: via the first cohomology group
H^{1}(L,E)
and via the automorphic vector fields. Following
[
45
]
we now sketch why these ways are equivalent.

At each point
p\in L
the fiber
E_{p}\cong\mathfrak{g}
has a natural decomposition

E_{p}=E_{1,p}\oplus E_{2,p}

into the subspace
E_{1,p}
of infinitesimal translations at
p
and the subspace
E_{2,p}
of infinitesimal rotations around
p
. This produces the decomposition
E=E_{1}\oplus E_{2}
of
E
into two respective sub-bundles. We remark that this decomposition is not preserved by the flat connection of
E
. The subspace
E_{1,p}
can be naturally identified with the tangent space
T_{p}L
