by associating to each tangent vector the infinitesimal translation in its direction, hence the sub-bundle
E_{1}
can be naturally identified with the tangent bundle
TL
, and we will use this identification freely. The Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{C})
has the standard complex structure, which induces a complex structure on
E
such that
E_{2}=iE_{1}
. For
X\in T_{p}L\cong E_{1,p}
the element
iX\in E_{2,p}
is the infinitesimal rotation with the axis parallel to
X
and having the same norm. Here the norm on
E_{1,p}
comes from the metric on
TL
and the norm on
E_{1,p}
comes from the usual metric on
\mathfrak{so}(3)
.

Lift a 1-form
\omega\in\Omega^{1}(L,E)
to an equivariant 1-form
\widetilde{\omega}\in\Omega^{1}(\widetilde{L},\widetilde{E},Ad\circ\rho)
. If
\omega
is closed, so is
\widetilde{\omega}
, and, as
\widetilde{L}
is simply connected,
\widetilde{\omega}
is exact. Hence,
\widetilde{\omega}=ds
, where
s
is a section of
\widetilde{E}
. However,
s
may be non-equivariant. But the notion of automorphicity extends to sections of
\widetilde{E}
and
s
is automorphic, see, e.g.,
[
45
, Lemma 2.1]
. Here a section is called automorphic if for every
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(L)
the section

s-\gamma^{-1}_{*}s

is closed. It follows conversely that for an automorphic section
s
the 1-form
ds
is equivariant. Using the complex structure on
\widetilde{E}
, we can consider every section of
\widetilde{E}
as a pair of vector fields. Clearly,
s
is automorphic (resp. equivariant or constant) if and only if the both corresponding vector fields are automorphic (resp. equivariant or Killing). It is evident that
s
is the sum of an equivariant section and a constant section if and only if
\omega
is exact, so it is cohomologous to zero.

Now we need to learn the passage from an automorphic vector field
X
on
\widetilde{L}
to a closed
E
-valued 1-form. Let
D
be the exterior derivative on
\Omega^{*}(\widetilde{L},T\widetilde{L})
induced by the Levi–Civita connection for the metric on
\widetilde{L}
. Thus,
DX
is a
(T\widetilde{L})
-valued 1-form. Any
(T\widetilde{L})
-valued 1-form can be viewed as an element of
Hom(T\widetilde{L},T\widetilde{L})
. We decompose it into the symmetric and the skew-symmetric parts using the metric on
\widetilde{L}
. As
T_{p}\widetilde{L}
is a 3-dimensional vector space with a positively-definite metric, it is canonically isomorphic to the skew-symmetric subspace of
Hom(T_{p}M,T_{p}M)
. Indeed, if
\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\}
is an orthonormal basis of
T_{p}M
and
\{e^{1},e^{2},e^{3}\}
is the dual basis, then the isomorphism is defined by sending
e_{i}
to
e_{j}\otimes e^{h}-e_{h}\otimes e^{j}
, where
(i,j,h)
is a cyclic permutation of
(1,2,3)
. This defines a bundle-isomorphism of
T\widetilde{L}
and
Hom(T\widetilde{L},T\widetilde{L})
. We denote by
\curl(X)
the vector field obtained from the skew-symmetric part of
DX
under this isomorphism. By
s_{X}:=X-i\curl(X)
we denote the
canonical lift
of
X
, which is a section of
\widetilde{E}
. Hodgson–Kerckhoff showed

Lemma 4.15

(
[
45
]
, Lemma 2.3)
.

The canonical lift of an automorphic vector field is an automorphic section. Every cohomology class in
H^{1}(L,E)
can be represented by a 1-form
\omega
such that the associated local section
s:\widetilde{L}\rightarrow\widetilde{E}
is the canonical lift of the vector field determined by its real part.

The following lemma follows from the discussion above.

Lemma 4.16
.

Assume that for an automorphic vector field
X
the 1-form
\omega\in\Omega^{1}(L,E)
obtained from the projection of the 1-form
ds_{X}
is cohomologous to zero. Then
X
is the sum of an equivariant and a Killing vector field.

4.4
Proof of the infinitesimal rigidity

We now have all the ingredients to prove Lemma
4.1
. As it was showed in Subsection
4.1
, it is enough to prove Lemma
4.9
.

Proof of Lemma
4.9
.

Let
K
be the closed hyperbolic cone-3-manifold obtained from
M(g)
by reflecting it along the boundary. As usually, by
\Sigma
we denote the singular locus and by
L
we denote
K\backslash\Sigma
. The triangulation
\mathcal{T}
extends to a triangulation
\mathcal{T}^{D}
of
K
. Note that due to Lemma
3.24
,
\mathcal{T}
is large, and one can see that
\mathcal{T}^{D}
is also large. By
\mathcal{U}
we denote a partition of unity with respect to an open cover from Lemma
4.11
. We can make
\mathcal{U}
symmetric with respect to the antipodal involution of
K
switching the copies of
M(g)
. Consider an infinitesimal deformation
\dot{l}
of the edge lengths of
\mathcal{T}
as in the statement of Lemma
4.9
. We associate to it a deformation
\dot{l}^{D}
of the edges of
\mathcal{T}^{D}
as follows. For every edge
e\in E(\mathcal{T}^{D})
coming from the boundary of
M(g)
we set
\dot{l}^{D}_{e}=0
. Next, there are two isometric copies of
M(g)
in
K
. In one copy, for every edge
e_{1}\in E(\mathcal{T}^{D})
coming from an interior edge
e\in E(\mathcal{T})
we set
\dot{l}^{D}_{e_{1}}=\dot{l}_{e}
. For the second copy and an edge
e_{2}\in E(\mathcal{T}^{D})
coming from the same interior edge
e\in E(\mathcal{T})
we set
\dot{l}^{D}_{e_{1}}=-\dot{l}_{e}
. It is trivial to see now that for every edge
e\in E(\mathcal{T}^{D})
we have
d\nu_{e}(\dot{l}^{D})=0
.

From Lemma
4.12
we associate to
\dot{l}^{D}
an affine automorphic vector field
X
on
\widetilde{L}
with respect to
\mathcal{U}
. Using Subsection
4.3
we consider its canonical lift
s_{X}:\widetilde{L}\rightarrow\widetilde{E}
and the 1-form
\omega\in\Omega^{1}(L,E)
obtained from the equivariant 1-form
\widetilde{\omega}=ds_{X}
.

We now remark that for every meridian
\gamma_{e}
around an edge
e
of
\Sigma
we have
\omega\in\ker(d\tr(\gamma_{e}))
. Indeed, we can choose
\gamma_{e}
to be symmetric with respect to the antipodal involution of
K
. Now let us consider a tetrahedron
T\subset\mathbb{H}^{3}
, a deformation
\dot{l}_{T}
of its edges producing an affine vector field
X_{T}=X_{\dot{l}_{T}}
on
T
corresponding to a 1-form
\omega_{X_{T}}
, and consider a path
\gamma_{T}
in
T
. Due to linearity, it is easy to see that
-X_{T}
corresponds to the deformation
-\dot{l}_{T}
. Hence, from the linearity of all constructions, we get

\int_{\gamma_{T}}\omega_{X_{T}}=-\int_{\gamma_{T}}\omega_{-X_{T}}.

Thus, returning to
L
, from the symmetry we see that

\int_{\gamma_{e}}\omega=0.

Theorem
4.14
implies that
\omega
is cohomologous to zero. Lemma
4.16
shows that the vector field
X
is the sum of an equivariant one and a Killing one. Subtracting the Killing part, we assume that it is equivariant.

Let
e^{\prime}
be any edge of
\mathcal{T}^{D}
coming from an edge
e
of
\mathcal{T}
connecting two vertices
v_{1},v_{2}\in V
. Note that every
v\in V
is adjacent to at least three edges of
g
, which belong to
\Sigma
after the doubling. Develop to
\mathbb{H}^{3}
any tetrahedron containing
e^{\prime}
and consider the extension of
X
to the vertices of
e
. Lemma
4.13
shows that this extension is zero. Hence,
\dot{l}^{D}_{e^{\prime}}=\pm\dot{l}_{e}=0
.

Now fix a copy of
M(g)
in
K
, consider a geodesic
\psi\subset\partial M(g)
and a face
F\subset\partial M(g)
adjacent to
\psi
. Take all tetrahedra adjacent to
\psi
having a face or an edge in
F
and develop them to
\mathbb{H}^{3}
, we continue to denote by
\psi
the obtained geodesic segment in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
. Let
T
be such a tetrahedron with an edge
e
belonging to
\psi
. By Lemma
4.13
, the extension of
X
to every vertex of
e
is parallel to
\psi
. As
\dot{l}_{e}=0
, we see that the extension vectors have the same direction and the same norm. This holds for all vertices
w\in\psi
. However, there exists at least one edge
e^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{T}
in
F
, which is adjacent to
\psi
by only one vertex and which is not orthogonal to
\psi
. The second endpoint of
e^{\prime}
is in
V
, hence the extension of
X
there is zero. Since
\dot{l}_{e^{\prime}}=0
, we get that all the obtained vectors at the points on
\psi
are zero.

Hence, the extension of
X
to any vertex of
K
is zero. Thus, we have
\dot{l}^{D}=0
, which implies
\dot{l}=0
. This finishes the proof of Lemma
4.9
.
∎

5
Proofs of the main results

5.1
Properness

Recall Definition
1.12
of proto-Fuchsian metrics on
\partial M
. We denote the set of classes of such metrics by
\mathcal{D}_{f}(\partial M)\subset\mathcal{D}_{c}(\partial M,\emptyset)
. By
\mathcal{D}_{f}(\partial M,V)
we denote the subset of
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{c}(\partial M,V)
corresponding to the lifts of metrics from
\mathcal{D}_{f}(\partial M)
and by
\mathcal{D}_{nf}(\partial M,V)
we denote its complement in
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{c}(\partial M,V)
. While studying converging sequences in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,V)
we need to exclude the degenerate limits: when the sequence of underlying convex cocompact metrics on
N
converges to a Fuchsian metric
\overline{g}
and
V
falls to the span of
C(\overline{g})
. We claim that in this case the induced metric on
\partial M
degenerates to a proto-Fuchsian one.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\{g_{i}\}
be a sequence in
\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
and
\{(\rho_{i},\widetilde{f}_{i})\}
be the sequence of its lifts to
\mathcal{R}(\pi_{1}(M),G)\times(\mathbb{H}^{3})^{V}
such that
\{\rho_{i}\}
converges to a Fuchsian representation
\rho
of a Fuchsian metric
\overline{g}
and
\{\widetilde{f}_{i}\}
converges to a map
\widetilde{f}:V\hookrightarrow\widetilde{\Pi}
, where
\Pi
is the geodesic plane containing
C(\overline{g})
. Then the sequence
\{d_{i}\}
of induced metrics on
\partial M
converges in
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{c}(\partial M,V)
to a metric
d\in\mathcal{D}_{f}(\partial M,V)
.

Proof.

We claim that the proof of Lemma
3.29
still applies to the current situation after slight changes. Indeed, as in Lemma
3.26
one can see that
\widetilde{M}(g_{i})
converge in the Hausdorff sense to
\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
. In the Fuchsian case of the first kind Lemma
3.28
applies without problems and the proof of Lemma
3.29
shows that
\{d_{i}\}
converges to
d\in\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{c}(\partial M,V)
that is a lift of the induced metric on
C(\overline{g})
on each component of
\partial M
(recall that here
C(\overline{g})
is a totally geodesic surface), so
d\in{\mathcal{D}}_{f}(\partial M,V)
.

In the Fuchsian case of the second kind Lemma
3.28
still applies, but the notion of the convergences should be corrected, as described in
[
2
,
4
]
. Let
\widetilde{S}_{i}\subset\mathbb{H}^{3}
be a sequence of complete convex surfaces converging in the Hausdorff sense to a planar closed convex subset
\widetilde{S}\subset\Pi\subset\mathbb{H}^{3}
, where
\Pi
is a geodesic plane. We consider the double
D\widetilde{S}
of
\widetilde{S}
along the relative boundary of
S
with the induced metric. Orient
\Pi
and for each
p
in the relative interior of
\widetilde{S}
, choose two rays orthogonal to
\Pi
,
r_{p}^{\uparrow}
and
r_{p}^{\downarrow}
, denoted with respect to the orientation. Denote the two copies of
p
in
DS
by
p^{\uparrow}
and
p^{\downarrow}
, depending on the copy of the relative interior of
\widetilde{S}
in
D\widetilde{S}
. We say that a sequence of points
p_{i}\in\widetilde{S}_{i}
converge intrinsically to
p^{\uparrow}
(respectively to
p^{\downarrow}
) if
p_{i}
converges to
p
and for all sufficiently large
i
the ray
r_{p}^{\uparrow}
(respectively
r_{p}^{\downarrow}
) points at infinity to the outer half-space of some supporting plane to
S_{i}
at
p_{i}
. In this case Lemma
3.28
still works in the sense that if
p_{i}
converge intrinsically to
p\in D\widetilde{S}
and
q_{i}
converge intrinsically to
q\in D\widetilde{S}
, then the intrinsic distance between
p_{i}
and
q_{i}
on
\widetilde{S}_{i}
converges to the intrinsic distance between
p
and
q
in
D\widetilde{S}
.

In the Fuchsian case of the second king, the convex core
C(\overline{g})
is a surface with non-empty geodesic relative boundary. Let
\widetilde{S}=\overline{\rm conv}(\widetilde{V})
be contained in the plane
\Pi
containing
\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
, where
\widetilde{V}
is the
\rho_{\overline{g}}
-orbit of
\widetilde{f}(V)
. Then the quotient
S\subset N(\overline{g})
is a totally geodesic surface with convex piecewise-geodesic relative boundary. Then the proof of Lemma
3.29
works to show that
\{d_{i}\}
converges to
d\in\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{c}(\partial M,V)
that is a lift of the double of
S
along its relative boundary, so
d\in{\mathcal{D}}_{f}(\partial M,V)
.
∎

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
\{g_{i}\}
be a sequence of metrics in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,V)
such that
I_{V}(g_{i})=:d_{i}\in\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{c}(\partial M,V)
converge to
d\in\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{nf}(\partial M,V)
.
Then up to passing to a subsequence
g_{i}
converge in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,V)
to
g\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,V)
. We have
V(g)=V(d)
. In particular, if
d\in\mathcal{D}_{c}(\partial M,V)
, then
g\in\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
.

Proof.

Lemma 4.4 in
[
74
]
implies that up to passing to a subsequence
\overline{g}_{i}
converge to
\overline{g}\in\mathcal{CH}(N)
. We just recall that the proof is a combination of the Busemann–Feller lemma, the Sullivan–Bridgeman–Canary theorem
[
88
,
19
]
producing a bilipschitz map between
\partial C(\overline{g})
and
\partial_{\infty}N(\overline{g})
, and of a compactness criterion for Teichmüller spaces.

Suppose that for some
v\in V
the distance
g_{i}(v,C(\overline{g}_{i}))
tends to infinity. Then if for some other point
p\in\partial M
the distance
g_{i}(p,C(\overline{g}_{i}))
does not tend to infinity, the distance
g_{i}(v,p)
tends to infinity. The Busemann–Feller lemma implies that then
d_{i}(v,p)
tends to infinity, which contradicts to the convergence of
d_{i}
.

For
h\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
let
S_{i,h}\subset N(\overline{g}_{i})
be the surface at the distance
h
from
C(\overline{g}_{i})
. Then the area of
S_{i,h}
is at least the area of
\partial C(\overline{g}_{i})
times
\cosh^{-1}(\sqrt{h})
. Indeed, each face of
\partial C(\overline{g}_{i})
corresponds to a constant-curvature piece on
S_{i}
of area equal to the area of the face times
\cosh^{-1}(\sqrt{h})
. Since the union of all edges of
\partial C(\overline{g}_{i})
has measure zero
[
25
, Theorem 4.9]
, we prove the claim.

Thereby, the distance
g_{i}(\partial M,C(\overline{g}_{i}))
grows to infinity. Hence, there exists a sequence of
h_{i}
growing to infinity such that
M(g_{i})\supset S_{i,h_{i}}
. Due to the argument above and the convergence of
\overline{g}_{i}
, the areas of
S_{i}
grow to infinity. Since the nearest-point projection from
\partial M(g_{i})
to
S_{i}
is contracting, we get that the areas of
d_{i}
grow to infinity, which is a contradiction. Thus, for each
v\in V
the distances
g_{i}(v,C(\overline{g}_{i}))
stay uniformly bounded.

As in Lemma
3.26
, we can choose the embeddings of
\widetilde{C}(\overline{g}_{i})
and
\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
to
\mathbb{H}^{3}
such that
\widetilde{C}(\overline{g}_{i})
converge in the Hausdorff sense to
\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
. As for each
v\in V
the distance
g_{i}(v,C(\overline{g}_{i}))
stay uniformly bounded, we can choose the maps
\widetilde{f}_{i}:V\hookrightarrow\mathbb{H}^{3}
such that up to taking a subsequence it converges to a map
\widetilde{f}:V\hookrightarrow\mathbb{H}^{3}
. By
\widetilde{V}_{i}
and
\widetilde{V}
we denote the orbits of
\widetilde{f}_{i}(V)
ans
\widetilde{f}(V)
under the respective holonomy representations. Consider
\overline{\rm conv}(\widetilde{V})
quotiented by the action of
\rho_{\overline{g}}
. We get a manifold homeomorphic to
M
except the case when
\overline{g}
is Fuchsian and
\widetilde{V}\subset\widetilde{C}(\overline{g})
. In the latter case Lemma
5.1
shows that
d\notin{\mathcal{D}}_{nf}(\partial M,V)
, hence this can not happen. As
\widetilde{V}_{i}\subset\partial\overline{\rm conv}(\widetilde{V}_{i})
, we get
\widetilde{V}\subset\partial\overline{\rm conv}(\widetilde{V})
, and the metrics
g_{i}
converge to
g\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,V)
. By Lemma
3.29
, the induced metric on
\partial M(g)
is
d
and it is easy to see that
V(g)=V(d)
. This finishes the proof.

∎

5.2
Existence of bent realizations

In this section we prove the existence part of Theorem
A
.

Proof.

Take arbitrary
d_{0}\in\mathcal{D}_{nf}(\partial M,V)
and
g_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
, define

d_{1}:=\mathcal{I}_{V}(g_{1})\in\mathcal{D}_{c}(\partial M,V).

By Lemma
2.27
there exist
W\supseteq V
and a path
\alpha:[0,1]\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{bc}(\partial M,W)
such that
\alpha(0)
,
\alpha(1)
are lifts of
d_{0}
,
d_{1}
and for all
t\in(0,1)
we have
\alpha(t)\in\mathcal{D}_{bc}(\partial M,W)
. We want to slightly modify the path so that also
\alpha(1)\in\mathcal{D}_{bc}(\partial M,W)
and
\alpha(1)
remains in the image of
{\mathcal{I}}_{W}
. Since
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{bc}(\partial M,W)
is open in
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{c}(\partial M,W)
, we can choose a neighborhood
U\ni\alpha(1)
in
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{c}(\partial M,W)
contained in
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{bc}(\partial M,W)
. As
{\mathcal{I}}_{W}
is continuous,
{\mathcal{I}}^{-1}_{W}(U)
is open in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,W)
and there is
g\in(\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,W)\cap{\mathcal{I}}^{-1}_{W}(U))
. Let
\alpha^{\prime}:[0,1]\rightarrow U
be a path connecting
\alpha(t^{\prime})\in(U\cap\mathcal{D}_{c}(\partial M,W))
for some
t^{\prime}
close to 1 with
\mathcal{I}_{W}(g)\in(U\cap\mathcal{D}_{c}(\partial M,W))
.
We use Lemma
2.26
to modify
\alpha^{\prime}
to a path
\alpha^{\prime\prime}:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{c}(\partial M,W)
connecting the same endpoints.
As
U
is open, we can do it so that
\alpha^{\prime\prime}(t)\in U\subset\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{bc}(\partial M,W)
. We modify
\alpha
by replacing the restriction of
\alpha
on
[t^{\prime},1]
with the path
\alpha^{\prime\prime}
.

Let
T\subset[0,1]
be a subset consisting of those
t
that
\alpha(t)
is in the image of
{\mathcal{I}}_{W}
. If
t\in T
and
{t>0}
, then
\alpha(t)
is balanced, thus Corollary
3.18
implies that
\mathcal{I}^{-1}_{W}(\alpha(t))\subset\mathcal{P}_{cp}(M,W)
. Hence, Corollary
4.2
implies that
\mathcal{I}_{W}
is a local homeomorphism at points of
\mathcal{I}^{-1}_{W}(\alpha(t))
. Thus, there is a neighborhood of
t
in
[0,1]
belonging to
T
. It follows that
T\cap(0,1]
is open. Lemma
5.2
implies that
T
is also closed. Therefore,
T=[0,1]
and
\alpha(0)
is in the image of
{\mathcal{I}}_{W}
. This implies that
d_{0}
is in the image of
\mathcal{I}_{V}
and finishes the proof.
∎

5.3
Topological interlude

For the proof in the next section we need to construct a covering space over
\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
, on which we can define the induced metric map with values in
\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\sharp}(\partial M,V)
. First, define the space of
pointed
convex cocompact hyperbolic metrics
\mathcal{PCH}(N)
to be
\mathcal{CH}(N,V)
for a set
V
of cardinality one. Next, by a
geometric end
of
N(\overline{g})
we will mean a connected component of the complement of
N(\overline{g})
to
C(\overline{g})
. There is a 1:1 correspondence between components of
\partial M
and geometric ends of
N(\overline{g})
. Denote the components of
\partial M
by
S_{j}
,
j=1\ldots m
, and denote the positive real numbers by
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Let
\mathcal{PCH}_{j}(N)
be the subset of
\mathcal{PCH}(N)
with the marked point belonging to the
j
-th geometric end. It was shown in
[
74
, Section 5.1]
that the space
\mathcal{PCH}_{j}(N)
can be trivialized as

\mathcal{PCH}_{j}(N)\cong\mathcal{CH}(N)\times S_{j}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}

(5.1)

such that
the sets
(\overline{g},p,\mathbb{R}_{+})
,
\overline{g}\in\mathcal{CH}(N),p\in S_{j},
are the gradient lines of the distance function to the convex core in the
j
-th geometric end of
N(\overline{g})
. In what follows we fix such a trivialization for every
j=1\ldots m
. The purpose of this is that now for every
\overline{g}\in\mathcal{CH}(N)
and for every totally convex surface
\Sigma
in
N(\overline{g})
(i.e.,
\Sigma
is a boundary component of a totally convex set) that does not touch
C(\overline{g})
, we have a homeomorphism
S_{j}\rightarrow\Sigma
for
j
indicating the geometric end containing
\Sigma
. Indeed, it is given by the inverse of the projection of
\Sigma
to the
S_{j}
-factor in trivialization (
5.1
). Things are slightly subtler if
\Sigma
touches the convex core, but one can see that in this case anyway the chosen trivializations furnish a continuous surjective map
S_{j}\rightarrow\Sigma
. It is not injective exactly if
\Sigma
contains an edge that is a closed geodesic. Then the preimage of this geodesic is a closed tubular neighborhood of a simple closed curve in
S_{j}
.

Next, consider arbitrary
V\subset\partial M
and define
V_{j}:=V\cap S_{j}
.
Denote by
\mathcal{CH}^{*}(N,V)
the subset of
\mathcal{CH}(N,V)
such that points from
V_{j}
mark points in the
j
-th geometric end, and no two marked points belong to the same gradient line of the distance function to the convex core. (Note that the first condition implies that all the marked points are at positive distance from the convex core.) By
S_{*V}
denote the product of
S_{j}^{*V_{j}}
, where
S_{j}^{*V_{j}}\subset S_{j}^{V_{j}}
is the space of injective maps from
V_{j}
to
S_{j}
. Trivializations (
5.1
) define a trivialization

\mathcal{CH}^{*}(N,V)\cong\mathcal{CH}(N)\times S_{*V}\times\mathbb{R}^{V}_{+}.

(5.2)

Denote by
\mathcal{CH}^{\sharp}
the universal cover of the latter space. We have a trivialization

\mathcal{CH}^{\sharp}(N,V)\cong\mathcal{CH}(N)\times\widetilde{S_{*V}}\times\mathbb{R}^{V}_{+}.

(5.3)

It is easy to see that
\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
belongs to
\mathcal{CH}^{*}(N,V)
. Denote its respective covering space, as a subset of
\mathcal{CH}^{\sharp}(N,V)
, by
\mathcal{P}_{b}^{\sharp}(M,V)
.

Our current goal is to define a map
\mathcal{I}^{\sharp}_{V}:{\mathcal{P}}_{b}^{\sharp}(M,V)\rightarrow{\mathcal{D}}_{c}^{\sharp}(\partial M,V)
. Denote by
H_{0}(\partial M)
the space of all self-homeomorphisms of
\partial M
isotopic to identity, endowed with the compact-open topology. Recall that
H_{0}^{\sharp}(\partial M,V)
denotes its normal subgroup consisting of those homeomorphisms, for whom an isotopy can be chosen fixing
V
. The inclusion
V\hookrightarrow\partial M
can be considered as an element of
S_{*V}
. Fix over this element some element
\widehat{V}
of
\widetilde{S_{*V}}
. Every homeomorphism
h\in H_{0}(\partial M)
, by the homotopy lifting, determines a self-homeomorphism of
\widetilde{S_{*V}}
. By considering its evaluation at
\widehat{V}
, we define a map
H_{0}(\partial M)\rightarrow\widetilde{S_{*V}}
, which factors through
H_{0}^{\sharp}(\partial M,V)
to a map

{\rm ev}:H_{0}(\partial M)/H_{0}^{\sharp}(\partial M,V)\rightarrow\widetilde{S_{*V}}.

It was shown in
[
39
, Lemma 3.1]
that this map is a homeomorphism.

Let
g\in{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,V)
, represented by a pair
(\overline{g},f)
, and
g^{\sharp}
be its lift to
{\mathcal{P}}_{b}^{\sharp}(M,V)
. Trivialization (
5.1
) provides a map
\chi:\partial M\rightarrow\partial M(g)
, as described above. We need to slightly modify it to obtain a homeomorphism. Indeed, let
\psi
be the union of all edges of
M(g)
that are simple closed geodesics. The preimage
\chi^{-1}(\psi)
is a closed tubular neighborhood
X
of a multicurve
\lambda\subset\partial M
, which has a parametrization
X\cong[-1,1]\times\lambda
so that
\chi
sends each fiber
[-1,1]\times p
,
p\in\lambda
, to a point. Consider a small thickening
Y\cong[-2,2]\times\lambda
so that
Y
is disjoint with
\chi^{-1}(V(g))
. Then
Z:=\chi(Y)
is a tubular neighborhood of
\psi
in
\partial M
. We can modify
\chi
inside
Y
by a fiberwise homeomorphism between
Y
and
Z
. We continue to denote the modified map by
\chi
. Trivialization (
5.3
) and the choice of the evaluation map provide a class in
H_{0}(\partial M)/H_{0}^{\sharp}(\partial M,V)
, for which we choose a representative
h
. One can see that the composition
f^{-1}\circ\chi\circ h
is the identity on
V
. Thereby, the pull-back of the induced metric on
\partial M(g)
by
\chi\circ h
to
(S,V)
gives an element of
{\mathcal{D}}_{c}^{\sharp}(\partial M,V)
, which we denote by
\mathcal{I}^{\sharp}_{V}(g^{\sharp})
, independent on the choice of
h
in its class and of the modification of
\chi
above. (Let us stress out that it is dependent only on trivialization (
5.1
) and on
\widehat{V}
.)

Remark 5.3
.

The group of deck transformations of
\widetilde{S_{\ast V}}\rightarrow S_{\ast V}
is naturally isomorphic to the pure braid group
B_{0}(\partial M,V)=H_{0}(\partial M,V)/H_{0}^{\sharp}(\partial M,V)
from Section
2.2
, see
[
39
, Remark 3.5]
. Thereby, it is also the group of deck transformations of the covering map
{\mathcal{P}}_{b}^{\sharp}(M,V)\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,V)
. It is also the group of deck tranformations of the covering map
{\mathcal{D}}^{\sharp}_{c}(\partial M,V)\rightarrow{\mathcal{D}}_{c}(\partial M,V)
. By construction, the map
\mathcal{I}^{\sharp}_{V}
is equivariant with respect to
B_{0}(\partial M,V)
.

Lemma 5.4
.

The space
{\mathcal{P}}_{b}^{\sharp}(M,V)
is connected.

Proof.

Take
g_{0}^{\sharp},g_{1}^{\sharp}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{b}^{\sharp}(M,V)
. Since
\mathcal{CH}^{\sharp}(N,V)
is connected, there is a path
g_{t}^{\sharp}
connecting
g_{0}^{\sharp}
and
g_{1}^{\sharp}
in
\mathcal{CH}^{\sharp}(N,V)
. Represent the projection of
g_{t}^{\sharp}
to
\mathcal{CH}(N,V)
as
(\overline{g}_{t},f_{t})
, and denote
\overline{\rm conv}(f_{t}(V))
by
M(g_{t})
. It is clear that since
f_{t}(V)
does not belong to
C(\overline{g}_{t})
, the set
M(g_{t})
is homeomorphic to
M
.

For every
v\in V
define

O_{v}:=\{t\in[0,1]:f_{t}(v)\in\overline{\rm conv}(f_{t}(V\backslash\{v\}))\}.

This is a closed set. Now for a given
v\in V
we can continuously increase its
\mathbb{R}_{+}
-coordinate from trivialization (
5.2
) over a small neighborhood of
O_{v}
in
[0,1]
so that for no other
w\in V
the set
O_{w}
enlarges. After doing this for every vertex, we obtain that
f_{t}(V)
is in a strictly convex position for every
t\in[0,1]
. This allows to modify
g_{t}^{\sharp}
to a path in
\mathcal{P}_{b}^{\sharp}(M,V)
.

∎

5.4
From local to global rigidity

In this section we prove Proposition
1.13
from the introduction stating that the local rigidity of a bent metric implies global rigidity.

Proof.

Define
\mathcal{P}_{bp}(M,V):=\mathcal{I}^{-1}_{V}(\mathcal{D}_{bc}(\partial M,V))
. As
\mathcal{D}_{bc}(\partial M,V)
is open in
\mathcal{D}_{c}(\partial M,V)
and
\mathcal{I}_{V}
is continuous,
\mathcal{P}_{bp}(M,V)
is open in
\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
. Due to Corollary
3.18
, we get
\mathcal{P}_{bp}(M,V)\subset\mathcal{P}_{cp}(M,V)
. Recall that Corollary
4.2
states that
\mathcal{I}_{V}
is a local homeomorphism over
\mathcal{P}_{cp}(M,V)
, hence it is a local homeomorphism over
\mathcal{P}_{bp}(M,V)
. Lemma
5.2
together with the definition of
\mathcal{P}_{bp}(M,V)
imply that
\mathcal{I}_{V}
is proper over
\mathcal{P}_{bp}(M,V)
. Hence,
\mathcal{I}_{V}
is a covering map over
\mathcal{P}_{bp}(M,V)
. Take into account, however, that
\mathcal{P}_{bp}(M,V)
and
\mathcal{D}_{bc}(\partial M,V)
may be disconnected.

Suppose that there are
g_{0},g_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
such that
\mathcal{I}_{V}
is a local homeomorphism around
g_{0}
and

\mathcal{I}_{V}(g_{0})=\mathcal{I}_{V}(g_{1})\in\mathcal{D}_{c}(\partial M,V).

We have a commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}^{\sharp}_{b}(M,V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}^{\sharp}_{c}(\partial M,V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}_{c}(\partial M,V)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}^{\sharp}_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}_{V}}

The vertical maps are covering maps. Moreover, their groups of deck transformations are naturally isomorphic to
B_{0}(\partial M,V)
and
\mathcal{I}_{V}^{\sharp}
is equivariant with respect to it, see Remark
5.3
. It follows that there are lifts
g_{0}^{\sharp}
and
g_{1}^{\sharp}
of
g_{0}
and
g_{1}
such that

\mathcal{I}_{V}^{\sharp}(g_{0}^{\sharp})=\mathcal{I}_{V}^{\sharp}(g_{1}^{\sharp})\in\mathcal{D}_{c}^{\sharp}(\partial M,V).

Lemma
5.4
implies that there exists a path
\zeta^{\sharp}:[0,1]\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}}_{b}^{\sharp}(M,V)
connecting
g_{0}^{\sharp}
and
g_{1}^{\sharp}
. Consider
[0,1]
with the identified endpoints as
S^{1}
. By composing
{\mathcal{I}}_{V}^{\sharp}
with
\zeta^{\sharp}
, we obtain a map
\beta^{\sharp}:S^{1}\rightarrow{\mathcal{D}}_{c}^{\sharp}(\partial M,V)
. Lemma
2.28
implies that there exist
W\supseteq V
and a lift
\alpha^{\sharp}:S^{1}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{bc}^{\sharp}(\partial M,W)
of
\beta^{\sharp}
such that the loop
\alpha^{\sharp}
is contractible in
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{bc}^{\sharp}(\partial M,W)
. Denote the projections of
\zeta^{\sharp}
and
\alpha^{\sharp}
to
\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
and
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{bc}(\partial M,W)
by
\zeta
and
\alpha
respectively. It follows that
\alpha
is contractible in
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{bc}(\partial M,W)
.

The path
\zeta:[0,1]\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,V)
uniquely lifts to a path
\eta:[0,1]\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,W)
such that
\alpha={\mathcal{I}}_{W}\circ\eta
. We now construct a homotopy
\eta_{s}:[0,1]\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,W)
of the path
\eta=\eta_{0}
such that
s\in[0,\varepsilon]
for some small
\varepsilon>0
and for all
s>0
and
t\in[0,1]
we have
\eta_{s}(t)\in\mathcal{P}_{bp}(M,W)
. To this purpose, for each
w\in W
and each
t\in[0,1]
choose an outer normal ray at
w
in
N(\overline{\eta}(t))
continuously depending on
t
. Move all points of
W
along these rays at distance
s
and take the closed convex hull. It is not hard to see that for every
s
the obtained points are in a strictly convex position in
N(\overline{\eta}(t))
. The closed convex hull is isotopic to
M(\eta(t))
in
N(\overline{\eta}(t))
by an isotopy sending the vertices to the respective points of
W
. This determines a class in
{\mathcal{P}}_{b}(M,W)
, which we denote by
\eta_{s}(t)
. We also denote
{\mathcal{I}}_{W}(\eta_{s}(t))
by
\theta_{s}(t)\in\mathcal{D}_{c}(\partial M,W)
. For each
t\in[0,1]
we see that
\theta_{s}(t)
converge in
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{c}(\partial M,W)
to
\alpha(t)\in\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{bc}(\partial M,W)
as
s\rightarrow 0
. Then, as
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{bc}(\partial M,W)
is open in
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{c}(\partial M,W)
, provided that
\varepsilon
is small enough, for all
s\in(0,\varepsilon]
we get
\theta_{s}(t)\in\mathcal{D}_{bc}(\partial M,W)
and so
\eta_{s}(t)\in\mathcal{P}_{bp}(M,W)
.

We claim that for each
s\in(0,\varepsilon]
the metrics
\theta_{s}(0)
and
\theta_{s}(1)
can be connected by arcs
\theta^{\prime}_{s}\subset\mathcal{D}_{bc}(\partial M,W)
such that after a reparametrization of curves
\theta_{s}\cup\theta^{\prime}_{s}
as loops
\alpha^{\prime}_{s}:S^{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{bc}(\partial M,W)
they constitute a homotopy of the loop
\alpha
. First, we consider the arcs

\theta^{\prime\prime}_{s}=\{\theta_{r}(0):r\in[0,s]\}\cup\{\theta_{r}(1):r\in[0,s]\}\subset\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{bc}(\partial M,W).

We reparametrize
\theta_{s}\cup\theta^{\prime\prime}_{s}
as loops
\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{s}:S^{1}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{bc}(\partial M,W)
so that
\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{s}
constitute a homotopy of
\alpha
,
\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{s}(0)=\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{s}(1)=\alpha(0)
, and for all
s\in(0,\varepsilon]
and
t\in(0,1)
we have
\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{s}(t)\in\mathcal{D}_{bc}(\partial M,W)
. Moreover, we reparametrize them so that the arc
\theta_{s}
corresponds to the restriction of
\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{s}
to the interval
[s,1-s]
(we can assume that
\varepsilon<1/2
).
Since the loop
\alpha
is contractible in
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{bc}(\partial M,W)
, so are the loops
\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{s}
, and the homotopy
\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{s}
of
\alpha
can be lifted to
\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{\sharp}_{bc}(\partial M,W)
. Then Lemma
2.26
can be used to modify it to have the image in
\mathcal{D}_{bc}^{\sharp}(\partial M,W)
for
s>0
. Denote the projection of the modified homotopy back to
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{bc}(\partial M,W)
by
\alpha^{\prime}_{s}
. Then the loops
\alpha^{\prime}_{s}
constitute the homotopy of
\alpha
and for
s\in(0,\varepsilon]
we have
\alpha^{\prime}_{s}:S^{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{bc}(\partial M,W)
.

We claim that if a loop
\alpha^{\prime}:S^{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{bc}(\partial M,W)
is contractible in
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{bc}(\partial M,W)
, then it is contractible in
\mathcal{D}_{bc}(\partial M,W)
.
Indeed, we take a map
\xi^{\prime}:\overline{D}^{2}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{bc}(\partial M,W)
contracting
\alpha^{\prime}
, lift it to
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{bc}^{\sharp}(\partial M,W)
, modify it there with the help of Lemma
2.26
, and project it back to a map
\xi^{\prime\prime}:\overline{D}^{2}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{bc}(\partial M,W)
.
Thereby, the loops
\alpha^{\prime}_{s}
, constructed above, are contractible in
\mathcal{D}_{bc}(\partial M,W)
and hence
(\mathcal{I}_{W})^{-1}(\alpha^{\prime}_{s})
consist of closed loops. Particularly, for each
s\in(0,\varepsilon]
the preimage
(\mathcal{I}_{W})^{-1}(\theta^{\prime}_{s})
contains an arc
\eta^{\prime}_{s}
connecting
\eta_{s}(0)
and
\eta_{s}(1)
.

Let
U
be a compact neighborhood of
\alpha(0)
in
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{c}(\partial M,W)
containing all
\theta^{\prime}_{s}
. By Lemma
5.2
,
{\mathcal{I}}_{W}
is proper. Hence,
X:={\mathcal{I}}^{-1}_{W}(U)
is compact too. All arcs
\eta^{\prime}_{s}
belong to
X
and we can consider their Hausdorff limit
\eta^{\prime}\subset X
(note that
\mathcal{P}_{b}(M,V)
is a finite-dimensional manifold and the Hausdorff limits are well-defined; we consider
\eta^{\prime}
as a set, not as a map). Since the arcs
\theta^{\prime}_{s}
converge to
\alpha(0)
, we have
{\mathcal{I}}_{W}(\eta^{\prime})=\alpha(0)
. Also we have
\eta(0),\eta(1)\in\eta^{\prime}
. Since
\mathcal{I}_{V}
is a local homeomorphism around
g_{0}
, Proposition
4.5
implies that
\eta(0)
is isolated in
{\mathcal{I}}^{-1}_{W}(\alpha(0))
.
Therefore,
\eta(0)
and the rest of
{\mathcal{I}}_{W}^{-1}(\alpha(0))
have disjoint open neighborhoods
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
. For all small enough
s
the arcs
\eta^{\prime}_{s}
should belong to
Y_{1}\cup Y_{2}
and meet both
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
, which is impossible since
\eta^{\prime}_{s}
are connected arcs. This contradiction finishes the proof.
∎
